L4: MEDICAL REVIEW(S)
L5: MEMORANDUM
L6: DATE: April 26, 2009FROM: Russell Katz, M.D.DirectorDivision of Neurology ProductsTO: BLA 1757 (STN 125274)SUBJECT: Recommendation for Action on BLA 1757 (STN 125274), for the use of Dysport (botulinum toxin A) in the treatment of Cervical Dystonia
L7: BLA 1757 (STN 125274), for the use of Dysport (botulinum toxin A) in the treatment of Cervical Dystonia (CD), was submitted by Ipsen Biopharm Limited on 11/29/07, and was the subject of a Complete Response (CR) letter (Dysport is also the subject of a BLA for its use in the treatment of glabellar lines; this BLA is under review in the Division of Dermatologic and Dental Products)
L8: The CR letter for the CD indication requested that the sponsor submit a Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategy (REMS), the goal of which was to address several potential safety issues identified during the review of the application.
L9: Specifically, the Agency had been evaluating, during the review of the Dysport BLA, the occurrence of events (with all botulinum toxin products) that clinically resembled botulism (weakness in muscles distant from the site of injection) and that are related to the spread of the effects of the toxin to sites distant from the site of injection. Our analyses of these events were initiated in response to reports identified in the Agency's AERS database, but more or less simultaneous with the initiation of our investigation, a Citizen's Petition requesting Agency action on this issue was submitted by Public Citizen. As part of our investigation, we asked the sponsors of the relevant products (Dysport [though not yet marketed in the United States, it has been marketed elsewhere, and such reports were available from these sources], Botox, Botox Cosmetic, and Myobloc) to submit comprehensive evaluations of these events, and the Agency performed numerous reviews of these reports and the reports in the AERS database. In brief, although cases of "distant spread of the toxin's effects" have been reported with on-label use, many, if not most, of the cases occurred in pediatric patients treated off-label for lower limb spasticity. Because the use of these products can result in these serious systemic effects (including some cases of death), the Agency had determined that a REMS should be in place to mitigate this risk.
L10: In addition, the approval of Dysport would introduce the second botulinum toxin type A product into the domestic market (the first is Botox/Botox Cosmetic). Botox is also approved for the treatment of CD (as well as other indications), but the dose for CD is very different from the dose of Dysport for the same indication. This is a result of the
L11: different potency assays used for different botulinum toxin products (including Myobloc, which is botulinum toxin type B). Because of the risk of under- or over-dosing if these products were inadvertently interchanged, we agreed that each product would adopt an established name with the suffix "...botulinumtoxinA (or B)", with each product having a unique prefix of no more than 5 letters, and devoid of meaning. In the CR letter, we asked Ipsen to propose such a prefix.
L12: The sponsor responded to the CR letter with a submission dated 2/27/09. This submission has been reviewed by Dr. Marc Stone, safety reviewer, Dr. Sally Yasuda, Safety team leader, as well as by reviewers in OBP and OSE. The review team recommends that the application be approved.
L13: As noted above, the sponsor has submitted a REMS that consists of a Medication Guide to be given to the patient/caregiver before each injection, a Dear Health Care Practitioner letter that describes the occurrence of cases of the distant spread of toxin effects and informs practitioners of the new established name, and a timetable of assessments of the success of the REMS.
L14: In addition, the sponsor has proposed the established name "abobotulinumtoxinA"; the Agency has determined that this name is acceptable, and the name has been approved by USAN.
L15: There are no new safety issues identified in the safety data submitted in this response, and the REMS has been deemed acceptable.
L16: We have determined that the sponsor has to provide additional data in Phase 4.
L17: First, they must perform a juvenile animal toxicity study and a repeat embryo-fetal study. Although the sponsor had previously submitted an embryo-fetal development study, it was considered inadequate because the high dose was lethal to the pregnant dams. However, in a preliminary study, that same dose was well tolerated. This issue will need to be definitively addressed in another study.
L18: In addition, we have required, as Phase 4 requirements, that the sponsor perform additional clinical trials: randomized controlled trials in the treatment of upper and lower limb spasticity, each in adults and pediatric patients, and an open-label safety study in these patients (this latter can be performed as a single study). The purpose of these studies is to establish the effectiveness of the treatment in these settings (recall that most of the cases of concern occurred in the off-label use of these products in these settings), and further to establish the effective dose range for these indications, so that appropriate dosing recommendations may be provided for these uses. We do not expect that any serious events of toxin spread will occur in these studies (given the believed relative rarity of these events), although events of lesser severity may be observed.
L19: In addition, there are several CMC PMRs and PMCs.
L20: We have discussed these with the sponsor, and they are in agreement with these PMRs and PMCs. Further, we have agreed to the language in the label and Medication Guide, and the elements of the REMS. We have further agreed that there will be a single label, incorporating both new indications (CD and glabellar lines). It is worth noting in particular that we have agreed that the risk of spread of the toxin's effects should be described in a Boxed Warning, as well as in other sections of the label. These changes will be included as class labeling in the labeling for the other approved botulinum toxin products (Botox, Botox Cosmetic, and Myobloc).
L21: For the reasons given above, then, we recommend that the BLA be approved, with the attached agreed upon package insert, Medication Guide, REMS, and PMRs and PMCs.
L22: Review and Evaluation of Clinical DataSafety Team Leader Memorandum
L23: BLA: 125274Drug: Clostridium Botulinum Toxin Type A Hemagglutinin Complex(abobotulinumtoxinA); DYSPORTRoute: Intramuscular InjectionIndication: Cervical DystoniaSponsor: Ipsen Biopharm LtdMaterial Submitted: Complete ResubmissionReview Date: 4/13/09 SNReviewer: Sally Usdin Yasuda, Safety Team LeaderNeurology Drug Products, HFD-120
L24: 1 Background and Summary of Findings from Safety Review
L25: Ipsen has submitted a Complete Resubmission in response to the Complete Response Letter issued by the Division on 12/23/08. The submission includes a safety update, final draft labeling, a proposed established name, and submission of REMS documentation.
L26: Dr. Marc Stone has summarized the Integrated Summary of Safety Update that included information from open-label Study 731 that was the continuation of the Phase 3 cervical dystonia study still being conducted at the time of the 120 day safety update. In the most recent update, Dr. Stone finds that the pattern of adverse events was similar to that seen across all open-label studies at the time of the 120-day safety update. Dysphagia (13.3% of all subjects) was the only adverse event that occurred in more than 5% of subjects. In that study and in the other cervical dystonia studies reported, the only serious or severe adverse events that were considered by Dr. Stone to be plausibly treatment related were dysphagia in 1 subject and muscle weakness in another. Spontaneous reports of serious adverse events in patients treated for cervical dystonia included loss of gag reflex, loss of consciousness and respiratory arrest in 1 patient, and dysphagia with dysphonia in 2 other patients, and dysphagia without dysphonia in 4 cases. I agree with Dr. Stone that these adverse events are addressed in the language already included in the proposed labeling. Serious adverse events that occurred in subjects treated for cosmetic indications or other indications in studies or postmarketing did not include new cases of dysphagia or breathing difficulties.
L27: The sponsor submitted proposed draft labeling that was revised in part by the Sponsor based on discussions held during the February 2, 2009 Type A meeting. Additional changes to the labeling and Medication Guide have been made by the Division and those changes were sent to the Sponsor on 4/10/09. As with the other botulinum toxin products, the distant spread of toxin effect following local administration is a concern, and this is addressed in class labeling language.
L28: Clinical postmarketing requirements to address the spread of toxin effect and other safety issues identified in Dr. Stone's review of the BLA were also sent to the Sponsor on
L29: Safety Team Leader MemoBLA 125274
L30: 4/10/09. These will include a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multiple fixed doses, parallel group clinical trial of Dysport in children with lower-limb spasticity associated with cerebral palsy, in botulinum toxin-naïve subjects. Safety data must be collected in the controlled trial, to include data on the systemic spread of the toxin. In addition to signs of systemic botulism, the Sponsor is to measure the effects of Dysport on blood glucose and alkaline phosphatase as a marker of bone metabolism. After the completion of the controlled phase, a long-term open-label extension study will be required to systematically collect safety data including data on the systemic spread of the toxin. Effects of Dysport on growth and maturation should also be examined. A second clinical postmarketing requirement is for a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multiple fixed doses, parallel group clinical trial of Dysport in adults with lower-limb spasticity, in botulinum toxin-naïve subjects. Safety data must be collected in the controlled trial, to include data on the systemic spread of the toxin.
L31: 2 Conclusions
L32: I agree with Dr. Stone's conclusion that the information contained in the Safety Update is consistent with what has been seen in the initial submission of the BLA, and that there is no need to alter labeling or risk management activities based on the Safety Update.
L33: Review and Evaluation of Clinical Data
L34: BLA: 125274
L35: Sponsor: Ipsen
L36: Drug: AbobotulinumtoxinA (Dysport)
L37: Material Reviewed: Integrated Summary of Safety Update
L38: Reviewer: Marc Stone, M.D. MSg
L39: Submission Dates: 2/27/2009
L40: Date Review Completed: 4/6/2009
L41: Ipsen has submitted a Safety Update as part of its Complete Response. It contains information from open-label Study 731, the only Phase 3 cervical dystonia study that was still being conducted at the time of the 120-day update. Additional safety data from patients with cervical dystonia are presented from Study 712, a Phase 4 cervical dystonia open-label study and Study 102, a post-marketing surveillance study. They also report data from clinical trials for other indications that were initiated or that continued to be conducted since the time of the 120-day update. Finally, they also review spontaneous reports of adverse events with Dysport or botulinum toxins that have occurred outside of Ipsen studies.
L42: In the continuation of Study 731, the pattern of adverse events was similar to that seen across all open-label studies at the time of the 120-day update. Dysphagia (13.3% of subjects) was the only adverse event occurring in more than 5% of subjects. The overall incidence of adverse events was slightly lower in the latter stages of Study 731 as might be expected: subjects susceptible to adverse events would have been more likely to have dropped out; there were no withdrawals due to adverse events in the period covered by this report. The only severe adverse event that was plausibly treatment related was dysphagia in one subject. There were three serious adverse events in other study subjects: back pain, lower limb fracture and nephrolithiasis. Laboratory data were limited but showed no notable anomalies.
L43: Among other studies of subjects with cervical dystonia, two subjects experienced severe adverse events. One subject developed appendicitis with peritonitis while another reported depression and muscle weakness causing difficulty in maintaining head position. The muscle weakness is likely the only one of these events that is plausibly due to treatment.
L44: In studies of subjects with other indications, there were a number of serious adverse events reported. Although Ipsen described this information as new, most of these events occurred more than three years ago and were noted in earlier stages of this BLA. For events occurring in 2007 or later, there was one case of cerebral contusion resulting from a fall out of an automobile, one case of appendicitis, one case of benign uterine tumors, one case of deep vein thrombosis with pulmonary embolism, one case of suicidal ideation, and one case of pneumonia. These all occurred in subjects treated for cosmetic indications.
L45: There were eleven serious adverse events in eight patients treated for cervical dystonia outside of Ipsen studies that were spontaneously reported. One patient suffered loss of gag reflex, loss of consciousness and a respiratory arrest. Two others suffered dysphagia with dysphonia. There were four other cases of severe dysphagia without dysphonia. One reported diminished efficacy. All of these events are adequately covered in the labeling currently being considered.
L46: Conclusions
L47: The information contained in the Safety Update is consistent with what has been seen in the past. There is no reason to alter labeling or risk management activities.
L48: A handwritten signature in black ink, appearing to be "M. R." with a stylized flourish.

L53: Summary
L54: I concur with the summary review of Dr. Russell Katz, Director, Division of Neurology Products. Approval of Dysport (Botulinum Toxin Type A) for cervical dystonia (CD) at the present time, without clearly distinguishable established names for this and other botulinum products (Botox [also Botulinum Toxin Type A] and Myoblock [Botulinum Toxin Type B]) could lead to medication errors with potentially fatal consequences. Lacking an appropriate established name to distinguish Dysport from other botulinum products at the present time, I concur with the attached CR letter. The Division asked Ipson Biopharm to provide candidate established names, and the applicant provided four; however, none were deemed acceptable, largely because they conveyed some type of meaning (b) (4)
L55: The other key issues identified by Dr. Katz and Office comments, where applicable:
L56: 1. Because of the risk of systemic spread of toxin after intramuscular injection, the Agency has determined that all botulinum products must include Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS). The sponsor submitted REMS materials, including a Medication Guide, Dear Health Care Provider letter, Dosing Card, and Physician and Patient Surveys, the latter to assess the effectiveness of the REMS. However, because these documents must bear Dysport's established name, and given that there is no agreed upon established name at this time, the submitted REMS are unacceptable and the product can not be approved at the time of this writing.2. Because botulism products have the potential to spread distantly from their sites of injection and lead to botulism, and in light of serious adverse events reported in association with off-label use of botulinum products for limb spasticity, the Division is requiring (under FDAAA provisions) that all sponsors of these products perform controlled studies in pediatric and adult subjects with lower limb spasticity, to determine whether these products are effective for that indication, and, if so, to determine the lowest effective dose.3. The Pharmacology/Toxicology Team notes concerns regarding a discrepancy between preliminary rabbit studies and the pivotal rabbit embryofetal toxicity study. In the former, a 20 Unit dose/animal was reasonably well-tolerated; in the latter, the same dose from a different batch was poorly tolerated, such that the group had to be terminated early. The review team recommends that the sponsor repeat the pivotal rabbit embryofetal developmental toxicology study, unless the sponsor can show that a 20 Unit dose can not be tolerated.4. The Division of Therapeutic Proteins (DTP), Office of Biotechnology Products, recommends a post-marketing requirement to establish tighter potency acceptance criteria for the qualification of new reference standards, and several post-marketing commitments (see DTP Review, pages 3-5).
L57: 5. Dr. Marc Stone identified changes in heart rate, laboratory values (serum bicarbonate, hepatic transaminases, glucose), and QT interval, either between the Dysport and placebo groups, or within the Dysport group (baseline versus on-treatment). On the basis of these findings, he recommended phase 4 commitments for the following: 1) a study to assess glucose control in subjects with diabetes; 2) a study of changes in ventilation, including serum bicarbonate, arterial blood gases, and pulmonary function tests, enriched if possible with subjects with mild obstructive lung disease; 3) a study of vital signs in the immediate post-treatment period in subjects with CD; 4) a thorough QT study; 5) a study to assess safety and effectiveness of Dysport in subjects of African, Asian, and Hispanic ancestry. He also recommended additional analyses of the effects of Dysport on hepatic function tests. Dr. Yasuda generally supported the views of Dr. Stone. She opined that changes in serum bicarbonate and glucose merited post-marketing requirements, and that vital signs and ECG/QT data could be collected in the context of another controlled study(ies). She also supported the need for a study of glucose control in diabetic patients as well as a study of Dysport in an ethnically diverse population. Following extensive discussions, however, the Division decided not to request additional studies to examine Dysport's respiratory effects, and not to request additional analyses of hepatic function tests or a study in subjects of African, Asian, and Hispanic ancestry.
L58: Given the plan to issue a CR letter, some issues remain under discussion by the Division. Specifically, the utility of a study in diabetic patients and consideration of a study of vital signs are topics of ongoing discussion by the Division. Of note, the currently proposed labeling addresses both of these issues under "6.1 Clinical Studies Experience:"
L59: • A subsection titled "Laboratory Findings" notes: "Subjects treated with Dysport exhibited a small increase in blood glucose relative to placebo-treated subjects. This was not clinically significant among subjects in the development program, but could be a factor in patients whose diabetes is difficult to control."• A subsection titled "Electrocardiographic Findings" notes the statistically significant (mean) 3 beat per minute decrease in heart rate, 30 minutes post-Dysport injection, and suggests monitoring of heart rate and ECG in patients with bradycardia or abnormalities of cardiac conduction.
L60: In my opinion, although these findings are of some interest and are statistically significant, they were detected using sensitive methods and must be considered in light of the multiplicity inherent in the analyses. Specifically, Table 7.1.7.3.2-1 on page 34 of Dr. Stone's safety review includes assessment of 34 laboratory parameters, each evaluated in two ways: Dysport versus placebo; and baseline versus on-treatment within the Dysport group. Thus, there were 68 possible parameters on which to detect a statistically significant difference. Although Dr. Stone took a number of factors into account when considering these findings, the key question is whether or not these treatment differences rise to the level of a "serious risk," given that a risk of this magnitude is necessary to warrant a post-marketing requirement under FDAAA. The language for glucose monitoring under the header "Clinical Studies Experience" seems appropriate to provide adequate instructions for use and mitigate risk. The language
L61: suggesting monitoring of heart rate and ECG in patients prone to bradycardia or abnormalities of cardiac conduction, based on a subtle finding without associated adverse events, seems overly conservative and probably unnecessary. Overall, therefore, given that the risks of serious adverse reactions related to hyperglycemia and bradycardia seem limited, and considering that the risks, if real, are mitigated in labeling, I do not favor post-marketing requirements to study changes in glucose in diabetic patients, or a study to assess acute changes in vital signs following Dysport injection.
L62: Dr. Stone recommend a thorough QT study; Dr. Yasuda recommended that the sponsor acquire QT data in the context of a clinical efficacy study, with a moxifloxacin control, if possible. Given that therapeutic biologic products are not generally thought to pose much risk of QT prolongation, and given that circulating Dysport concentrations are undoubtedly extremely low and, if present at all, present only transiently, the risk does not seem to justify a thorough QT study, and anything short of a thorough QT study is unlikely to be informative.
L63: Advisory Committee
L64: This application was not referred to an FDA advisory committee. Although Dysport is a therapeutic biologic and technically a new molecular entity, its active ingredient (Botulinum toxin Type A) is the same as that of Botox, which was approved under section 351 of the Public Health Service Act in 1991. The pharmacological properties of Botulinum toxin Type A are well-recognized and established, and Dysport's safety and efficacy data are consistent with expectations. Moreover, Myobloc, another product belonging to the botulinum class (Botulinum toxin Type B), has been approved in the US for CD since 2000. Thus, although Dysport is technically a new molecular entity, no differences between Dysport and Botox were expected, and none were apparent in the development program. For these reasons, early in the review cycle the Division decided not to refer Dysport to an advisory committee, and the Office concurred with this decision. In retrospect, this decision still seems correct.
L65: Other Comments
L66: Despite the fact that all subjects received the same initial dose (500 Units), based on the sponsor's analyses, there were no meaningful differences in the frequency or severity of adverse events between subjects with body mass index (BMI) &lt;25, 25-30, and &gt;30 kg/m2 (although the numbers of subjects in the latter two categories were small).
L67: Of note, there were probably no subjects with clinically significant pulmonary disease in the development program. Based on the pharmacologic mode of action, and, as supported by changes in serum bicarbonate noted in the clinical trials by Dr. Stone, such patients might be expected to be at higher risk with Dysport. The Botox package insert does not mention increased risk in patients with underlying pulmonary insufficiency; presumably the risk of administering Dysport to a patient with CO2 retention would be readily recognized by a provider, and is not necessary for labeling.
L68: Dr. Stone reported that 3.6% of subjects with negative neutralizing antibody titers at baseline developed antibodies on treatment with Dysport; however, there was no apparent effect on efficacy. The situation seems similar to that with interferon \beta-1b in multiple sclerosis, where the development of neutralizing antibodies is well-described, but there are no known clinical consequences. Dr. Stone also sought evidence of hypersensitivity reactions in the controlled trial safety database, and found no important differences in the frequencies of pruritis, urticaria, asthma, wheezing, or hypotension between Dysport and placebo.
L69: Review Team Findings
L70: Product
L71: The Division of Therapeutic Proteins, Office of Biotechnology Products, is recommending approval of Dysport manufactured by Ipsen, Biopharm limited. They opined that the manufacture of purified Clostridium botulinum neurotoxin type A complex, naturally secreted by C. botulinum, is well controlled, producing a consistent product that is potent and free of endogenous or adventitious infectious agents. The product will be on lot release per 21CFR 610.
L72: Dysport was first registered in 1990 in the United Kingdom (UK) for the treatment of blepharospasm and hemifacial spasm. Subsequently, the product has been registered in over 70 countries for various indications, including also adult post-stroke arm spasticity, cervical dystonia (CD), pediatric cerebral palsy spasticity, hyperhidrosis, and glabellar lines.
L73: Dysport is supplied as a sterile single use vial containing 500 units of lyophilized C. botulinum toxin type A, lactose monohydrate (2.5 mg) and human albumin (125 \mug). Each vial is intended for single patient use, and the formulation is preservative-free.
L74: (b) (4)
L75: In addition to the post-marketing requirement to establish a (b) (4), DTP requested several post-marketing commitments (see DTP review, pages 3-5).
L76: Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L77: The Pharmacology/Toxicology Team has recommended approval of Dysport. A limited battery of non-clinical studies was submitted, in light of the large prior human experience with Dysport, and the nature of the product. The sponsor performed a 6-month (with a 4-week recovery) pivotal toxicology study in the rat, a fertility and embryonic developmental study in rat, embryofetal development studies in rat and rabbit, and a pre- and post-natal development study in rat. In general, these studies identified predicted/expected issues. However, the primary reviewer noted a discrepancy between the preliminary rabbit studies and the pivotal rabbit embryofetal toxicity study (see Summary, page 2 of this memo).
L78: In addition, the Dysport drug substance lot used for the reproductive toxicology studies contained significantly lower levels of the flagellin contaminant (b) (4) than the to-be-marketed product (b) (4). The review team recommends repeating the pivotal rabbit embryofetal developmental toxicology study using a lot of Dysport that contains the highest available flagellin concentration, unless the applicant can show that such a dose would not be tolerated.
L79: Clinical Pharmacology
L80: The sponsor did not submit any pharmacokinetic studies in support of the BLA because there is no validated method to assess circulating levels of botulinum toxin type A hemagglutinin complex; moreover, the administration of quantities of product that would lead to measurable blood levels would be unethical. The review team agrees with the proposed Pharmacokinetic section of labeling, which states that the product is not expected to be present in peripheral blood at measurable levels following intramuscular or intradermal administration at recommended doses.
L81: Evidence of Effectiveness
L82: Ipsen submitted the results of two randomized, placebo-controlled, parallel group studies in support of effectiveness (studies 45 and 51), and they have been well-summarized by the review team. They were essentially the same in design: subjects with CD were randomized to receive Dysport 500 units, or placebo, injected in up to 4
L83: muscles. The Toronto Western Spasmodic Torticollis Rating Scale (TWSTRS), which has been accepted by the Agency as a primary endpoint measure for related agents, was used both to determine study eligibility and as the 1o efficacy endpoint. Although the studies were purportedly 12 weeks in duration, the 1o endpoint, change from baseline in TWSTRS total score, was assessed at Week 4. The efficacy results were statistically robust in both studies: in Study 51, the adjusted mean TWSTRS total score improved by 15.6 in the Dysport group, compared with 6.7 in the placebo group (p &lt; 0.0001); in Study 45, the corresponding changes in TWSTRS total score were 9.6 and 3.7 (p &lt; 0.0013). In both studies, the treatment effect was similar for subgroups based on age (&lt;, \geq 65), sex, geographic location (Russia vs. US for Study 51), and prior exposure to botulinum toxin.
L84: Interpretation of data beyond Week 4 was problematic, because many subjects discontinued treatment (approximately half of the subjects in both studies discontinued by Week 8; approximately three-quarters of subjects by Week 12). The Clinical Studies section of labeling originally submitted by the applicant has been edited, accordingly, with removal of Week 8 data from Study 45 (where subject retention was particularly poor), and removal of Week 12 data from both studies.
L85: (b) (4)
L86: Safety
L87: There were 365 subjects in the controlled CD trials, and these subjects received approximately 946 Dysport treatments. The population was &gt;99\% Caucasian and 58% female, with a median age of 51 years. The sponsor proposes to permit single doses of \leq 1000 Units as often as q12 weeks. This is supported by data from the extension studies, where 152 subjects received a mean dose of 730 Units at cycle 4, and 66 subjects received a mean dose of 795 Units at cycle 5. Approximately 150 doses of 1000 Units were administered in the controlled studies (Dr. Stone's review, page 51, figure 7.2.1.3-1).
L88: The sponsor also provided support of safety from uncontrolled studies in CD and other indications. There are phase 4 post-marketing surveillance data from approximately 3000 patients who collectively received approximately 6000 injections. About two-thirds of this experience was in patients with focal dystonias, and one-third from patients with CD.
L89: The adverse events were consistent with the known pharmacologic actions of the toxin. In the controlled studies, there were only two adverse events categorized as leading to discontinuation, and no deaths. There were 5 deaths in studies of post-stroke subjects, but none could be reasonably related to treatment. There were additional 12 deaths in post-marketing studies. In the few cases that could be reasonably considered possibly related to Dysport, the action was consistent with the known pharmacological effects of the product, and the risk seems to be adequately mitigated in the proposed labeling.
L90: MEMORANDUM
L91: DATE: December 23, 2008FROM: DirectorDivision of Neurology Products/HFD-120TO: File, BLA 125274/0SUBJECT: Recommendation for Action on BLA 125274/0, for the use of Dysport (submitted as Clostridium botulinum type A hemagglutinin complex) in the treatment of Cervical Dystonia (CD)
L92: BLA 125274/0, for the use of Dysport (submitted as Clostridium botulinum type A hemagglutinin complex) in the treatment of Cervical Dystonia (CD), was submitted by Ipsen Biopharm Limited on 11/29/07. The application contains the results of two randomized controlled trials in patients with CD, as well as long-term, open label safety experience and other relevant data.
L93: The application has been reviewed by Dr. Carole Davis, medical officer, Dr. Ohidul Siddiqui, statistician, Dr. Marc Stone, safety team reviewer, Dr. Sally Yasuda, safety team leader, Dr. Barbara Wilcox, pharmacologist, Dr. Lois Freed, pharmacology team leader, Dr. Amy Toscano and Michelle Safarik, Division of Drug Marketing, Advertising, and Communications (DDMAC), Sharon Mills, Division of Risk Management, Sheila Rawls and Drs. Ennan Guan, Susan Kirshner, and Amy Rosenberg, Office of Biotechnology Products, Dr. Vaneeta Tandon, Office of Clinical Pharmacology, Jose Javier Tavarez, Division of Scientific Investigations, Drs. Brenda Uratani, Donald Obenhuber, and Patricia F. Hughes, DMPQ, and Dr. Devanand Jillapalli, neurology team leader. The review team has concluded that the sponsor has submitted substantial evidence of effectiveness for Dysport as a treatment for CD, and that they have also provided adequate safety data for the recommended dosing regimen. However, there are several issues still unresolved (that I will briefly describe below) that preclude approval at this time. In this memo, I will very briefly summarize the effectiveness and safety data, and offer the rationale for the division's recommendation for action on this BLA.
L94: Effectiveness
L95: As described in the clinical and statistical memos, the sponsor has submitted the results of two randomized, placebo-controlled, parallel group trials in patients with CD, Studies 45 and 51. In each study, patients were randomized to receive either Dysport, a total of 500 Units (to be injected into a maximum of 4 muscles) or placebo, given once. Although the trials were ostensibly of 12 weeks duration, the primary outcome was to be assessed at 4 weeks. The primary outcome, the TWSTRS, a scale that rates symptom severity and disability, has served as the
L96: primary outcome in studies of other similar agents and has previously been accepted by the Agency. At 4 weeks, there were statistically significant differences between treatment and placebo in both studies on the primary outcome, and on several related secondary outcomes. The sponsor continued to assess effectiveness out to 12 weeks, but the discontinuation pattern made interpretation of these later time points difficult. Specifically, in Study 45, 45% (Dysport) and 64% (Placebo) patients had data imputed for Week 8, and 69% (drug) and 78% (Placebo) patients had data imputed for Week 12. This very large number of discontinuations makes it impossible to reliably assess whether the drug is still working beyond 4 weeks. In Study 51, there were fewer discontinuations. However, although the LOCF analyses of this study yielded nominally significant p-values for both Weeks 8 and 12, LOCF analyses are problematic when assessing effectiveness over time. As noted by Dr. Kun Jin, biometrics team leader, analysis of the observed cases at Week 12 in Study 51 is not significant (though it is for Week 8). For this reason, we cannot conclude that, in this study, the drug demonstrated effectiveness beyond Week 8.
L97: The sponsor had proposed that numerous secondary outcomes be described in product labeling. However, as described by Dr. Jillapalli, we have proposed that they not be permitted to do so. This division has adopted a general policy in which the following elements must be satisfied in order for secondary outcomes to be described in labeling: there must be a prospective statistical plan that preserves the Type 1 error for the secondary outcomes, there must be replication, the secondary outcomes must assess a clinical domain distinct from that of the primary outcome, and, typically, the inclusion of specific secondary outcomes must be agreed to in advance with the division. In Study 45, there was no prospective statistical plan to preserve the Type 1 error for the secondary outcomes, but, more importantly in this case, essentially all of the sponsor's proposed secondary outcomes assessed clinical outcomes that were simple variations on the primary outcome (this is true for Study 51 also). Primarily for this reason, we have declined to describe these outcomes in product labeling.
L98: Each study included an open-label extension, in which patients could be continued on treatment, or switched to treatment if they had been randomized to placebo in the controlled trial. In these extensions, patients could be treated every 12-16 weeks, with individual total doses of up to 1000 Units. Because these extensions were open-label and uncontrolled, they do not provide substantial evidence of effectiveness for long-term treatment.
L99: Safety
L100: The sponsor has submitted safety experience from controlled studies in CD and other indications, and also from open-label, uncontrolled studies in CD and other indications. The sponsor has proposed, in product labeling, to permit individual doses of up to 1000 Units be given (no more frequently than every 12 weeks), and they have submitted safety experience in patients with CD who have
L101: received many cycles of treatment with doses up to greater than 2000 Units. For example, Sponsor's Table 7.2.1.3-1, reprinted in Dr. Stone's review, page 51, shows 152 patients received 157 treatment cycles, with a mean dose of 730 Units, and 66 patients with 165 cycles (at least 5 cycles/patient), with a mean dose of about 800 Units. Sponsor's Table 7.2.1.1, reprinted in Dr. Stone's review, pages 46-49, describes the studies in which safety data has been obtained, including experience in about 3700 treated patients and about 7300 total treatments.
L102: Most of the adverse events seen with this treatment are entirely consistent with the known pharmacologic actions of this treatment, including the serious adverse events and adverse events that led to discontinuation (though there were very few of either). There were 5 deaths in studies of post-stroke patients, and 12 post-marketing deaths. None of the post-stroke deaths could reasonably be related to the treatment, and the few post-marketing deaths that could reasonably be considered possibly treatment-related were also consistent with the known pharmacologic effects of the treatment.
L103: Dr. Stone has, however, identified several adverse events or laboratory abnormalities that he believes should be further investigated.
L104: Regarding laboratory abnormalities, as Dr. Stone notes, labs were assessed only in the two controlled studies and in their open-label extensions. In Study 45, labs were assessed at baseline and at Weeks 4, 8, and 12. In Study 51, labs were assessed at baseline, Week 4, and at study end (typically Week 12). In the open-label extension to Study 51, labs were assessed on the day of re-treatment, and 4 weeks after any re-treatment. In the open-label extension to Study 45, labs were assessed 4 weeks after any re-treatment. His analyses consider the results of the drug-placebo comparisons in the controlled trial, as well as within-patient comparisons (that is, values obtained in the open-label treatment compared to values obtained on those patients who received placebo in the controlled portion).
L105: Overall, there were very minor mean changes for lab tests (see Dr. Stone's tables 7.1.7.3.1-1 and -2, pages 24 and 25). However, he highlights several specific changes.
L106: 1) Serum bicarbonate-he notes a modest increase on Dysport (compared to placebo) in mean bicarbonate in the controlled trials that becomes more prominent in the comparison of values on drug compared to their previous placebo values. He concludes that "...there is good reason to believe that this could be a cumulative effect of respiratory acidosis caused by respiratory depression from Dysport."2) Serum glucose-he notes that this laboratory parameter is the only one where "...the effect of Dysport is more prominent in the double-blind
L107: period than in the on drug/off drug comparison.", although the changes in both phases are small (geometric means: 3.9% vs. 0.2%, respectively).
L108: According to Dr. Stone, 5 patients had clinically significant values on open-label Dysport. Most of these patients had maximum glucose levels of about 150, but one had a maximum of 308 (also associated with an increase of Lefts, with a maximum SGPT of 308), and one had a maximum of 207 (this latter patient had a baseline level of 148).
L109: 3) Liver Function Tests (LFTS)-Dr. Stone notes that the slight mean elevation of SGPT and SGOT in the controlled trials is likely due to a single patient with Hepatitis C, but also notes an increase in the open-label/previous placebo comparison (geometric means: SGPT 6.2%, SGOT 2%).
L110: According to Dr. Stone, besides the patient noted above under 2), and the patient with Hepatitis C, there was one patient with a maximum SGPT of 204. No patient had a meaningful increase in bilirubin.
L111: 4) EKG-only one study, the extension to Study 51, evaluated EKG, and then only in a sub-set of 25 patients. As Dr. Stone describes, on visits at which patients were to be re-treated (usually 12-16 weeks apart), they were to have 3 EKG (separated by 1-2 minutes) prior to treatment (baseline), then 3 EKGs (separated by 1-2 minutes) 30 minutes after injection. At follow-up, about 4 weeks after treatment, 3 more EKGs (separated by 1-2 minutes) were to be taken. A similar strategy was to be followed at the last visit.
L112: In actuality, 11 subjects had appropriate testing up to and including the 4 week follow-up (but not at last visit), 2 subjects had complete testing, and one subject had one complete cycle of testing and baseline and 30 minute post-treatment EKGs for a second cycle.
L113: As Dr. Stone notes, there was a slight (about 4%) average decrease in heart rate at the 30 minute post-treatment time point. In those patients with 4 week data, this decrease is resolved by that point. He also notes a mean increase in QT interval of about 9 msec that persists after a Fredericia correction, but that is not apparent at 4 weeks post-treatment. No change in heart rate was seen in controlled trials, although, as he describes, the timing of the testing was not described, so that it could have been assessed prior to dosing (in other words, many weeks after injection). Upon examination of outliers, there was a signal for the proportion of patients with a heart rate under 60 bpm on treatment compared to placebo, consistent with the mean effect on heart rate (see Table 7.1.9.3.2-1, page 40 of Dr. Stone's review). There was also one patient who experienced a QT interval of &gt;500 msec (average of 506 msec at 30 minutes after treatment, with a baseline of 493 msec).
L114: Based on these findings, and the lack of data in several populations, Dr. Stone has recommended the following studies be performed in Phase 4:
L115: 1) a study in diabetics2) a study of changes in ventilation including assessment of various related measures3) a study to assess the effects of treatment in the immediate post-injection period4) a thorough QT study5) a study of safety and effectiveness in patients of African, Asian, and Hispanic descent
L116: Dr. Stone also recommends additional analyses of the effects of Dysport on LFTs.
L117: Pharmacology
L118: The sponsor performed a 6 month toxicity study in rat, a fertility and embryonic development study in rat, embryo-fetal development studies in rat and rabbit, and a pre- and post-natal development study in rat. In general, these studies identified expected issues, but Dr. Wilcox has concluded that the embryo-fetal development study in rabbits should be repeated. In this study, most animals in the high dose group (20 Units) died, a finding in contrast to an earlier preliminary study in which a 20 Units dose was well tolerated. Because of the deaths in the HD group, no embryo-fetal data were collected, and there were slight decreases in fetal weight and a slightly increased incidence of malformed fetuses in the mid-dose group (10 Units). There was no safety margin between the MD group and the maximum proposed human dose of 1000 Units. Because the findings at the MD group suggest the possibility of significant findings at a higher dose, and because the mortality in the HD group precluded any examination of the fetuses (and because these deaths were not seen in an earlier study at this dose), the pharmacology staff recommends that this study be repeated in Phase 4. However, as Dr. Freed notes, if the sponsor can demonstrate that a 20 Unit dose is truly not tolerable, the study need not be repeated.
L119: Dr. Freed also notes that flagellin is present in the final product, and the sponsor is proposing a specification of up to (b) (4) for this protein contaminant. For the reasons Dr. Freed lists, however, this is acceptable (for example, humans are typically exposed to flagellin as a component of bacteria, flagellin is only present in Dysport as peptide fragments, not as the biologically active intact protein, see her memo, pages 2,3).
L120: CMC
L121: The staff of the Division of Therapeutic Proteins has recommended that the
L122: application be approved, although they have proposed that the sponsor provide, in Phase 4, considerable additional information.
L123: Comments
L124: Dysport is a botulinum toxin Type A product that has been marketed since 1990 in Europe. In the United States, another botulinum toxin Type A product, Botox, is marketed for the treatment of cervical dystonia, blepharospasm, hyperhidrosis, and glabellar lines. A botulinum toxin Type B product, Myobloc, is also marketed in the United States for the treatment of CD. It should also be pointed out that Ipsen has submitted to the Agency another BLA for the use of this product in the treatment of glabellar lines (although they have proposed a different tradename, Reloxin, for this use); that application is currently under review. The adverse effects of these products, all similar, are well known, but there are several issues related to these products that require discussion; I will describe these below.
L125: Regarding Dysport, the sponsor has submitted two adequate and well controlled studies that constitute substantial evidence of effectiveness of a dose of 500 Units of Dysport, divided among up to 4 muscles, as a treatment for Cervical Dystonia (CD). The sponsor has concluded that beneficial effects have been demonstrated in controlled trials out to 12 weeks. I do not believe that this has been demonstrated. As discussed above, I believe that the sponsor has demonstrated, in Study 51 alone, a beneficial effect out to 8 weeks post-treatment (though this has not been replicated), and this finding in Study 51 can be described in product labeling.
L126: The sponsor proposes to describe, in product labeling, the use of Dysport at doses of up to 1000 Unit, at intervals no shorter than every 12 weeks. Although there is no controlled trial data that establishes Dysport's effectiveness for this duration, I believe it is reasonable to provide dosing recommendations for this proposed regimen. Given the well accepted pharmacology of these agents, it is reasonable to conclude that the effects of a given injection with this product are independent of the effects of previous injections, if the treatments are given sufficiently separated in time. The appropriate separation in time would depend, in my view, on the demonstrated safety of any proposed regimen. In this case, I believe the sponsor has submitted sufficient evidence of the safety of their proposed regimen; that is, of doses of up to 1000 Units (reached with an appropriate titration scheme, as the sponsor has proposed), given no more frequently than every 12 weeks. Although practitioners would be free to treat more frequently (if they believe the effects of a single treatment have worn off before 12 weeks), the submitted safety experience supports only a once every 12 week regimen.
L127: However, before this application can be approved, we recommend that the issues noted below need to be addressed.
L128: As noted above, if Dysport is approved, it will be the second botulinum toxin Type A product marketed in the United States. It is critical to note at this point that the potency of Dysport and Botox (the other botulinum toxin Type A product) are quite different, and the existence of two toxic compounds with identical established names and different potencies might pose a significant risk of medication errors with potentially serious clinical consequences. Because of this, Allergan, the manufacturer of Botox, in anticipation of Dysport's marketing, petitioned USAN for a change in established name. USAN approved the established name onaclostox for Botox, and Allergan had asked the Agency to permit it to use that name, in order to distinguish Botox from Dysport. Initially, the Agency refused Allergan's request (letter from me to Allergan dated 8/6/08), after a discussion that included the directors of the Office of Medical Policy and the Office of New Drugs, as well as others, because it was felt that prescribers would not identify onaclostox with the well known dangers of botulinum toxin. However, after further reflection, the Agency has decided (again, based on discussions with upper management, including the Director of the Center for Drug Evaluation and Research, that followed an additional request from Allergan) that each botulinum toxin product must have a distinct established name, so as to minimize the likelihood of interchanging these different-potency products. The Agency has rejected the USAN adopted name of onaclostox for Botox, because it is insufficiently reflective of the fact that these are botulinum toxins. At a meeting of Agency staff, it was decided that each of these products would have an established name with the common root "botulinumtoxinA (or B)", and that each product would have a distinct prefix (of no more than 5 letters) that was devoid of meaning. Accordingly, we have asked Ipsen to supply us with candidate established names meeting this rule. They have supplied us with 4 such options, none of which were acceptable (b) (4)
L129: Beyond the issue of potential name confusion, the Agency has recently undertaken detailed analyses of reports of systemic botulism occurring after use of botulinum toxin products. Although current Botox labeling describes the rare occurrence of such events, the statement in labeling is limited to a description of these cases in patients with underlying neuromuscular conditions. The Agency's recent reviews have established the potential for systemic botulism (including respiratory failure) in patients without conditions that could reasonably be considered neuromuscular conditions. Specifically, our review had detected cases of "distant" spread of toxin after injection of leg muscles in pediatric patients with spasticity, as well as after (though less frequently) injection in the legs of adults with spasticity and after all other labeled uses (including "cosmetic" use). Although these events appear to be quite rare, they can have (and in some cases have had) devastating consequences.
L130: For these reasons (lack of interchangeability of the products and the risk of
L131: systemic botulism), the Agency has determined that all botulinum products must have Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS). Ipson has submitted the requested materials for a REMS, including a Medication Guide, a Dear Health Care Provider letter, a Dosing Card, and Physician and Patient Surveys, the latter designed to assess the success of the REMS. Although numerous of these documents have been reviewed by Agency staff, not all have as of this writing. Further, and as noted above, these documents must include the new established name, and make clear that this is still a botulinum toxin product. However, also as noted above, we do not yet have an acceptable established name for Dysport. For this reason, then, the application cannot be approved at this time.
L132: In addition, because we have determined that botulinum toxin products spread distantly from the site of injection and cause systemic symptoms, and because many of the cases of concern have resulted from off-label use of these products in (mostly) pediatric and adult patients with spasticity, we are requiring (under FDAAA provisions) that all sponsors of these products perform controlled trials in adults and pediatric patients with lower limb spasticity. These studies will attempt to identify effective doses in these settings (including lowest effective doses). We do not expect that they will be able to identify effective doses that are not associated with systemic spread, because these adverse events are quite rare, and would not be expected to be seen in small controlled trials (and, of course, because therapeutic doses may, in fact, cause, in rare cases, these adverse events). Nonetheless, it is important to identify whether or not the treatment is effective for this indication, and, if so, the lowest effective dose.
L133: With regard to labeling, our original proposal was to include language in the Warnings and Precautions section describing the risk of systemic spread of toxin. However, after recent discussions with members of the staff, including members of the staff of the DDDP and of the Office of Regulatory Policy (the latter are involved in drafting a response to a Citizen's Petition regarding the class issue of systemic spread), we are currently re-thinking the placement of this warning. Specifically, we are currently considering the placement of this information in a Boxed Warning. These discussions are on-going, and a definitive decision about this has not yet been made.
L134: Regarding the pharmacology issue described above, we will ask the sponsor to perform another pre- and post-natal development study in rabbit, unless, as described by Dr. Freed, the sponsor can demonstrate that a 20 Unit dose is not tolerated.
L135: We will also ask the sponsor to provide the additional CMC information requested by the staff of the Division of Therapeutic Proteins (see their review, pages 3-5).
L136: Regarding the post-marketing studies requested by Dr. Stone, the review team has had extensive discussions about these proposals. We have agreed, as a
L137: team, that we will not request additional studies examining the respiratory effects (including serum bicarbonate) of injection with Dysport or a study in patients of African, Asian, or Hispanic descent, or additional analyses of LFTs. Whether or not we will require Phase 4 studies in patients with diabetes or a study to further examine the acute effects on vital signs has not yet been settled. The findings to date are described in the current version of draft labeling (with which the sponsor agrees), and they feel that, given these labeling statements, additional studies are not required. The arguments in favor of requiring these studies are largely that there may be patients who could suffer significant harm from these potential effects, despite the small mean effects seen in the studies to date (indeed, we have no formally collected data in patients with diabetes). On the other hand, it is reasonable to ask whether or not any study that could reasonably be done is likely to identify such a (potentially small) group of patients at higher risk. In any event, because we are recommending that we issue a CR letter at this time, we do not have to reach a final decision on these requirements, and we recommend that these discussions continue with the signatory authority.
L138: For the reasons stated above, then, (in particular, because we do not have an acceptable established name, and the resultant REMS documents cannot, therefore, be finalized at this time) we recommend that the attached CR letter be issued.
L139: A handwritten signature in black ink, appearing to be "R Katz".
L140: Russell Katz, M.D.

SubjectClinical Team Leader Review Memo

Recommended:Complete Response
L143: 1. Introduction
L144: In this original BLA #1787 (Submission tracking number #125274) submitted on 11/29/07, the sponsor [Ipsen Biopharm Limited (IBL), UK] is seeking the approval of Dysport for Injection (Clostridium botulinum toxin type A hemagglutinin complex) for the treatment of patients with cervical dystonia to reduce the severity of abnormal head position, disability and neck pain in both toxin naïve and previously treated patients. Cervical dystonia (also called spasmodic Torticollis) is the most common of the focal dystonias affecting approximately 20,000 – 125,000 people in the US. Although cervical dystonia can occur at any age, symptoms typically begin in the fourth decade of life. Approximately 10-20% of subjects with cervical dystonia experience spontaneous remission typically early in the course but the remission does not last long. In the US, two botulinum toxin products have been approved for the treatment of cervical dystonia: Botox (approved on 12/9/91) and Myobloc (approved on 12/8/00).
L145: The Agency designated Dysport as an orphan drug for cervical dystonia on 8/12/98. The sponsor also has filed another separate BLA with the Division of Dermatology Products for the cosmetic indication.
L146: 2. Background
L147: Dysport was first licensed in UK in 1990 for the treatment of blepharospasm and hemifacial spasm. Since then, it has been licensed in over 72 countries for the following indications:
L148: blepharospasm, adult post-stroke arm spasticity, hemifacial spasm, cervical dystonia, pediatric cerebral palsy spasticity, hyperhidrosis, and glabellar lines.
L149: Under BB-IND 7434, two randomized placebo-controlled studies were conducted: Y-97-52120-045 and Y-47-52120-051 (referred to as Study 45 and Study 51, respectively, in this review). Study 51 was the subject of a special protocol assessment. Subjects from Study 45 entered open-label Study 45b where they received re-treatments with Dysport in doses ranging from 250 to 1000 units, i.e., at doses adapted to subject response. Subjects from Study 51 entered open-label Study 731, which is ongoing. This BLA includes an interim report of safety data from Study 731 as of 9/20/06 (ECG data as of 5/31/07).
L150: A pre-BLA meeting was held on 12/5/06, which is referred to in the relevant sections of this review.
L151: In response to the Division's request, the sponsor submitted a Communication Plan as part of Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategy on 9/8/08. The review clock was extended by three months (to 12/28/08) to facilitate review of this material.
L152: 3. CMC/Device
L153: Dr. Ennan Guan from the Division of Therapeutic Proteins, Office of Biotechnology Products, was the primary reviewer. Dr. Susan Kirshner was the secondary reviewer. Both Drs. Guan and Kirshner recommend the approval of Dysport for human use under the conditions specified in the package insert.
L154: Loss of potency over time:
L155: (b) (4)
L156: (b) (4)
L157: (b) (4)
L158: Post-marketing requirements and commitments: The Division of Therapeutic Proteins proposes one post-marketing requirement (b) (4), and several post-marketing commitments.
L159: 4. Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L160: This review is pending.
L161: 5. Clinical Pharmacology/Biopharmaceutics
L162: During the pre-BLA meeting on 12/5/06, the Agency agreed with the sponsor's argument that since Dysport is an extremely potent neurotoxin, having low therapeutic dose and localized action, it is not likely to have any measurable systemic exposure at doses administered, and therefore, formal systematic PK assessment was not necessary. In her brief clinical pharmacology review memo, Dr. V. Tandon, states that the BLA has no clinical PK information to review and therefore, has no labeling recommendations from a clinical pharmacology standpoint.
L163: 6. Clinical Microbiology
L164: Not applicable.
L165: 7. Clinical/Statistical- Efficacy
L166: Dr. C. Davis was the clinical efficacy reviewer, and Dr. O. Siddiqui was the statistical reviewer for this application. Both Drs. Davis and Siddiqui recommend approval action. The
L167: sponsor primarily relied on the results of two randomized placebo-controlled phase 3 studies (Study 45 and Study 51) in support of this BLA.
L168: Design: Study 45 and Study 51 were both similarly designed. Study 51 was subject of special protocol assessment. Both studies were randomized (1:1), placebo-controlled, double-blind, multicenter, single dose followed by a planned 12-week observation period, parallel group study of the efficacy and safety of Dysport for the treatment of cervical dystonia. Study 45 was conducted only in the US (13 centers) and Study 51 was conducted in US (16 centers) and Russia (4 centers). Eligible subjects were \geq 18 years of age, had cervical dystonia \geq 18 months since onset, were either toxin-naïve or previously treated with botulinum toxin (who had to have at least a 16-week washout period prior to study entry), and had a Toronto Western Spasmodic Torticollis Rating Scale (TWSTRS) total score \geq 30, a severity sub-scale score of \geq 15 and a disability sub-scale score of \geq 3. Subjects randomized to Study 45 or Study 51 received either a single administration of 500 units of Dysport or placebo. Efficacy assessments including TWSTRS were done at baseline and, weeks 4, 8, and 12 weeks post-treatment; however, subjects had the option of switching to open-label extension studies after week 4 assessments. The single dose of 500 units was divided for administration as IM injection to a maximum of four clinically indicated muscles in a single dosing session, with or without EMG guidance; the left and right muscle of a muscle group were considered as two muscles.
L169: The primary efficacy variable in both studies was the change from baseline to week 4 in the TWSTRS total score. The TWSTRS total score ranged from 0 – 85. A decrease in the TWSTRS score reflects an improvement in a subject's cervical dystonia. The TWSTRS has three subscales: severity (maximum score = 35); disability (maximum score = 30); and pain (maximum score = 20).
L170: The secondary variables and their statistical plan of analyses were different in these two studies. In Study 51, there were 10 secondary endpoints ranked in order of priority and tested sequentially for statistical significance (further discussed below), whereas in Study 45, there were many secondary and tertiary endpoints without correction for multiplicity of testing.
L171: Efficacy was assessed in the intent-to-treat (ITT) population, defined as all subjects as randomized. For Study 45, missing data at weeks 4, 8 and 12 were imputed using the baseline value. However, for Study 51, missing data at weeks 4 were imputed using the baseline value, but the Last Observation was carried forward for missing values of TWSTRS scores at weeks 8 and 12. The primary and secondary efficacy variables (using numerical change from baseline) were analyzed with an analysis of covariance (ANCOVA). Logistics model was used for variables using proportion of treatment successes. In the analysis strategy for Study 51, the primary variable and then 10 secondary efficacy variables were ranked in order of priority and tested sequentially for statistical significance at the 5% level. No claim of significance was to be made on any secondary endpoint ranked lower than the first nonsignificant result. In Study 45, the secondary variables were not ranked in order of priority. Additionally, in Study 45, when the potency of Batch 159J was found to be below minimum specifications, 57 subjects had already entered the study. Consequently, after discussion with the Agency, the sponsor recruited an additional 80 subjects (for a total of 137 subjects
L172: compared to the originally planned 80 subjects), so that efficacy analyses with sufficient power could be done on subjects who did not receive Batch 159J (Group 1 – see below). Therefore, in Study 45, the primary efficacy analysis was done on the ITT population of Group 1:
L173: • Group 1: All subjects who received batches other than 159J [total n=80 (Dysport = 37; placebo = 43)]
• Group 2: All subjects who received any batch of study drug.
• Group 3: All subjects who received treatment before potency issues with Batch 159J were found (10/16/98).
L174: Via communication dated 3/17/05, the Agency agreed that the TWSTRS is an acceptable clinical tool for the clinical assessment of cervical dystonia, and that the change in TWSTRS total score from baseline to 4 weeks after treatment is acceptable for demonstrating efficacy. Further, the Agency also agreed that the single-treatment, placebo-controlled, double-blind study design was adequate to assess efficacy and safety of Dysport in cervical dystonia. The Agency has prior regulatory experience with the use of the TWSTRS scale. Change in the TWSTRS total score at week 4 was the primary efficacy outcome measure in two phase 3 studies that supported the approval of Myobloc (botulinum toxin Type B).
L175: Results: A total of 136 subjects were randomized in Study 45 and 116 (75.6% in the US; remaining in Russia) subjects in Study 51. Drs. Siddiqui and Davis, in their review find that there was a statistically significant beneficial effect of Dysport (500 units) over placebo in the change from baseline in the TWSTRS total score at 4 weeks (primary endpoint), in both Study 45 (p = 0.013) and Study 51 (p &lt; 0.0001) [see Table 2]. The efficacy results for the TWSTRS total score at week 4 is also supported by other secondary measures of subject and investigator assessing the change in the symptoms and signs of cervical dystonia using Visual Analog Scale (VAS) assessment at week 4 (Table 1). In Study 45, a global assessment of efficacy and safety was also performed by the investigator on completion of the study; overall treatment success was defined as a global efficacy assessment of at least moderate improvement and a global safety assessment of no worse than moderate in regards to adverse events. There were more subjects in the Dysport group (35%) than in the placebo group (23%) who were considered treatment successes. Since the majority of the subjects exited Study 45 after week 4, and therefore completed this study at this time point (see discussion below), the results of the global measure are for the most part a reflection of the efficacy at week 4, and supportive of the efficacy of Dysport at week 4. Study 51 also had similar global measure, but the majority of the subjects went on to complete the study at 12 weeks.

L181: Dr. Siddiqui's review also finds a greater improvement in TWSTRS score at 4 weeks for Dysport compared to placebo in both the elderly (\geq 65 years) and in the non-elderly subjects, consistently in both Study 45 and Study 51. There was also similar efficacy of Dysport over placebo between male and female subjects, and between US and non-US subjects. During the pre-BLA meeting held on 12/5/06, the Agency told the sponsor to provide analyses of the efficacy of Dysport in toxin-naïve versus toxin-treated subjects. There were 20 (17%; n=116) subjects in Study 51, and 21 (25%; n=80) in Study 45 (Group 1) who were naïve to botulinum toxin. Dr. Davis concludes that the treatment benefit for Dysport over placebo in the naïve subjects was comparable to that in the non-naïve subjects. In addition, dose-related efficacy was demonstrated in Study 20 conducted in Germany which enrolled 75 subjects, all of whom were toxin-naïve. This study is further discussed under Dosing Regimen section below.
L182: Duration of effectiveness: The sponsor's analyses show a statistically significant effect in favor of Dysport for the change from baseline in TWSTRS total score at weeks 8 and 12 in both Study 51 and Study 45 (summarized in Table 2). Dr. Davis, in her review, states that the high rate of subjects exiting early (after week 4) to receive open-label treatment, particularly in Study 45, limits the efficacy conclusions that can be drawn at these later time points. Dr. Siddiqui has similar concerns about Study 45 but not for Study 51. The proportion of subjects completing week 8 and 12 (in Study 51) in the Dysport and placebo group were 82% and 75%, and 82% and 62%, respectively. All subjects who withdrew in the placebo group did so for insufficient clinical response compared to only 9% in the Dysport group who did so for the same reason, by itself supporting the efficacy of Dysport at these later time points. LOCF was the method to impute missing data at week 8 and 12. Using LOCF ANCOVA analyses of the primary measure, p-values were &lt;0.0001 and 0.019 at week 8 and 12, respectively. However, carrying forward values from earlier time points when the efficacy is likely greater than at later time points may not be appropriate to assess the efficacy at later time points. For this reason, and since the p-value for the observed cases analyses at week 12 was 0.314, Dr. Kun Jin (statistical team leader) argues that he does not see convincing evidence to conclude that efficacy is maintained through week 12. I agree with Dr. Jin that there is not enough consistent evidence for efficacy at week 12 in Study 51.
L183: In contrast, in Study 45, the majority of subjects had left the study by week 12. Baseline scores were imputed for missing values at week 4, 8 and 12. As can be seen from Table 3, for Dysport and placebo groups, 45% and 64% of the subjects had baseline values imputed at week 8, and 69% and 78% of subjects had baseline values imputed at week 12, respectively. Even though the majority of subjects had imputed baseline values at week 8 and 12, statistical significance is still achieved at these weeks. Although difficult to verify, the most likely explanation is that there is a drop out bias in favor of Dysport.
L184: The sponsor proposes to include the efficacy results of the change from baseline in TWSTRS total score at weeks 8 and 12 for both studies (Table 2) in the Clinical Studies section of the label. For Study 45, in addition to the high drop out rate as discussed above, the change from baseline at week 8 and 12 were two of many secondary and tertiary endpoints that were not corrected for multiplicity. For these reasons, the efficacy results at week 8 and 12 for Study 45 should not be included in the label. For Study 51, there is a statistically significant change
L185: Results of late time pointsshg yk
L186: from baseline in the TWSTRS score at week 8 but not at week 12, and therefore efficacy information at week 12 should not be included in the label.

L194: Secondary endpoints: As pointed above, there were 10 secondary endpoints ranked in order of priority and tested sequentially for statistical significance in Study 51. During the pre-BLA meeting on 12/5/06, the Agency outlined the criteria for secondary variables that are intended for inclusion in the label: Replication; Secondary outcomes be from a different clinical domain than the primary outcome; FDA agreement in advance as to their acceptability; and a statistical plan for the secondary variables. The sponsor proposes to include the results of all the secondary endpoints in the Clinical studies section of the label. A description of these secondary endpoint results, as proposed, should not be allowed in the Clinical Studies section for the following reasons. The result of the first secondary endpoint (efficacy at week 8) is already included in the label (Table 2), and therefore their inclusion again is redundant. The second secondary endpoint (efficacy at week 12) should not be included for reasons discussed
L195: Handwritten: 2 adjs Phil
L196: above (duration of effectiveness). Since the statistical plan called for a no claim of significance on any secondary endpoint ranked lower than the first nonsignificant result, secondary outcomes #9 and #10 should not be included in the label, despite the sponsor's argument that secondary endpoint #10 provides an easily visualized comparison between treatment groups of the proportion of subjects benefiting from Dysport. For secondary endpoints # 3-8, the subject self-assessed cervical dystonia symptoms severity at weeks 4, 8 and 12, using a 0-100 mm visual analog scale of 'no symptoms' to 'worst possible symptoms'. Similarly, the investigator also assessed cervical dystonia symptoms using the same methodology at the same time points as the subject did. The clinical information captured by these secondary endpoints (# 3-8)) does not add to that already captured by the primary endpoint, TWSTRS. Although, historically in the label for Myobloc, patient global and physician global secondary endpoints used a 100 mm visual-analog scale, such analog scales have limitations. The clinical meaning of a change in total score over time is uncertain when using a visual analog scale since a change in response is based upon measuring differences in millimeters on a ruler, rather than using descriptive terms (e.g. mild, moderate, severe). Further, it is not clear how the physician assessment of cervical dystonia symptoms severity provides added information than subject assessment of cervical dystonia symptoms severity.
L197: Further, although there appears to have been some discussion at this meeting regarding secondary endpoints using TWSTRS scale at weeks 8 and 12 (to evaluate the duration of effectiveness) and subject-evaluated pain or quality of life, it is not clear from the minutes of this meeting if the Agency agreed to their acceptability for inclusion in the label.
L198: Dosing Regimen: As discussed above, the efficacy of the initial starting dose of 500 units has been established in both the phase 3 studies. This is also the recommended initial starting dose for Dysport in the United Kingdom for the treatment of cervical dystonia. The dose-ranging study (Study 20) provided basis for 500 units as the starting dose. Briefly, Study 20 was a placebo-controlled, dose ranging study that randomized (1:1:1:1) 75 subjects with cervical dystonia who were toxin-naïve into four parallel treatment groups to receive Dysport 250, 500 units, 1000 units or placebo. Efficacy was assessed using the Modified Tsui Scale at weeks 2, 4 and 8. The Modified Tsui Scale (used mainly in Europe in clinical trials of cervical dystonia) has a score that ranges from 0 – 43, and evaluates Amplitude of sustained movements, Shoulder elevation, and Head tremor. A reduction in the total score indicates improvement of cervical dystonia. At week 4, the mean Tsui score percentage reduction from baseline was 7% for placebo, 22% for 250 units group (not statistically significant compared to placebo), 25% for 500 units (p=0.045 compared to placebo), and 32% for 1000 units (p=0.045 compared to placebo), showing a dose response. There was dose-related increase in the frequency of adverse events: 30% in the placebo group, 37% in the 250 units group, 65% in the 500 units group, and 83% in the 1000 units group.
L199: For subsequent dose modification, the sponsor is proposing dose adjustment in 250 unit step according to patient's response every 12-16 weeks. The total dose administered in a single treatment ranges between 250 units to 1000 units. The recommendations for dose modification and single dose range come from the open-label extension of Study 45. After exiting the double-blind phase of Study 45 (when one injection was given after
L200: randomization), subjects entered the open-label phase (Study 45b) to receive additional three injections of Dysport. The first of these three injections was fixed at 500 units, but subsequent injections were titrated in 250 unit steps depending on the subject's clinical response, from a minimum of 250 units to a maximum of 1000 units. The minimum duration in between each injection was \geq 12 weeks. Efficacy was assessed by measuring the change from baseline in mean TWSTRS total score at week 4 for each retreatment period.
L201: Data from these three additional injection cycles in Study 45b show that the mean TWSTRS total score 4 weeks after each injection was lower (p &lt; 0.001) than the mean score at baseline, and the median re-treatment time was about 14-15 weeks. The mean total dose administered and Dose categories for each of the re-treatments are given the Table 4. From this table, it can be seen that the mean total dose administered at each succeeding retreatment progressively increased from 500 units. The majority of subjects at retreatments were on 500 unit doses or higher, and in a minority of subjects, the dose was actually decreased from 500 units (8% at Retreatment #2 and 5% at Retreatment #3). The efficacy for 250 unit dose in Study 20 has been discussed above. Data from this study also suggest that there was a greater benefit in women than in men for the 250 and 500 unit doses. Despite its limitations, open-label data from Study 45b does provide the only basis for the proposed dose modification, and the range of single dose for Dysport in the Dosage and Administration section of the label. However, "efficacy" data from this open-label study should not be included in the Clinical Studies section of the label.
