L205: Disability claim: The sponsor is also seeking to include an effect on disability in the Indication section of the label. Neither of the two approved botulinum toxin products (Botox or Myobloc) has this language (effect on disability) in the Indication section of the label. One of the three subscales of the TWSTRS is disability (maximum subscale score = 35). In both Study 51 and Study 45, there was a greater reduction in the disability subscale score for the Dysport group compared to placebo at week 4 and 8, and to a lesser extent at week 12. A claim of an effect on disability in the Indication section should not be granted for the following reasons. During the pre-BLA meeting on 12/5/06, the Agency told the sponsor that in regard to the TWSTR scale, the three evaluations must not be evaluated separately, but rather as a whole. Disability concept is distinct from the symptoms of cervical dystonia such as pain and abnormal neck position. Therefore, in order to support the claim of disability, independent substantiation should be necessary using a patient-reported instrument assessing disability based on input from the target population and shown to be valid. In this context, additional
L206: disability claim
L207: validation studies should be necessary if a single domain (disability) is selected from a previously validated multiple domain instrument (TWSTRS assesses severity, pain and disability) to support as a distinct claim (disability). Further, a change from 0 to 1 or vice versa in the six disability items of TWSTRS scale does not constitute a change in disability and has unclear clinical meaning.
L208: 8. Safety
L209: Dr. Marc Stone conducted the primary safety review. Secondary safety review was done by Dr. Sally Yasuda, the safety team leader.
L210: Safety Database and Exposures: The sponsor states that Dysport has been studied in over 80 clinical trials in a wide variety of neuromuscular indications throughout the world. Since many of these studies were of open-label design, and conducted according to the prevailing standards in the countries where they were conducted, these studies were classified in this BLA application into the following categories (the Agency agreed to this during the pre-BLA meeting on 12/5/06):
L211: • Category 1: comprised of randomized controlled studies conducted with FDA-agreed endpoints for treatment of cervical dystonia (Study 45 and Study 51)• Category 2: comprised of randomized controlled studies not conducted with FDA-agreed endpoints and open-label studies in cervical dystonia• Category 3: comprised of post-marketing reports (in countries where approved) from patients treated for cervical dystonia and other less reliable studies in cervical dystonia (no study reports, CRFs or reliable databases)• Category 4: comprised of studies/post-marketing information in other indications.
L212: dgale PMG. HolmgrenThe safety review was primarily based on the pooled Category 1 and 2 studies (365 subjects). In addition, safety data from other categories was reviewed as well. The sponsor states that since approval in UK in 1990 there has been over two million patient exposures to Dysport in over 72 countries. Although the safety assessment was mainly based on the safety database contained in this BLA, other sources that were relied on included the following: Review of Iatrogenic Botulism conducted by the Office of Surveillance and Epidemiology (OSE) and the Office of New Drugs (OND) of the Agency (7/3/08), and the approved labels for Botox, Myobloc, and the United Kingdom label for Dysport.
L213: Deaths: Dr. Stone notes that there were no deaths in Categories 1 and 2 studies, six deaths in other studies that have either confounding explanation or insufficient information, and 12 spontaneous post-marketing reports of deaths. Dr. Stone believes that five of these 12 post-marketing reports of death occurred in situations that could be considered complications of known side effects of Dysport (skin infection/aspiration pneumonia, pneumonia, dysphagia, botulism/cerebral aneurysm). Additionally, for two other post-marketing reports of death, Dr. Stone speculates that an unrecognized systemic autonomic effect could conceivably have played a role, but concedes that additional evidence of systemic autonomic effects would be necessary for such a conclusion. I agree with Dr. Yasuda that there is not enough evidence to attribute the death of these two cases to Dysport.
L214: Serious Adverse Events: In the Dysport group, there were 28 (13.3%) subjects who reported a serious adverse event (SAE) compared to 21 (11.5%) in the placebo group. Dr. Stone concludes that only one SAE (disabling neck pain the day after treatment) could be related to Dysport treatment with high plausibility. Among the 15 subjects who experienced non-fatal SAEs in other studies (category 3), only severe dysphagia appeared to be consistently associated with Dysport.
L215: Withdrawals due to Adverse Events: There were two subjects who withdrew as a result of adverse events: one in double-blind studies (n=210) due to headache, heavy eyelids, nervousness and irritability three days after injection, and the other in open-label studies (n=301) due to severe dysphagia six days after treatment that lasted 20 days.
L216: Common Adverse Events: Across the four placebo-controlled trials in Categories 1 and 2, adverse events were reported in 61% of the Dysport subjects compared to 50% of placebo subjects. The most common adverse events (MedDRA preferred terms) that were more frequent in the Dysport group than placebo group were: muscular weakness, dysphagia, dry mouth, dysphonia, injection blurred, injection site pain or discomfort and blood glucose increased. Among these adverse events that are more common in the Dysport than placebo group, Dr. Stone views dysphagia (17.4% in Dysport versus 3.8% in placebo) as potentially the most serious, possibly involved in the development of pneumonia and a death in clinical studies, and the most common serious event in post-marketing reports. Dr. Stone found a similar distribution of adverse events among subjects treated with Dysport in the double-blind phase of these studies and subjects in the open-label phase who were treated with placebo in the double-blind phase. Although limited by the small number of subjects in each dose group, a dose-related increase in the frequency of any adverse events was seen in Study 20 (which used multiple fixed doses): 30% in the placebo group, 37% in the 250 units group, 65% in the 500 units group and 83% in the 1000 units group. Individual AEs – muscular weakness, dysphonia, dysphagia, dry mouth and injection site, showed a dose-related increase in Study 20.
L217: Other safety findings of Dr. Stone include the following. There was no evidence of cumulative effects with repeated cycles of treatment. Blurred vision, although relatively uncommon complaint, was eight times more likely to occur in Dysport subjects compared to placebo. Further, loss of visual acuity appeared to be more common in Dysport subjects who also took anticholinergic drugs. Although, the overall incidence of adverse events was similar for naive and non-naïve subjects, the incidence of dysphagia and dyspnea was higher in naive subjects than in the non-naive subjects in both Dysport and placebo groups. Lastly, overall, Russian subjects were one-seventh as likely as their American counterparts to report at least one adverse event. Although Dr. Stone argues that Russian subjects' data should probably be excluded from statistical analyses of adverse events, he admits that such exclusion may not affect the conclusions as the number of Russian subjects is too small. The DSI inspection of the two clinical sites in Russia (Drs. Sofia Timerbaeva and Olga Orlova) concludes that no under-reporting of adverse events was noted.
L218: Laboratory tests: Clinical laboratory testing was limited to only Study 45, Study 51, and their respective open-label extensions. Dr. Stone notes several abnormal laboratory parameters. The following are his major concerns:
L219: • Blood glucose. For change from baseline, there was a treatment difference (Dysport – placebo) of 0.23 mmol/L that was statistically significant in the controlled trials [95% CI: 0.04; 0.42]. The normal reference range for glucose is: Fasting 70 - 110 mg/dL (conventional units) and 3.8 - 6.1 mmol/L (SI units); 2 hour postprandial &lt; 120 mg/dL (conventional) and &lt; 6.6 mmol/L (SI units). Analyses of outliers for blood glucose during the double-blind phase showed that there were 27.4% of subjects in the Dysport group with abnormal results compared to 15% in the placebo group. There were also more subjects in the Dysport group who had abnormal urine glucose compared placebo group. There were 23 subjects (11%) in Category 1 and 2 studies whose blood glucose values shifted from normal blood glucose values to high levels. Dr. Stone also shows that in all subgroups, there was an increase in blood glucose in Dysport subjects compared to placebo subjects. Dr. Yasuda argues that a mechanistic basis for the glucose findings is supported by literature showing in vitro inhibition of insulin secretion by botulinum neurotoxin A.
L220: A hand-drawn arrow pointing from the left margin towards the text about blood glucose findings.
L221: Given the significant potential for increased blood glucose associated with Dysport in the context of the lack of adequate exposures in diabetic subjects (Dr. Yasuda notes that only 1.4% subjects in Category 1 and 2 studies had non-insulin diabetes), I agree with Dr. Stone's recommendation for a phase 4 trial of Dysport in patient with diabetes.
L222: • Blood bicarbonate. For change from baseline, there was a treatment difference (Dysport – placebo) of 0.2 mmol/L that was not statistically significant (p=0.35) but achieves statistical significance (p&lt;0.0005) when values in subjects during exposure to Dysport (open-label) are compared with those obtained when they were not being treated (placebo during controlled phase). These findings, in Dr. Stone's view, suggest that there may be a cumulative effect of respiratory acidosis over time caused by respiratory depression. Consistent with the lack of a significant change from baseline during the double-blind phase, analyses of outliers during the double-blind phase showed that there were similar numbers of subjects with abnormal results in the Dysport group (3.9%) and placebo group (3.4%). The normal reference range for bicarbonate is: 22 - 28 mEq/dL (conventional units) and 22 - 28 mmol/L (SI units).
L223: Dr. Stone recommends a study of changes in ventilation due to Dysport treatment including serum bicarbonate and arterial blood gas measurement and pulmonary function tests, with inclusion of subjects with mild obstructive lung disease in the study. While this study may provide data to support whether or not suppression of ventilation is the likely cause of respiratory acidosis, it most likely may not provide additional safety information to what is already included in the "Dysphagia and Breathing Difficulties in treatment of cervical dystonia" of the Warnings and Precautions section of the label.
L224: Vital signs and ECG:
L225: Vital signs were measured at baseline and on subsequent visits. Dr. Stone notes that there was a small increase from baseline in the average heart rate in Dysport compared placebo. The treatment difference (Dysport – placebo) was 0.22 (p=0.35) during the controlled phase, and 0.85 (p&lt;0.0005) when values in subjects during exposure to Dysport (open-label) are compared with those obtained when they were not being treated (placebo during controlled phase). However, the number of subjects with abnormal results for heart rate was greater in the placebo group than Dysport group.
L226: Dr. Stone notes that usable ECG data was obtained in only one study – Study 731. These ECGs were recorded prior to retreatment, post retreatments (at 30 minutes and 4 weeks later) and at the end of the study completion. There was a mean treatment difference (post dose - baseline) for change in heart rate at 30 minutes post injection of 2.9 beats/minimum (p&lt;0.0005), and for QTcF interval of 3.7 (p=0.008). At 4 weeks post injection, however, neither the treatment differences for mean heart rate or QTcF interval were significant. Dr. Stone hypothesizes that the reduction of heart rate may be due to either a drug effect or due to vagal reflex secondary to injection. There were no subjects with corrected QT intervals of 500 msec or change in QTc of &gt; 30 msec from baseline. In the absence of placebo control arm or positive control to assess assay sensitivity, it is difficult to interpret the ECG data. Further, heart rate from ECG data cannot be compared to the heart rate from vital sign data as the timing of the vital sign measurement in relation to injection at any given visit was not specified.
L227: Dr. Stone recommends that a study of the effect of Dysport injection on vital signs in the immediate post-treatment period in patients with cervical dystonia and a thorough QT study be conducted as phase IV requirement. I agree with the study on vital signs particularly if it can address whether or not the statistically significant reduction in heart rate compared to baseline at 30 minutes post injection is possibly due vagal reflex secondary to the injection. If the relationship between the heart rate reduction and injection can be established, then a thorough QT study would not be necessary.
L228: Race subject-group analysis: Since 99.2% of subjects in Category 1 and 2 studies were Caucasian, Dr. Stone proposes a study of safety and effectiveness of Dysport including substantial numbers of patients with African, Asian and Hispanic ancestry. In the absence of a reason to expect that efficacy/safety may be different among ethnic groups, I believe that labeling would be sufficient rather than a study requirement.
L229: Immunogenicity: In his review, Dr. Stone finds that after treatment with Dysport, 3.6% of subjects who did not have neutralizing antibodies at baseline developed antibodies on retesting. Further, data from the double-blind placebo controlled phases of Category 1 and 2 studies showed no difference in the incidence of adverse effects indicative of hypersensitivity (pruritus, urticaria, asthma, wheezing or hypotension) following Dysport injections (1.9%) compared with placebo (2.2%). If subjects from open-label phase were also included, these reactions occurred in 4.2% of Dysport-treated subjects.
L230: Other important safety issues:
L231: Spread of Toxin Effect: A review (conducted by the OSE and OND of the Agency, 7/3/08) of postmarketing safety data from the approved botulinum toxins suggests that the effects of botulinum toxin can be observed beyond the site of injection. The reported symptoms include potentially life threatening swallowing and breathing difficulties; the onset of these symptoms appears to occur within hours to weeks following injection. There have been rare reports of death related to the spread of toxin effect. Adults and in particular children treated for lower limb spasticity appear to be the most vulnerable groups of patients. This safety risk is being addressed by strengthening the Warnings and Precaution section of the label, and the requirement of a Medication Guide and a Communication Plan. This is further discussed under Section 13 (Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategy, REMS) of this review.
L232: Lack of interchangeability of botulinum toxin products: If Dysport (botulinum toxin Type A) is approved, there will be three botulinum toxin products in the US market, two of them type A (Botox and Dysport), and one type B (Myobloc). There is a different dose to potency ratio between the various botulinum toxin products. In addition, each of these three botulinum toxin products will have different units of dosing for cervical dystonia. In this setting, medication errors including overdosing can occur due to the potential for healthcare providers to substitute one product for another and interchange dose units. In order to mitigate this risk, a Communication Plan (as part of REMS) to educate health care providers that the various botulinum toxin products are not interchangeable is being required. This is further discussed under Section 13 (Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategy, REMS) of this review.
L233: Established names for botulinum toxin products: As discussed above, if Dysport is approved, there will be two botulinum toxin products (Botox and Dysport) in the US market that share the same established name (botulinum toxin A). Since these two products have the same established name but have different and non interchangeable dosing units, it may lead to confusion and dosing errors among health care practitioners. Additionally, there is one other approved botulinum toxin product (Myobloc, botulinum toxin type B), and there remains the possibility of additional botulinum toxin products coming in to the US market in future. Since these factors adversely affect patient safety, the agency decided on 12/5/08 that all botulinum toxin products be given unique established names. For botulinum toxin A products, the established name will be a unique prefix+botulinumtoxinA; each unique prefix having one or two syllables with no particular meaning or bearing no reference to the company name, brand name or mechanism of action. The sponsor of this application has been contacted and asked to submit several established names from which the Agency will choose an established name.
L234: Milk protein contaminant: Dysport contains bovine-derived lactose as an inactive ingredient, and a small amount of contaminant milk protein. In this context, during the review process, concerns about the safety of Dysport in subjects with history of milk protein allergy were discussed. Dr. Ennan Guan estimates that every 500 units of Dysport contains approximately 15-45 µg of milk protein [via email of 9/19/08, Dr. Guan's calculation was based on the following:
L235: (b) (4)
L236: Dr.
L237: Stefano Luccioli, Senior Medical Advisor, Food Additive Safety, CFSAN, FDA, opinions
L238: (email 9/19/08) that since the lowest observed adverse-effect level to major food allergens in oral challenge studies have been reported in the high hundreds of microgram protein range, and given that these contaminant milk proteins will be administered IM at only a 10-fold lower range than protein exposures reported to cause problems by oral challenge, there is a concern about their allergenic potential. Therefore, the label should contain a statement that Dysport contains cow's milk protein, and that patients known to be allergic to cow's milk protein should not be treated with Dysport.
L239: Loss of potency over time and Flagellin contaminant: These issues have been discussed in Section 3 of this review memo.
L240: 9. Advisory Committee Meeting
L241: No advisory meeting was held.
L242: 10. Pediatrics
L243: Since Dysport is a biologic product and has been designated as an orphan drug for cervical dystonia (on 8/12/98), neither PREA nor BPCA provisions would apply. However, the Division is requiring pediatric studies under FDAAA (see section 13 of this review).
L244: 11. Other Relevant Regulatory Issues
L245: Four sites were selected for the Division of Scientific Investigations (DSI) audit: two in US – Drs Truong and Duane (both in Study 45); and two sites in Russia – Drs Olova and Timerbaeva (both in Study 51), based on large number of subjects that were enrolled at these sites. In addition, sponsor inspection was also requested because Dysport is a new molecular entity. The DSI inspection concludes that the data generated at all these four sites appear acceptable for use in support of this BLA. After the inspection of the sponsor, DSI concludes that Study 51 appears to have been conducted adequately, and the data submitted by the sponsor may be used in support of the respective indication. The Division of Manufacturing and Product Quality (Office of Compliance) inspected the sponsor's manufacturing facility, and recommends approval from a microbial control, sterility assurance and product quality perspective.
L246: 12. Labeling
L247: The main labeling recommendations are outlined:
L248: • Clinical recommendations:○ A claim of an effect on disability should not be granted○ Efficacy results of Study 51 at week 12, and Study 45 at week 8 and 12 should not be included in the Clinical Studies section.○ Several secondary endpoints should not be included in the Clinical Studies section.○ Open-label data regarding dosing should not be included in the Clinical Studies section.• Safety:○ Inclusion of class labeling language for the spread of toxin effect in the Warnings and Precautions section.○ More detailed discussion of potential breathing and swallowing difficulties in the Warnings and Precautions section○ Inclusion of discussion of changes in blood glucose and electrocardiographic findings seen with Dysport treatment in the Adverse Reactions section.• Office of Biotechnology products review of the Dysport Carton and Container Labels: had no major label recommendations.• Office of Surveillance and Epidemiology / Division of Medication Error Prevention and Analysis (DMEPA), after a review of the proprietary name, does not object to the use of the proprietary name, Dysport. Following a review of the Label and Labeling Risk Assessment, DMEPA has identified several risks that have been addressed, and provided several recommendations that have been incorporated into the label.
L249: 13. Recommendations/Risk Benefit Assessment
L250: • Recommended Regulatory Action
L251: Since the proposed REMS is deficient (see below), I recommend taking a Complete Response action. However, once these deficiencies are satisfactorily addressed, I recommend the approval of BLA 125274.
L252: • Risk Benefit Assessment
L253: The efficacy of Dysport in the treatment of cervical dystonia has been established in two placebo-controlled clinical trials, one of which was the subject of a special protocol assessment. Both these studies showed a statistically significant beneficial effect of Dysport 500 units over placebo in the change from baseline in the TWSTRS total score at 4 weeks (primary endpoint). Efficacy up to 8 weeks was demonstrated in Study 51.
L254: There are several potentially serious safety concerns which can be reasonably minimized or mitigated using the following strategies. The risk of the spread of botulinum toxin effects beyond the site of injection is being addressed by strengthening the Warnings and Precautions section of the label, the requirement of a Medication Guide and a Communication Plan targeting health care providers (the later two as part of the REMS). Adults and in particular children treated off-label for lower limb spasticity appear to be the most vulnerable groups of patients. Therefore, under FDAAA, phase IV trials in children and adults with lower limb spasticity are being required in order to identify effective doses, and further evaluate safety including the spread of toxin effects beyond the injection site in these patients.
L255: If Dysport (botulinum toxin Type A) is approved, there will be three botulinum toxin products in the US market – two of them (Botox and Dysport) with the same currently used established name i.e., botulinum toxin type A, and the other product Myobloc (botulinum toxin B). These products have different dose to potency ratio and different units of dosing for cervical dystonia, and therefore, are not interchangeable. In this context, medication errors including overdosing can occur due to the potential for healthcare providers to substitute one product for another and interchange dose units. These risks are mitigated by the inclusion of prominent language in the Dosage and Administration section of the label stating that Dysport units are not interchangeable, compared to or converted into units of any other botulinum toxin products, the requirement of a Communication Plan (as part of REMS) to educate health care providers that the various botulinum toxin products are not interchangeable, and by requiring all botulinum toxin products to have unique established names (unique prefix+botulinumtoxinA or B as the case may be).
L256: Concern regarding the loss of potency for Dysport of over (b) (4) during the one year dating period, potentially leading to dosing errors, has been addressed. Further, there will be a post-marketing requirement to establish tighter potency acceptance criteria for the qualification of new reference standards.
L257: A statistically significant potential for increased blood glucose associated with Dysport was seen in the Category 1 and 2 studies. However, the clinical significance of this observation in diabetic patients is unknown, as only 1.4% subjects enrolled in Category 1 and 2 studies had non-insulin diabetes. Therefore, there will be a requirement for a phase IV clinical trial of Dysport in patient with diabetes. There was also a statistically significant reduction in heart rate of
L258: approximately 3 beats per minute at 30 minutes post injection, and an increase in QT interval of 3.7 msec. In order to further evaluate whether the above findings are due to either a drug effect or due to vagal reflex secondary to injection, a phase IV clinical trial on vital signs will be required.
L259: Since the safety issues are either reasonably minimized or mitigated as discussed above, the benefits of Dysport outweigh the risks.
L260: • Recommendation for Postmarketing Risk Management Activities
L261: Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategy: A multidisciplinary (OSE and OND) review of postmarketing safety data from the approved botulinum toxins suggests that the effects of botulinum toxin can be observed beyond the site of injection. The reported symptoms include potentially life threatening swallowing and breathing difficulties; the onset of these symptoms appears to occur within hours to weeks following injection. Further, if Dysport (botulinum toxin Type A) is approved, there will be three botulinum toxin products in the US market, two of them botulinum type A (Botox and Dysport), and one type B (Myobloc). There is a different dose to potency ratio between the various botulinum toxin products. In addition, each of these three botulinum toxin products will have different units of dosing for cervical dystonia. In this setting, medication errors including overdosing can occur due to the potential for healthcare providers to substitute one product for another and interchange dose units. Therefore, the submission of a REMS is required to ensure that the benefits of the drug outweigh the risks under FDCA (section 505-1(a)(1)). The REMS include the following elements:
L262: 1. Medication Guide: The serious and significant public health concerns outlined above require the distribution of a Medication Guide to ensure that patients and their family/care givers are educated concerning the risks which could affect their decisions to use, or continue to use Dysport. This education is expected to occur on a recurring basis since it is given to the patient each time Dysport is administered.2. Communication Plan: A Communication Plan targeted to healthcare providers who are likely to prescribe and/or inject Dysport is required to disseminate information regarding the risks of potential spread of botulinum toxin effects after local injection and lack of interchangeability of Dysport units with those of other licensed botulinum toxin products. The Communication Plan includes the following components:• Dear Healthcare Provider Letter (DHCP): This letter is to be distributed at launch of Dysport to healthcare professionals who are likely to prescribe and or inject Dysport.• Dosing Guide: Dosing Guide for physicians that includes information on correct dose selection including lack of interchangeability of Dysport units with those of other licensed
L263: botulinum toxin products, reconstitution and volume of injection, and technique of injection.
L264: 3. Assessment of REMS: As part of the assessment of REMS, there will be a survey of patient's understanding of the serious risks of Dysport, and a survey of prescriber's understanding of the serious risks of Dysport and the lack of interchangeability of Dysport units with those of the others.
L265: Reviewer's comments: REMS, as currently submitted by the sponsor, has several deficiencies. The new established name (yet to be decided) should be reflected in all the documents related to REMS, and a statement that Dysport (established name) is a botulinum toxin product. The REMS and DCHP letter should mirror the bolded text in section 2 of the label regarding the non-interchangeability of the units of different botulinum toxin products. Both REMS and the DCHP letter do not emphasize the reasons why REMS is necessary in the first place. Since the target audience for DCHP is the healthcare provider, it should include the labeling language regarding Spread of Toxin Effect, Dysphagia and Breathing Difficulties in Treatment of Cervical Dystonia, and Pre-Existing Neuromuscular Disorders in the label. The 'Dosing Guide' should be omitted from the REMS since practitioners may rely on the 'Dosing Guide' exclusively and not refer to the more detailed package insert labeling. Finally, the physician survey should have additional questions designed to assess the healthcare providers understanding of the spread of toxin effects, and the patient survey should assess if they were provided with Medication Guide prior to injection with enough time to read it, and whether they had an understanding of what symptoms to watch out for after injection.
L266: • Recommendation for other Postmarketing Study Commitments
L267: Required Post marketing Studies:
L268: Clinical Trials:
L269: • Clinical trials in children and adults with lower limb spasticity in order to identify effective doses, and further evaluate safety including the spread of toxin effects beyond the injection site in these patients.• Clinical trial of Dysport in patients with diabetes.• Clinical trial of the effect of Dysport injection on vital signs in the immediate post-treatment period in patients with cervical dystonia.
L270: Product Postmarketing Requirement:
L271: • Establish tighter potency acceptance criteria for the qualification of new reference standards.
L272: Post marketing Commitments:
L273: • Several postmarketing product related commitments.
L274: Review and Evaluation of Clinical DataSafety Team Leader Memorandum
L275: BLA: 125274Drug: Clostridium Botulinum Toxin Type A Hemagglutinin ComplexRoute: Intramuscular InjectionIndication: Cervical DystoniaSponsor: Ipsen Biopharm LtdReview Date: 9/15/08Reviewer: Sally Usdin Yasuda, Safety Team LeaderNeurology Drug Products, HFD-120Sally Usdin Yasuda91.5708
L276: 1 Background
L277: Botulinum toxin A, injected intramuscularly into affected muscles, is used in the treatment of cervical dystonia. The mechanism of botulinum toxin is thought to be due to cleavage of proteins responsible for release of neurotransmitter from the nerve endings. The target protein for botulinum toxin type A is SNAP-25. (Botulinum toxin type B acts on a different protein). The result is inhibition of acetylcholine release from peripheral cholinergic nerve endings, including the neuromuscular junction. Selectivity is gained by injection into a specific muscle group. The Ipsen botulinum toxin Type A product (Dysport) that is under review in the present BLA has been registered in the United Kingdom since 1990 and is available for various indications in over 70 countries.
L278: This memorandum summarizes the primary concerns from the safety review of the Dysport BLA (125274) for cervical dystonia that was conducted by Dr. Stone. In addition to considering the primary safety data from this submission, Dr. Stone has considered the review of the “spread of toxin” issue for the Solstice (botulinum toxin B) and Allergan (botulinum toxin A) products that has been conducted by OSE (ISE RCM #2008-31) in some of the recommendations made for the Dysport product. I will summarize the main findings of his review and will focus especially on the findings that may be unexpected from a locally administered drug that is not expected to have systemic effect: laboratory findings and vitals signs/ECG analysis.
L279: The studies included in the safety review were designated by the Sponsor as Category 1 (pivotal safety information from prospective adequate studies in cervical dystonia - studies 051, 045 and long-term open label follow-up - studies 731 and 045b), Category 2 (controlled and open-label studies in cervical dystonia), Category 3 (supportive safety information in cervical dystonia studies and spontaneous postmarketing reports in cervical dystonia), and Category 4 (studies and post-marketing reports in other indications).
L280: 2 Summary of Findings from the Safety Review
L281: 2.1 Integrated Review of Safety
L282: Dr. Stone reports that for events other than injection site reactions, the reported adverse events followed a similar time pattern with median time of onset of 5-7 days after treatment and a median duration of 2-6 weeks. The onset is in agreement with the onset of symptoms with spread of toxin as reported in the OSE review (OSE RCM # 2008-31).
L283: 2.1.1 Deaths
L284: There were no deaths in the Category 1 and 2 studies. In category 3 studies in cervical dystonia there was 1 death due to a fall in a 73 y.o. patient with arterial occlusive disease and Parkinson's disease. Further information was not available to the Sponsor, although according to the ISS no reports of muscle weakness were recorded at that time. I agree with Dr. Stone that there is insufficient information to determine a role for Dysport.
L285: Five deaths were reported in Dysport-treated subjects in studies of post-stroke upper limb spasticity. Three of the 5 were due to events that occurred 2 months or more after treatment and thus not within a time frame in which the event was likely to be due to Dysport. Two events (fatal stroke 8 days after treatment and complications from a stroke that occurred 1 month after treatment) were within a relevant time frame, although Dr. Stone believes that both subjects were at high risk for stroke.
L286: There were 12 spontaneous post-marketing reports of deaths. According to Dr. Stone's review, 4 occurred too long after administration of Dysport (6 weeks or longer) to be reasonably associated with Dysport. An additional case was considered by a pathologist to be due to myocardial infarction thought to occur approximately 3 days after Dysport injection into the leg muscles in a 57 y.o. patient with severe coronary atherosclerosis. Dr. Stone reports that 5 deaths could be reasonably due to complications of known side effects of Dysport or due to administration technique. These included breathing difficulty prior to a diagnosis of pneumonia in a 1 y.o. with cerebral palsy; cerebral aneurysm/botulism in a 57 y.o. with lower limb spasticity in which Dr. Stone hypothesizes that the stress of mechanical ventilation and other treatment could have predisposed rupture of the aneurysm; skin infection that Dr. Stone hypothesizes could be due to injection technique in an 80 y.o. patient treated for lower limb spasticity (this eventual resulted in aspiration pneumonia although that did not appear to be related to the Dysport injection); dysphagia/bronchopneumonia in a 62 y.o. patient treated for torticollis; and worsening of dysphagia in a 78 y.o. patient treated for oromandibular dystonia that required insertion of a gastric tube, leading to severe hemorrhage, aspiration, and sepsis. I agree with his assessments. In 2 cases, an unrecognized systemic autonomic effect is postulated to have resulted in fatal events. One was a 36 y.o. patient with severe, poorly controlled insulin-dependent diabetes and diabetic autonomic neuropathy who died in her sleep 5 days after injection of Dysport into lumbar spine muscles. The other case was a patient with a history of cardiac conduction disturbance was treated with botulinum toxin (Dysport was not specified) for achalasia who died of sepsis subsequent to loss of consciousness with complete AV block that occurred 3 weeks after injection. This digoxin-treated patient had a digoxin serum concentration of 1.2 ng/ml before the administration of botulinum toxin and a level of 1.87 ng/ml at the time of the adverse events, according to the literature report that was the
L287: source of this case.1 I believe that a contribution of digoxin to this event cannot be ruled out. I do not believe there is enough evidence to attribute either of the suspected cases of autonomic disturbances to Dysport or botulinum toxin or to rule out the role of Dysport or botulinum toxin.
L288: 2.1.2 Serious Adverse Events
L289: In the double-blind phase, 28/210 (13.3%) Dysport treated subjects vs 21/182 (11.5%) of placebo-treated subjects reported at least 1 severe adverse event (defined as causing incapacitation or significant impairment of function). There were no events that were statistically significantly greater than placebo. Severe dysphagia occurred in 4 Dysport-treated subjects and in no placebo treated subjects. Seven serious AEs occurred in the Dysport-treated subjects compared to 1 in the placebo group. There was a case of traumatic injury within 1 month after treatment but no other details were known. Dr. Stone reports that the only event that could plausibly be related to treatment was an episode of severe/disabling neck pain the day after treatment.
L290: In the open label periods of Category 1 and 2 trials, there were 2 serious adverse events within 30 days of administration: 1 case of appendicitis the day after treatment and an inter-vertebral disc protrusion occurring 17 days after treatment. Fifteen subjects had serious adverse events in category 3 studies. These included dysphagia in 7 (and weight loss in 4 of those), as well as a single case of dyspnea and dysphonia each in those patients. Dr. Stone also identifies 8 subjects with serious events such as myocardial infarction and heart failure (in patients with pre-existing cardiovascular disease), retinal hemorrhage in a 72 y.o. patient that is a common finding in the elderly, hip fracture as a result of a fall in an elderly patient, and arm weakness due to generative changes in the shoulder. The post-marketing reports of serious adverse events include dysphagia, dyspnea, dysarthria, muscular weakness and fatigue, malaise, or flu-like syndrome.
L291: 2.1.3 Dropouts and Other Significant Adverse Events
L292: In Category 1 and 2 studies (cervical dystonia), only 1 subject withdrew due to an adverse event and in the open label studies only 1 subject withdrew due to an adverse event. These were headache, heavy eyelids, nervousness and irritability in 1 patient beginning 3 days after treatment and dysphagia in another beginning 6 days after treatment. An additional patient developed dysphagia, dry mouth, dysphonia, fatigue, and neck muscle weakness beginning 4 days after treatment but completed the double-blind phase of the study. No withdrawals were identified in category 3 studies.
L293: 2.1.4 Common Adverse Events
L294: Dr. Stone compared the frequency of events for Dysport vs placebo in the double-blind phases of the Category 1 and 2 trials according to the MedDRA hierarchy. The Preferred Terms for which Dysport was greater than placebo included dysphagia (17.6% vs 3.8%), muscular weakness (18.1% vs 4.3%), dry mouth (16.2% vs 6.5%), vision blurred (3.8% vs 0.5%) and dysphonia (7.6% vs 2.7%). These findings are in agreement with common adverse events results that showed muscular weakness, dysphagia, dry mouth, and dysphonia among the most common adverse events and significantly greater than placebo
L295: 1 Malnick SDH, Metchnik L, Somin M, Bergman N, Attali M. AJG 2000; 95:3334-5.
L296: in the double blind periods of the cervical dystonia studies. The distribution of adverse events among Dysport-treated patients was similar in the double-blind and open label phases (the latter including only the patients in the open label phases who received placebo in the double blind phase). Dr. Stone reports that there was a dose-related increase in the probability of reporting an adverse event, although there was little power to detect dose-related trends in specific adverse events.
L297: The overall incidence of adverse events in the double-blind phase was similar for naïve and non-naïve subjects in both the Dysport and placebo groups. Dysphagia and dyspnea had a numerically higher incidence in naïve subjects, although the relative incidence was similar in naïve and non-naïve subjects. The odds ratio for dysphonia or dysarthria, as well as for neck, jaw, limb, or muscle weakness was greater for naïve subjects than for non-naïve subjects.
L298: Dr. Stone addresses interactions with concomitant medications and development of adverse events with Dysport treatment. Some might be predictable based on similar drug target. An example is loss of visual acuity that appeared more commonly in Dysport-treated subjects who also took anticholinergic drugs. It may be helpful to include some information in the labeling regarding the potential for pharmacodynamic interaction with drugs that block cholinergic function. Currently the proposed labeling only discusses using caution with concomitant treatment with aminoglycosides or other agents interfering with neuromuscular transmission.
L299: 2.1.5 Laboratory findings
L300: Laboratory testing was performed in the pivotal clinical studies at baseline, 4 weeks, and 12 weeks after treatment and in pivotal study 045 also at 8 weeks. In the open label extensions samples were collected 4 weeks after retreatment and in the open-label study (study 731) samples were also collected on the day of retreatment.
L301: Dr. Stone has raised some concerns regarding laboratory values that have been unexpected with local administration of botulinum toxins. Dr. Stone suggests the possibility that certain patients may be predisposed to these changes and recommends future follow-up as Phase 4 commitments.
L302: Liver: Dr. Stone identifies small decreases in alkaline phosphatase and in LDH. Dr. Stone reports a 2% decrease in serum alkaline phosphatase levels particularly in the on drug/off drug comparison (it was not statistically significant for drug vs placebo). The alkaline phosphatase change may be important to evaluate when the drug is evaluated for use in children in consideration of potential effects on bone. He identifies more prominent increases in SGOT and SGPT that are primarily driven by results in a Hepatitis C-infected subject who had a 3.5-fold increase and an 8.4-fold increase in those values, respectively at the end of open label treatment. One other subject was identified with an approximate 7-fold and 14-fold increase in SGOT and SGPT, respectively from baseline/placebo to the end of open-label treatment. Dr. Stone states that there were no reported cases in clinical trials or post-marketing reports that met the definition of Hy's law. Dr. Stone does not include these hepatic changes in labeling, but recommends that
L303: further analysis, post-marketing, of the effects of Dysport on liver-related biochemistry tests should be requested. I agree with his recommendation.
L304: Bicarbonate: Dr. Stone identifies a modest increase in serum bicarbonate levels that he proposes could be a cumulative effect of respiratory acidosis caused by respiratory depression from Dysport. There were very few subjects in the Category 1 and 2 studies with a history of respiratory disease. Table BL1.3 of the ISS shows 1 subject (0.5%) in the double blind phase and 3 subjects (1%) in the open label phase with a history of COPD and 1 subject (0.3%) in the open label phase with a history of emphysema. Dr. Stone recommends a study of changes in ventilation due to Dysport treatment including serum bicarbonate and arterial blood gas measurement and pulmonary function tests, and possibly to enrich such a study with subjects with mild obstructive lung disease. If the outcome of further study would lead to a prediction of patients who are likely to develop respiratory compromise and the time course of this development, then I think the recommended study would be valuable. Since this may be a cumulative effect, this evaluation would require a long-term study. I do think that these clinical laboratory measurements should be systematically included in future clinical studies with Dysport in cervical dystonia or other indications.
L305: Glucose: An increase in blood glucose, most prominent in the double-blind comparison where it was statistically significant, is noted by Dr. Stone. The reasons for the greater effect observed in the double-blind comparison vs the on drug/off drug comparison is hypothesized by Dr. Stone to be the result of a general trend toward lower blood glucose values in the population over time, an adaptation to drug effect, or a statistical anomaly. The mean changes were small, the difference from mean baseline to mean post-treatment maximum in Category 1 and 2 studies (all phases) being approximately 0.94 mmol/L, according to table 72 in the ISS. Dr. Stone reports a mean &lt; 4% difference between the changes from baseline for drug vs placebo. However, 27% subjects on Dysport vs 15% for placebo had an abnormal result for blood glucose and 3.4% on Dysport vs 0% on placebo had an abnormal result for urine glucose. According to Table 73 in the ISS, in Category 1 and 2 studies, 11% of subjects had a shift in blood glucose from normal to high levels.
L306: Dr. Stone reports shifts in blood glucose levels in 4 subjects from approximately 99-107 mg/dl at baseline/placebo to up to approximately 130 –162 mg/dl on Dysport with one subject having blood glucose levels of up to 311 at end of open label treatment after approximately 2 years of treatment. In the case of the latter subject, Dr. Stone reports that from baseline of 107 mg/dl that subject had fluctuations in the range of 95-149 during treatment over approximately a 1.5 year period. One subject had baseline glucose of 148 mg/dl without glycosuria followed by blood glucose levels of 145 and 207 mg/dl on Dysport with trace and +glycosuria, respectively. According to the ISS Table BL1.3 there were only 2 patients assigned to Dysport (1%) who had a history of diabetes mellitus and 3 patients (1.4%) who had non-insulin dependent diabetes in the Category 1 and 2 studies, and therefore, there is little experience in diabetic patients in the controlled clinical studies in this Dysport program.
L307: I have not found any reports in the literature of hyperglycemia in humans following administration of botulinum toxin A. However, a mechanistic basis for the glucose findings is supported by literature showing in vitro inhibition of insulin secretion by botulinum neurotoxin A.2 Dr. Stone has included language about the blood glucose findings in his proposed labeling and recommends that the findings of elevations of blood glucose and glycosuria should be examined in more subjects for confirmation. Since he suggests that these changes could be important in patients whose diabetes is difficult to control, he recommends a study in patients with Diabetes. I agree with the recommendations to further characterize the blood glucose findings.
L308: Other laboratory values: Other shifts in laboratory values noted by Dr. Stone are an increase in uric acid levels from baseline to the last measurement in 2 subjects. Dr. Stone showed statistically significant decreases in hematocrit, neutrophils, and red blood cells in the on drug vs off drug comparison but not in the double-blind period, and not in the shifts from normal to abnormal.
L309: 2.1.6 Vital Signs and ECG
L310: Vital signs were not routinely measured throughout all of the studies in the development program, although weight, temperature, blood pressure, and heart rate were measured at baseline and on subsequent visits in three Category 1 and 2 studies. Mean changes did not appear to be affected by Dysport treatment and there were no marked outliers or dropouts for vital sign abnormalities.
L311: ECG testing was performed in open label study 731. Twenty-five subjects were enrolled, although not all subjects had ECG recordings performed according to the protocol. ECG recordings were to be performed prior to Dysport administration, 30 minutes after treatment, and 4 weeks after treatment and end of study. There was an average reduction in heart rate (and increase in RR interval) of 3 beats per minute from baseline to 30 minutes after injection. There was an increase in QTcF of 3.7 msec and an increase in QTcB of 0.8 msec. One subject had two ECGs with uncorrected QT intervals longer than 500 msec – at baseline that subject had QTc F of 472 msec (mean of 3 measurements, mean heart rate of 53 bpm) and 30 minutes after treatment mean QTcF was 482 msec. There were no subjects who had change in QTc of &gt; 30 msec from baseline. This study was not placebo controlled and did not have a moxifloxacin control.
L312: These QT data are very difficult to interpret due to lack of placebo and lack of moxifloxacin or other control for sensitivity. In addition, it is impossible to determine whether the time course is consistent with the injection vs the pharmacological actions of the drug product because there was no time point between 30 minutes and 4 weeks after treatment. Dr. Stone has recommended a thorough QT study. According to the Guidance for Industry: E14 Clinical Evaluation of QT/QTc Interval Prolongation and Proarrhythmic Potential for Non-Antiarrhythmic Drugs, the recommendations regarding thorough QT studies are generally applicable to new drugs having systemic
L313: 2 Huang X, Wheeler MB, Kang Y-h, Sheu L, Lukacs GL, Trimble WS, Gaisano HY. Truncated SNAP-25 (1-197), like botulinum neurotoxin A, can inhibit insulin secretion from HIT-T15 insulinoma cells. Mol Endocrinology 1998; 12:1060-70.
L314: bioavailability, but may not apply to products with highly localized distribution and those administered topically and not absorbed. Although Dysport is meant to have local activity, some systemic distribution occurs as expressed in the adverse effects observed even within the context of a clinical study (and more so with the spread of toxin that is under separate review with botulinum toxin products). However, the degree of systemic exposure has not been determined, variability in systemic exposure has not been characterized, and an exposure-response relationship for systemic effects has not been identified.
L315: With respect to adverse events that could be related to consequences of QT prolongation, there was 1 episode of syncope in the double blind period that did not appear to be related to study drug, and there was 1 episode (not considered serious) in the open label period that occurred 3 days after the dose. There was 1 death in post-marketing reports in which the patient died in her sleep. As Dr. Stone reported, this death was proposed to be due to diabetic autonomic neuropathy that may have facilitated a fatal event such as cardiac arrhythmia.
L316: I agree with Dr. Stone's recommendation for a study of the effect of Dysport injection on vital signs in the immediate post-treatment period in patients with cervical dystonia. Data evaluating the immediate time course of any observed changes and the time course of resolution would lead to an understanding of whether the observed changes are due to the injection itself or due to the drug product. With respect to the QT finding and further study, I believe that the available data are too weak to rule out or identify a signal for QT prolongation. I believe that this could be addressed initially by a study with a placebo control utilizing multiple ECG determinations and performing vital sign measurements at those time points. Dr. Stone has suggested 10, 20, 30, 45, 60, 90 and 120 minutes after injection and at 1, 3, 7, 14, and 21 days, and I believe that is reasonable. If carefully performed, this evaluation could be considered in the setting of an ongoing clinical efficacy study, rather than performing a traditional thorough QT study at this time. Addition of a moxifloxacin arm prior to initiation of study drug would help determine the sensitivity.
L317: 2.1.7 Other findings and considerations
L318: Immunogenicity: As Dr. Stone reports, and as reflected in the proposed labeling, 3.6% of subjects with negative neutralizing antibody tests at baseline developed antibodies after treatment with Dysport. The presence of neutralizing antibodies at baseline or their development during treatment had no apparent effect on the occurrence of adverse events.
L319: Dr. Stone reports that in the double-blind placebo controlled phases of Category 1 and 2 studies there was no difference in incidence of adverse events indicative of hypersensitivity (pruritus, urticaria, asthma, wheezing, or hypotension) following Dysport compared with placebo. The incidence of possible hypersensitivity events was higher in subjects with a prior history compared with those who had no prior history of hypersensitivity. There were no post-marketing reports described or coded as anaphylactic shock.
L320: Human Carcinogenicity: Dr. Stone reports that carcinogenicity studies were not conducted in animals. One case of prostate cancer was diagnosed during a clinical trial for cervical dystonia and a case of adenocarcinomas of the lung was discovered in a subject in a clinical trial for another indication.
L321: Human Reproduction and Pregnancy Data: There were 11 pregnancies in Category 4 subjects; 7 subjects were known to have received Dysport and 4 occurred in ongoing studies that were still blinded. Women with unfavorable outcomes (complicated pregnancies with normal babies or Spontaneous abortions) were older and had slightly higher average total exposure to Dysport. There were 24 spontaneous reports of pregnancies, 2 of which were in patients treated for cervical dystonia. One outcome was reported of a child born with deformity (blind and deaf). Spontaneous abortion tended to occur in older women.
L322: Demographics: For the 357 subjects treated with Dysport in Phase 2 and 3 studies for cervical dystonia (Category 1 and 2 studies), 99.2% were Caucasian. Dr. Stone has recommended a study of safety and effectiveness of Dysport that includes substantial numbers of patients with African, Asian, and Hispanic ancestry. I would suggest that the Sponsor attempt to include a diverse population in future clinical studies, or justify why that is not possible. The Dysport label should include demographic information in the clinical studies (the Sponsor's proposed label identifies the % of males and females but not race/ethnicity). In a recent evaluation in the database of Kaiser Permanente of Northern California, it was reported that the incidence of cervical dystonia (66 cases in 8.2 million person years of observation) was approximately 8x higher in white individuals than in individuals of other races. (Hispanic, Asian, and black members account for almost 45% of the membership of the health maintenance organization).3
L323: Dr. Stone has also commented on the difference between adverse events in the Russian centers vs the other study centers and expressed concern that this may suggest a failure of adverse event detection and reporting in the Russian centers. Since Dr. Stone's review, the Russian sites have been inspected and according to the DSI review of 9/4/08, no underreporting of adverse events was noted.
L324: 2.1.8 Measuring Exposure in the Plasma
L325: In the pre-BLA meeting of December 5, 2006, the Sponsor was asked to include in the BLA a justification of their rationale for the lack of contribution of pharmacokinetic studies and that Botulinum toxin Type A is not present in peripheral blood (after administration of Dysport). In the BLA submission, the Sponsor states (Section 2.7.2.1) that "based on Dysport's high potency, low therapeutic dose and localized mechanism of action, standard systemic PK studies cannot be conducted". The Sponsor states that "it is unlikely that BTX-A would be measurable in the peripheral blood following intramuscular (i.m.) injection at therapeutic doses in humans". We have evidence from a review of the "spread of toxin" issue and from this BLA that botulinum toxin products, including Dysport, can cause systemic effects that are distant from the site of injection.
L326: 3 Marra C, Van den Eeden SK, Fross RD, et al. Neurology 2007; 69:676-680.
L327: The mechanism for travel to distant sites is unknown, but understanding this may help to more fully understand the risk for serious systemic effects. I believe that it would be useful now to further characterize this spread and that developing a sensitive assay for detecting botulinum toxin in the blood may be one way to begin to address this.
L328: 2.1.9 Labeling and Post-Marketing Risk Management Plan
L329: Dr. Stone has recommended some changes to the Sponsor's proposed labeling and that is being reviewed by the Division. In addition, the Division is considering class labeling regarding the spread of toxin that would be part of the Dysport label.
L330: I have several other specific comments regarding labeling:
L331: • Currently the proposed labeling (Section 7) only discusses using caution with concomitant treatment with aminoglycosides or other agents interfering with neuromuscular transmission. I suggest adding “Use of anticholinergic drugs after administration of Dysport may potentiate systemic anticholinergic effects such as blurred vision”.• I suggest adding the following demographic information to Section 14, end of paragraph 1 of Clinical Studies: “Nine-nine percent of the patients were Caucasian”.• Section 12.2 Clinical Pharmacology/mechanism of Action suggests that Dysport acts selectively at the neuromuscular junction. It should reflect that Dysport inhibits acetylcholine release from peripheral cholinergic nerve endings, including the neuromuscular junction and that local injection is given with the aim of selectively blocking those nerve endings although the drug disposition may not be limited to the sites of injection.
L332: The spread of toxin issue that will be reviewed elsewhere requires class labeling and a MedGuide. Dr. Stone has drafted class labeling language and incorporated it into his proposed labeling. A MedGuide has been drafted by the Division and is currently under review prior to sending to the Sponsor. The Sponsor is developing a communication plan.
L333: 3 Conclusions
L334: Dr. Stone has not identified any issues in his review of the safety data that would prevent approval of Dysport.
L335: Dr. Stone has identified signals for changes in laboratory values for which I agree that further evaluation should be done and considered as post-marketing requirements. These include changes in blood glucose, as well as changes in serum bicarbonate levels, and liver-related tests. I think it may be possible to satisfy these recommendations with careful data collection in a clinical efficacy study if one is planned, rather than as a separate study designed to evaluate these changes. Vital signs and ECG data (QT interval) have not been adequately collected or evaluated and should be collected in the immediate post-administration period as well as at other specific time points (for example 10, 20, 30, 45, 60, 90 and 120 minutes after injection and at 1, 3, 7, 14, and 21 days after injection), and this could also be done in the context of a clinical efficacy study with a
L336: placebo control. A moxifloxacin arm could be added for consideration of sensitivity of QT evaluations. A separate study of blood glucose in diabetic patients may be useful. The Sponsor should discuss a plan for meeting these requirements with the Division prior to collecting these data.
L337: I would suggest that the Sponsor attempt to include a diverse population in future clinical studies, or justify why that is not possible.
L338: Dr. Stone has proposed labeling changes that are currently being reviewed by the Division. I have made additional suggestions in Section 2.1.8 of this review.
L339: Consideration should be given to asking the Sponsor to develop a sensitive assay to measure botulinum toxin in the blood. This would begin to characterize exposure due to systemic spread of toxin and the potential mechanism for the spread.
L340: The “spread of toxin issue”, reviewed elsewhere, requires class labeling and a MedGuide. These have been drafted by the Division and are currently under review prior to sending to the Sponsor. The Sponsor is developing a communication plan.
L341: Seal of the Center for Drug Evaluation and Research, Food and Drug Administration, U.S. Department of Health and Human Services. The seal features a caduceus (a staff with two snakes and wings) in the center, surrounded by the words 'CENTER FOR DRUG EVALUATION AND RESEARCH' and 'FOOD AND DRUG ADMINISTRATION'.

L352: 1. EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
L353: The sponsor submitted a Biologics License Application for a marketing approval of DYSPORT to treat patients with cervical dystonia. The sponsor submitted results of two pivotal studies to support the claim.
L354: 1.1. Conclusions and Recommendations
L355: In both of the pivotal studies, Dysport 500 U was statistically significantly efficacious in reducing TWSTRS Total Score at Week 4 in subjects with CD. The consistent treatment benefit of Dysport compared to placebo at Week 4 was maintained through Weeks 8 and 12 in Study 051. The efficacy of Dysport was also supported by the observed cases analyses of primary measure and pre-specified key secondary efficacy endpoints. The subgroup analyses by age, gender, region, and naïve vs. non-naïve to the treatment with botulinum toxin did not show any meaningful differences in efficacy of Dysport.
L356: 1.2. Brief Overview of Reviewed Clinical Studies
L357: 1.2.1. Phase III Studies
L358: The two studies #051 and #045 were randomized, double blind, single dose, placebo controlled studies. In each study, there was a washout period of at least 16 weeks to allow symptoms to return to baseline before administration of study medication. Study#045 was conducted in the US, and Study#051 was conducted in the US and Russia.
L359: Studies #051 and #045 involved a single dose of Dysport or placebo followed by a planned study period of 12 weeks. Study visits were planned at Weeks 4, 8, and 12.
L360: The primary efficacy variable, the change from baseline of the TWSTRS total score at Week 4 of the two studies, was analyzed with an ANCOVA model where the treatment group, baseline TWSTRS score, the strata whether or not the subject was previously treated by botulinum toxin and center were included in the model.
L361: 1.3. Statistical Issues and Findings
L362: No statistical issues were found in the reviewed studies.
L363: 2. INTRODUCTION
L364: 2.1. Overview
L365: The sponsor submitted efficacy findings of two pivotal, phase III studies to support the use of Dysport for treating patients with Cervical Dystonia (CD). The two studies #051 and #045 were randomized, double blind, single dose, placebo controlled studies. In each study, there was a washout period of at least 16 weeks to allow symptoms to return to baseline before administration of study medication. Study#045 was conducted in the US, and Study#051 was conducted in the US and Russia. Table 1 and Figures 1 &amp; 2 show an overview of the two studies.

  Flow chart of study 045 design. The study begins at Week 0 with Screening/Eligibility Criteria, Stratified Randomization, and Baseline Assessments. Study Medication Administration occurs at the start. At Week 2, a Telephone Contact Safety Assessment is performed. At Week 4, an Efficacy/Safety Assessment is conducted. Responders (TWSTRS-Total Score decrease &gt; 10%) proceed to Week 8. Non-responders (TWSTRS-Total Score decrease ≤ 10%) end the double-blind phase and receive open retreatment with DYSPORT (500 units). At Week 8 and Week 12, Efficacy/Safety Assessments are performed. Ongoing responses continue, while lost responses lead to retreatment indication and end of the double-blind phase. A follow-up until retreatment is required, leading to a final Efficacy/Safety Assessment and the end of the double-blind phase.

L373: 2Retreatment was to be delayed until week 8 whenever possible, or in the case of any study drug-related adverse event, until the event had fully resolved.
L374: Figure 2. Flow chart of study 045 design (Source: Study Report)
L375: Inclusion Criteria
L376: Eligible patients were male and female patients of \geq 18 years older. The subjects had cervical dystonia \geq 18 months since onset and previously untreated with botulinum toxin, or previously treated with botulinum type A or type B toxin with a minimum interval of 16 weeks since the last injection (and having returned to inter-treatment baseline status of clinical dystonia). Toronto Western Spasmodic Torticollis Rating Scale (TWSTRS) scores which met the following criteria at baseline: (i) TWSTRS total score \geq 30; (ii) TWSTRS severity subscale score \geq 15; and (iii) TWSTRS disability subscale score \geq 3.
L377: Study Durations
L378: Studies #051 and #045 involved a single dose of Dysport or placebo followed by a planned study period of 12 weeks. Study follow-up visits were at Weeks 4, 8, and 12 post-treatment or until re-
L379: treatment if done prior to Week 12. Subjects were informed that they had the right to discontinue the study at any time for any reason.
L380: Efficacy Measures
L381: The primary efficacy measure of the two pivotal studies was change from baseline to Week 4 in TWSTRS total score. TWSTRS is a standard rating scale for assessing the severity of CD. The scale has three subscale components: severity, disability and pain.
L382: In study #051, the secondary measures were ranked in order of priority and each was tested at the 5% level. Each endpoint was tested in the order as specified in the protocol. If a nonsignificant result was encountered, no claims of significance were made on any subsequent significant results. Ordering sequence of the secondary endpoints was: (1) Change from baseline in TWSTRS total score at Week 8; (2) Change from baseline in TWSTRS total score at Week 12; (3) Change from baseline in subject's VAS for symptom assessment of cervical dystonia at Week 4; (4) Change from baseline in investigator's VAS for symptom assessment of cervical dystonia at Week 4; (5) Change from baseline in subject's VAS for symptom assessment of cervical dystonia at Week 8; (6) Change from baseline in investigator's VAS for symptom assessment of cervical dystonia at Week 8; (7) Change from baseline in subject's VAS for symptom assessment of cervical dystonia at Week 12; (8) Change from baseline in investigator's VAS for symptom assessment of cervical dystonia at Week 12; (9) Change from baseline in SF-36 scores at Week 8; (10) Proportion of subjects considered by the investigator to be overall treatment successes at Week 12, defined by a global efficacy assessment of "better" or "much better" and a global safety assessment of no worse than "moderate."
L383: In study #045, secondary measures were (i) Change from baseline in TWSTRS total score at Week 4 (for the per protocol population); (ii) Subject VAS for pain at Week 4; and (iii) Subject assessment of change in the signs and symptoms of CD at Week 4. The secondary measures were not ranked in order of priority and not considered as key secondary endpoints. The efficacy findings of the endpoints were considered as supportive evidences of the efficacy of Dysport.
L384: A total of 60 patients (30 per treatment group) were randomized in study#045. At the end of randomization of 57 patients, the sponsor discovered that batch 159J had fallen below the minimum potency specifications. After a discussion with the FDA on this issue, it was decided that a further 80 patients should be recruited. Therefore, including the 57 patients treated with study medication on or before 16 October 1998, a total of 137 patients was planned for and recruited in this study. The randomized patients were sub-classified according to treatments involving batch 159J as follows:
L385: Group 1: all patients who received double-blind study medication after 16 October 1998 (i.e., all patients who received batches other than 159J);
L386: Group 2: all patients who received double-blind treatment regardless of the date of administration (i.e., all patients regardless of batch received);
L387: Group 3: all patients who received double-blind treatment on or before 16 October 1998 (i.e., all patients who received batch 159J).
L388: Analysis of Efficacy Data
L389: Primary Efficacy Analysis
L390: In study#051, the primary efficacy analysis was conducted on the intent-to-treat (ITT) population, and in study#045, the primary efficacy analysis was performed on the ITT population of Group 1. The ITT population comprised all patients randomized to receive study medication.
L391: The primary efficacy variable, the change from baseline of the TWSTRS total score at Week 4 of the two studies, were analyzed with an ANCOVA model where the treatment group, baseline TWSTRS score, the strata whether or not the subject was previously treated by botulinum toxin and center were included in the model.
L392: The pre-specified secondary endpoints # 1 to 9 of the study#051 were analyzed using the ANCOVA model as used for the primary efficacy analysis. The endpoint #10 was analyzed using a logistic model with treatment, strata (i.e., whether or not the subject was previously treated by botulinum toxin), and center as factors in the model. The ANCOVA analysis was also used to analyze the secondary efficacy endpoints of study#045.
L393: Missing data was imputed using the subject's baseline value. In the event of imputing missing values for TWSTRS and VAS for pain scores at Weeks 8 and 12, the last observation carried forward (LOCF) was used.
L394: Disposition of Subjects
L395: In study #051, a total of 116 subjects were randomized to treatment with Dysport (55 subjects) or placebo (61 subjects). Before Week 4 (primary endpoint), four patients were dropped out from the placebo and Dysport groups. The main reason for dropping out was insufficient clinical response (Table 2). Among the randomized subjects, 82% subjects in the Dysport group attended the final study visit at Week 12 compared to 62% subjects in the placebo group.

L399: In study #045, a total of 136 subjects (Group2: 66 DYSPORT, 70 placebo) were randomized in the double-blind phase. All subjects completed the double-blind phase, except for one patient in the placebo group, who withdrew from the study after baseline and before week 4 assessments. The reason for withdrawal of this subject was classified as lost to follow-up (Table 3). At the end of week 8, only 10 patients (=43-27-6) out of 43 patients in the placebo group, and 15 patients (=37-15-7) out of 37 patients in the Dysport group (within group 1) were available at week 12.

L406: 3. STATISTICAL EVALUATION
L407: Demographic and Other Baseline Characteristics
L408: Within each of the two studies, the Dysport and placebo groups were similar with respect to sex, race, ethnicity, height, and weight. In study #051, most subjects (75 of 116, 65%) were females; all subjects were Caucasians in race; 7 of 116 (6%) were Hispanic/Latino ethnicity; and the mean age (53 years) was typical for a population of subjects with CD. In study #045, most patients were Caucasians (90%) and females (63%). The mean age of the Patients was 53 years.
L409: In both studies, the DYSPORT and placebo groups were also similar with respect to CD history and previous botulinum toxin treatment.
L410: Efficacy Evaluation
L411: Analysis of Efficacy- Study#051
L412: Tables 4, 5 and Figure 3 summarized the results for change from baseline in TWSTRS total score at Week 4 (primary efficacy endpoint) and the secondary measures for the ITT population. The adjusted LSMEAN mean TWSTRS total score improved significantly more for the Dysport group than for the placebo group at Weeks 4 and 8, and to a lesser extent at Week 12. The adjusted LSMEAN of the change (improved) from baseline at week 4 in TWSTRS total score was -15.58 in the Dysport group compared to -6.74 in the Placebo group (p-value&lt;0.0001). The observed mean scores of TWSTRS total score were smaller for the Dysport group as compared to the means for the placebo group at Weeks 4 and 8, and Week 12. The p-values obtained from the observed cases analyses of TWSTRS total score at weeks 4, 8, and 12 were 0.0001, 0.017, and 0.314. Each of the key secondary endpoints also showed superiority of Dysport compared to placebo until the SF-36 mental health score for QoL (p-value=0.061). In Figure 3, the horizontal bars represented the 95% CI of the treatment difference in change from baseline. For each parameter, the horizontal line was completely to the right of the zero axis. Dysport demonstrated a consistent treatment benefit compared to placebo across all the parameters tested.

L424: Study #045
L425: The primary efficacy analysis was performed on the ITT population for Group 1 and listed in Table 6. The mean TWSTRS total score improved significantly more for the Dysport group than for the Placebo group at Weeks 4. The adjusted LSMEAN of the change (improved) from baseline at week 4 in TWSTRS total score by -9.6 in the Dysport group compared to -3.7 in the placebo group (p &lt; 0.0013).
L426: The change from baseline in TWSTRS-Total score at weeks 8 and 12 was a tertiary efficacy analysis. The mean TWSTRS-Total score improved 7.5 (adjusted mean) points and 5.2 (adjusted mean) points from baseline at weeks 8 and 12, respectively, in the DYSPORT group compared with an improvement of 1.7 (adjusted mean) points and 0.8 (adjusted mean) points from baseline at weeks 8 and 12, respectively, in the placebo group. An analysis of covariance showed the improvement from baseline in the TWSTRS-Total score at weeks 8 and 12 was statistically significantly better in the DYSPORT group compared with the placebo group (p-value = 0.007 and p-value = 0.030, respectively).
L427: The change from baseline in TWSTRS Sub-scale score for severity, disability and pain at weeks 4, 8, and 12 was also a tertiary efficacy analysis and the findings supported the efficacy of DYSPORT. The tertiary efficacy findings based on other secondary endpoints (i) Subject VAS for pain at Week 4; and (ii) Subject assessment of change in the signs and symptoms of CD at Week 4 also supported the efficacy of DYSPORT.

L433: 3.1.7.4. FDA Reviewer's Data Analyses and Comment
L434: This reviewer re-analyzed the efficacy data of the two pivotal studies according to the ANCOVA models as specified in the protocols. The findings were consistent with the sponsor's reported efficacy findings. Although missing data was imputed using the subject's baseline value, but it has no impact on the efficacy conclusions.
L435: In Study #045, the sponsor reported a tertiary efficacy analysis of the change from baseline in TWSTRS-Total score at weeks 8 and 12. According to the study design (Figure 2), the subjects who were responders at Week 4 (TWSTRS-Total Score Decrease &gt;10%) were followed up to Week 12. Majority of the subjects (69% from Placebo group, and 47% from Dysport group) exited from the study at the end of Week 4.
L436: According to the Figure 2 (i.e., Study design of Study#045), only the responders (TWSTRS-Total Score Decrease &gt;10%) at the end of week 4 were followed up to week 12. Only 10 (23%) patients (=43-27-6) out of 43 patients in the placebo group, and 15 (40%) patients (=37-15-7) out of 37 patients in the Dysport group (within group 1) were available at the end of week 12. Since the missing data of the exited patients were due to the study design, the data do not follow any particular type of missing data mechanisms. Statistical inferences based on the LOCF method or any likelihood-based imputation method for imputing missing data of the exited patients might not be interpretable. Therefore, the findings of the LOCF analyses at Week 8 and Week 12 (although the Dysport group was significantly efficacious than the placebo group) might not be considered as supportive evidences of the efficacy conclusion of Dysport at weeks 8 and 12.
L437: 4. SUBGROUP ANALYSES
L438: 4.1. Subgroup Analyses
L439: Subgroup analyses on the primary efficacy measure were conducted for individual studies to evaluate the uniformity of treatment effect across patients' subgroups (age, gender and region). All subjects in Study#051 were Caucasians. About 90% subjects in Study#045 were Caucasians.
L440: Table 7 lists the subgroup analyses results of the two studies. The improvement in TWSTRS total score was greater for Dysport than for Placebo in both age groups (&lt;65 years, and \geq 65 years). This treatment difference was consistent between the two studies. There were no differences in efficacy of Dysport between male and female subjects in either of the studies. Mean improvements in TWSTRS total score were better with Dysport than with Placebo for both male and female subjects, indicating presence of the efficacy in both men and women.
L441: The effect of region was assessed for Study #051 as the study contained 16 centers in the United States (US) and four nonUS centers (Russia). Efficacy of Dysport compared to Placebo was similar for US and non-US centers in Study #051 (Table 7). Study #045 was conducted in the US alone.

L449: 5. SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS
L450: 5.1. Collective Evidence of Efficacy
L451: In both studies (#051 and #045), Dysport 500 U demonstrated its statistically significant efficacy than Placebo after a single injection based on the primary analysis of reduction in TWSTRS Total Score at Week 4 in subjects with CD. The efficacy findings of Study #051 confirmed that the consistent treatment benefit of Dysport compared to placebo at Week 4. Both the primary LOCF and observed cases analyses also confirmed that the consistent treatment benefit of Dysport compared to placebo was maintained through Weeks 8 and 12. The efficacy of Dysport was also supported by the key secondary efficacy assessments of TWSTRS in Study #051. The subgroup analyses by age, gender, region, and naïve vs. non-naïve to the treatment with botulinum toxin did not show any meaningful differences in efficacy of Dysport.
