L467: 1 EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
L468: 1.1 Recommendation on Regulatory Action
L469: There are no safety issues that prevent approval of this drug for the stated indication.
L470: 1.2 Recommendation on Post-marketing Actions
L471: 1.2.1 Risk Management Activity
L472: No risk management activity is recommended specifically for Dysport beyond standard labeling and post-marketing surveillance for adverse events. Any risk management activity required for the class of botulinum toxin products should be applicable to Dysport.
L473: 1.2.2 Required Phase 4 Commitments
L474: There should be a commitment to five Phase 4 studies:
L475: 1. A study of Dysport in patients with diabetes2. A study of changes in ventilation due to Dysport treatment including serum bicarbonate and arterial blood gas measurement and pulmonary function tests. If possible, the study should be enriched with subjects with mild obstructive lung disease.3. The effect of Dysport injection on vital signs in the immediate post-treatment period in patients with cervical dystonia4. A thorough QT study5. A study of safety and effectiveness of Dysport including substantial numbers of patients with African, Asian and Hispanic ancestry
L476: 1.2.3 Other Phase 4 Requests
L477: Further analysis of the effects of Dysport on liver-related biochemistry tests should be requested.
L478: 1.3 Summary of Clinical Findings
L479: 1.3.1 Brief Overview of Clinical Program
L480: 1.3.2 Efficacy
L481: Efficacy is not considered in this review. Review of efficacy is being conducted by the primary medical reviewer, Dr. Carole Davis. This review is concerned with safety and is conducted under the assumption that the drug is efficacious.
L482: 1.3.3 Safety
L483: There were 365 subjects treated with Dysport in the principal clinical studies. There has also been extensive post-marketing experience with the drug outside the United States for a variety of indications. In the treatment of cervical dystonia, the drug can cause excessive muscular weakness in the area of treatment, dysphagia, dysphonia or dysarthria, dry mouth, blurring or other loss of visual acuity, dyspnea, injection site discomfort and elevation of blood glucose. Adverse events other than injection site discomfort commonly last for several weeks. Dysphagia appears to be the most serious adverse effect; it can lead to complications such as aspiration and the need for a feeding tube. Dyspnea likely results from weakening of accessory muscles of ventilation and may pose a risk for patients with lung disease.
L484: Issues requiring further investigation include the effect of Dysport on patients with diabetes, the cumulative effect of the drug on ventilation, changes in heart rate and electrocardiogram seen during treatment and the results of treatment in ethnic minorities.
L485: 1.3.4 Dosing Regimen and Administration
L486: The sponsor proposes an initial dose of 500 U of Dysport given intramuscularly in doses divided among affected muscles. Retreatment may occur after a minimum of 12 weeks with doses between 250 U and 1000 U. Titration in steps of 250 U is recommended.
L487: 1.3.5 Drug-Drug Interactions
L488: Formal drug interaction studies have not been conducted.
L489: 1.3.6 Special Populations
L490: There are no adequate and well-controlled studies in pregnant women. Available data on Dysport and pregnancy are discussed in Section 7.1.14. Dysport has not been studied in the pediatric population.
L491: 2 INTRODUCTION AND BACKGROUND
L492: This application is for intramuscular use of Dysport (botulinum toxin type A) in cervical dystonia. Two other botulinum toxin products, Botox (botulinum toxin type A) and Myobloc (botulinum toxin type B), are currently licensed for this indication. These three products have distinct methods of assay. Their units of potency are not equivalent and they cannot be used interchangeably. Group labeling is currently being considered for the issue of spread of toxin to areas distant from the site of injection.
L493: 3 SIGNIFICANT FINDINGS FROM OTHER REVIEW DISCIPLINES
L494: Please refer to the discipline-specific reviews.
L495: 4 DATA SOURCES, REVIEW STRATEGY, AND DATA INTEGRITY
L496: The information in the Integrated Summary of Safety is principally based on findings from the main efficacy studies (Studies 045 and 051), the long-term open label follow-up studies (Studies 045b and 731, respectively), and two Phase 2 randomized studies (Studies 020 and 042). These are described in greater detail in Sections 7.1, 7.2.1 and 7.2.1.1.
L497: 5 CLINICAL PHARMACOLOGY
L498: According to the clinical pharmacology review of Dr. Vaneeta Tandon, botulinum toxin type A at recommended dosages is not expected to be present in the peripheral blood at measurable levels. The application does not include pharmacokinetic or other clinical pharmacology data as there are no valid methods for measuring this product at levels in the blood that are not highly toxic.
L499: 6 INTEGRATED REVIEW OF EFFICACY
L500: Dr. Carole Davis is conducting the review of efficacy.
L501: 7 INTEGRATED REVIEW OF SAFETY
L502: 7.1 Methods and Findings
L503: The information in the Integrated Summary of Safety is principally based on findings from the main efficacy studies (Studies 045 and 051), the long-term open label follow-up studies (Studies 045b and 731, respectively), and two Phase 2 randomized studies (Studies 020 and 042). The sponsor refers to these studies as Category 1 and 2; these are pooled together in most analyses. These studies all collected information on spontaneously reported adverse events. Four of the studies (045, 045b, 020 and 042) also solicited adverse events through the use of a checklist. Vital signs were collected in all studies except 020. Neurological and physical examinations and
L504: Clinical Review
L505: Marc Stone, M.D.
L506: BLA 125274
L507: Dysport® (Clostridium botulinum toxin type A hemagglutinin complex)
L508: laboratory tests including botulinum toxin antibodies were performed in all studies except 020 and 042. Electrocardiograms were obtained in study 731 (and at baseline only in 045).
L509: Information was reported on serious adverse events and adverse events leading to withdrawal from another prospective study (Study 024). There were also three post-marketing surveillance studies that collected serious adverse event information by subject and one additional study that had incomplete information on serious adverse events, all involving subjects with cervical dystonia. The sponsor refers to these five cervical dystonia studies as Category 3. Complete serious adverse event data were also reviewed for 32 studies for other indications and incomplete information was obtained in an additional four studies. The sponsor refers to this final group as Category 4.
L510: 7.1.1 Deaths
L511: There were no deaths in the Category 1 and 2 studies.
L512: In study 022, a post-marketing surveillance study (Category 3), a 73 year-old woman with a history of arterial occlusive disease and Parkinsonism was administered 500 U Dysport into the left and right trapezius, splenius capitis and sternocleidomastoid muscles on 12 January 1994 and 03 February 1994. On the data collection form detailing the (b) (6) treatment, no text was entered into the 'occurrence of side effects' field. However, a note of 'patient died as the result of a fall' was entered in the 'further treatment?' field. No date or causality was reported by the investigator. The event was identified during a review of the data collection forms as part of a reconciliation process of old trials. No further details were available to the sponsor. The lack of information as to when the fall occurred relative to the last treatment makes it impossible to judge the role Dysport may have played in her death. This was the only death reported in a Category 3 study.
L513: The sponsor reported five deaths of Dysport-treated subjects in studies of post-stroke upper limb spasticity: 1) a 78 year old man with known lymphoma died of this cancer (b) (6) months after Dysport treatment; 2) a 66 year-old man suffered a fatal stroke (b) (6) days after treatment; 3) a 27 year-old man developed fatal pneumonia (b) (6) months after treatment; 4) a 66 year-old man suffered a fatal intracerebral hemorrhage (b) (6) months after treatment; 5) a 49 year-old woman died of complications from a stroke that occurred (b) (6) after treatment. Of these cases, only 2) and, possibly, 5) occurred within a time frame where drug effect could have potentially played a role but both subjects would have been considered to have been at high risk for stroke regardless of treatment.
L514: The sponsor reported one other subject who was murdered (b) (6) weeks after treatment for glabellar lines.
L515: The sponsor provided twelve spontaneous reports of deaths in Dysport patients (Table 7.1.1-1):

L520: Of the twelve cases reported, four (numbers 3, 6, 7 and 12 in the table) occurred too long after treatment for Dysport to be plausibly associated with the death. In case 1, there was some indication of breathing difficulty the day before the diagnosis of pneumonia; Dysport could plausibly have caused unrecognized dysphagia and aspiration or diminished respiration and atelectasis leading to pneumonia. In case 2, there were clear signs of systemic botulism; the stress of mechanical ventilation and other treatment could have predisposed rupture of the aneurysm. In case 4, the skin infection was likely due to injection technique rather than the drug itself; aspiration pneumonia developed much later as a consequence of the patient's debilitated condition that resulted from the skin infection. In case 5, it would be necessary to postulate a systemic effect on autonomic tone for botulinum toxin (not necessarily Dysport, the brand was not specified in the report) treatment of the esophagus to have contributed to heart block; the age
L521: Clinical Review
L522: Marc Stone, M.D.
L523: BLA 125274
L524: Dysport® (Clostridium botulinum toxin type A hemagglutinin complex)
L525: and prior condition of the patient, including the use of digoxin, are, however, sufficient to explain his terminal illness. In case 8, the patient had a history of severe COPD and developed dysphagia seven days after Dysport treatment. The dysphagia was a likely complication of treatment which could have facilitated aspiration and consequent fatal pneumonia. In case 9, the patient had pre-existing dysphagia that worsened after Dysport treatment, necessitating insertion of a gastric tube, leading to severe hemorrhage, aspiration and sepsis. In case 10, the patient died in her sleep. It was speculated that diabetic autonomic neuropathy may have facilitated a fatal event such as cardiac arrhythmia or apnea and it could be speculated further that a systemic autonomic effect of Dysport could have worsened the neuropathy. Case 11 is a straightforward autopsy-confirmed case of myocardial infarction in a patient with severe coronary atherosclerosis with no suggestion of any role for Dysport.
L526: To summarize, five deaths (Cases 1, 2, 4, 8 and 9) occurred in situations that could be considered complications of known side effects of Dysport, five other deaths had no reason to suspect Dysport treatment as a contributing factor and two deaths occurred for which an unrecognized systemic autonomic effect could conceivably have played a role; the credibility of such a role would depend on additional evidence of systemic autonomic effects.
L527: 7.1.2 Other Serious Adverse events
L528: In the double-blind phase, 28/210 (13.3%) of Dysport treated subjects reported at least one severe adverse event compared to 21/182 (11.5%) of placebo subjects (Odds Ratio 1.24, 95% CI 0.63 – 2.47, p=0.63). As seen in Table 7.1.2-1, these events were scattered over a variety of categories and give little indication of an incidence notably greater than placebo; the event that appeared closest to statistical significance was severe dysphagia which occurred in four Dysport treated subjects and no placebo subjects (Odds Ratio 4.75, 95% CI 0.57 – Infinity, p=0.16 by the exact method).

L531: The sponsor distinguished Serious adverse events; those that resulted in death, persistent or significant incapacity, birth defect, hospitalization, immediate risk of death, or medical or surgical intervention; from Severe adverse events, those causing incapacitation or significant impairment of function. They identified seven serious adverse events occurring among Dysport-treated subjects in the double-blind phase and only one serious adverse event in the placebo group. This difference is somewhat remarkable (Odds Ratio 6.24, 95% CI 0.79 – 283, p=0.07) although not formally statistically significant. Three of the serious events occurring in Dysport-treated subjects were unlikely to be drug-related as they occurred more than (b) (6) days after treatment: two falls that resulted in injury that required hospitalization or surgery and an episode of hypoglycemia. There was another case of traumatic injury that occurred within (b) (6) of treatment but no other details are known. One subject suffered a marked increase in hepatic enzymes but was discovered to be Hepatitis C antibody positive. Another subject experienced bleeding from a uterine leiomyoma (b) (6) after injection and had a subsequent hysterectomy. The only event that could be related to treatment with high plausibility was episode of severe and disabling neck pain that occurred (b) (6) after treatment.
L532: Seventeen subjects had reports of serious adverse events during open-label periods of the Category 1 and 2 trials. Most of these were surgical procedures including three knee arthroplasties and two breast implant procedures. Aside from elective surgical procedures, there were only two serious adverse events that occurred within (b) (6) days after treatment: one case of
L533: Clinical Review
L534: Marc Stone, M.D.
L535: BLA 125274
L536: Dysport® (Clostridium botulinum toxin type A hemagglutinin complex)
L537: appendicitis occurring (b) (6) after treatment and an intervertebral disc protrusion (location not described) occurring (b) (6) days after treatment.
L538: There was a suggestion of increasing incidence of serious adverse events with treatment dose (Table 67 of the ISS) but the trend was not statistically significant (p &gt; 0.2).
L539: In addition to the death described in section 7.1.1, there were fifteen other subjects with serious adverse events in Category 3 studies. Seven of these suffered dysphagia and four had significant weight loss as well. Dyspnea was also reported in one of the seven subjects and dysphonia in another but there was no information that would indicate whether these symptoms could be local effects or the result of systemic spread of toxin. Another eight subjects experienced serious events such as myocardial infarction, heart failure, retinal hemorrhage, hip fracture, and arm weakness occurring within (b) (6) days of treatment. The cardiac events appear to be the result of pre-existing cardiovascular disease and the hip fracture was the result of a fall in an elderly woman. The retinal hemorrhage occurred in a 72 year-old woman, a relatively common event in that age group. The arm weakness was described as due to degenerative changes in the shoulder; it was not disclosed whether the shoulder was the same side as Dysport treatment to the neck.
L540: In summary, the only serious adverse event that either appeared consistently or was strongly plausible as a result of Dysport treatment was severe dysphagia.
L541: 7.1.3 Dropouts and Other Significant Adverse events
L542: 7.1.3.1 Overall profile of dropouts
L543: Of the 210 subjects assigned to treatment with Dysport in the double-blind phases of Category 1 and 2 studies, eleven withdrew after receiving treatment. Seven of the eleven withdrew in order to enter open-label treatment. Of the other four subjects, one refused blood draws, one had schedule conflicts and one was lost to follow-up. Only one subject was identified as withdrawing due to an adverse event.
L544: Among the 301 subjects treated under open-label protocols, there were thirty-one withdrawals. Thirteen were described as withdrawal of consent without other information, six were withdrawn for protocol violations, four withdrew due to insufficient clinical response and four were lost to follow-up. Two other subjects left the open label study because of remission of symptoms, one was terminated by the sponsor and only one subject was identified as withdrawing due to an adverse event.
L545: No withdrawals could be identified in the Category 3 studies.
L546: 7.1.3.2 Adverse events associated with dropouts
L547: There were only two adverse events associated with dropouts. In Study 020, a 52 year-old woman reported a number of complaints which started three days after injection, including
L548: Clinical Review
  Marc Stone, M.D.
  BLA 125274
L549: Dysport® (Clostridium botulinum toxin type A hemagglutinin complex)
L550: headache, heavy eyelids as well as nervousness and irritability. All the events were recorded as lasting for approximately four weeks. In open-label Study 731, a 53 year-old woman experienced severe dysphagia six days after treatment, resolving 20 days later.
L551: 7.1.3.3 Other significant adverse events
L552: In study 045, a 66 year-old man developed dysphagia, dry mouth, dysphonia, fatigue, and neck muscle weakness four days after treatment. These resolved after approximately two months. The patient completed the double-blind phase but did not enter the open label phase due to these adverse events.
L553: 7.1.4 Other Search Strategies
L554: The adverse event data file provided by the sponsor was analyzed using the MAED Service program which compared the frequency of events Dysport to placebo in the double-blind phases of the Category 1 and 2 trials according to the MedDRA hierarchy. Tables 7.1.4-1 through 7.1.4-6 show the classifications with the greatest excess of events for Dysport subjects compared to placebo subjects (according to p-value) respectively for Preferred Terms, High Level Terms, High Level Group Terms, System/Organ/Class, Standardized MedDRA Queries (Broad Scope) and Standardized MedDRA Queries (Narrow Scope). The cutoff point on each of these tables is the first instance where the rate of occurrence of the event is higher in the placebo group than the Dysport group on the premise that this is likely a chance occurrence; it is assumed there is no reason for the rate of occurrence to be lower with Dysport.

L563: These analyses are not stratified by trial, which is necessary because of differences among trials in the proportions of subjects allocated to Dysport and placebo and because adverse events were actively solicited in some trials but not in others.
L564: 7.1.5 Common Adverse events
L565: 7.1.5.1 Eliciting adverse events data in the development program
L566: All Category 1 and 2 studies collected information on spontaneously reported adverse events. Four of the studies (045, 045b, 020 and 042) also solicited adverse events through the use of a checklist. The checklists consisted of MedDRA Preferred Terms used in MedDRA queries for Dysphagia, Neck Muscle Weakness, Jaw Weakness, Voice Changes, Dry Mouth, Hypersensitivity, Injection Site Reactions, Tiredness, Breathing Difficulties, Visual Difficulties, Cardiac Events and Hypotension, Limb Weakness and Muscular Weakness.
L567: 7.1.5.2 Appropriateness of adverse event categorization and preferred terms
L568: In the adverse event dataset for the Category 1 and 2 studies, there were 2993 adverse events reported using 1535 verbatim terms classified into 758 lower level terms, 507 preferred terms, 289 higher level terms. I reviewed the classification of verbatim terms into preferred terms and found very few discrepancies, none of any consequence.
L569: 7.1.5.3 Incidence of common adverse events
L570: In the double-blind phases of all Category 1 and 2 studies, adverse events were reported in 61% of Dysport subjects and 50% of placebo subjects. As shown in Table 7.1.5.3-1, the incidence was higher with drug than with placebo in all four trials. Stratified by trial and region using random effects logistic regression, the odds ratio for a subject having any adverse event was 2.23 for Dysport compared to placebo.

L588: 7.1.5.5 Identifying common and drug-related adverse events
L589: In the one randomized multiple fixed-dose study, Study 020, there was a dose-related increase in the probability of reporting an adverse event: 37% of subjects treated with the 250 U dose, 65% treated with 500 U and 83% treated with 1000 U reported at least one adverse event (p=0.006 for trend). Table 7.1.5.5-1 lists the preferred terms for adverse events reported by more than two subjects in Study 020:

L592: *Trend for dose-dependent increase in incidence of Preferred Term among doses of active drug
L593: In this study there were only 17-20 subjects in each treatment arm, hence very little statistical power to detect dose-related trends in specific adverse events. Nonetheless, muscular weakness and dysphonia both showed a statistically significant dose-dependent difference in incidence. Three other adverse events that among the statistically strongest associations with Dysport treatment in the analysis of all four randomized trials; dysphagia, dry mouth and injection site
L594: discomfort; showed patterns of generally increasing incidence with dose that were not statistically significant in this one small trial. In addition to considering the statistical association, the proximity of these events to the site of application and their consistency with the drug's mechanism of action makes it likely that all five of these adverse events are drug-related. Musculoskeletal pain was not reported by more than two subjects in Study 020 but the increased occurrence of this preferred term in all studies among Dysport subjects relative to placebo subjects along with the plausibility of the injected drug causing pain in the treated muscle would also lead to the conclusion that this could be a drug-related effect. Blurred vision was a relatively uncommon complaint reported in Dysport subjects (3.8%) but it was eight times more likely to occur in Dysport subjects than placebo subjects. Blurred vision was only reported as part of the solicitation of adverse events by investigators, never spontaneously, suggesting that the effects were subtle. This could plausibly be the result of difficulty in visual accommodation resulting from pupillary meiosis, a manifestation of Horner's syndrome caused by the effect of Dysport on the superior sympathetic ganglion. The number of events reported within the System/Organ/Class of Eye Disorders showed evidence of dose dependency; the events included visual blurring and loss of acuity along with an eyelid disorder, possibly ptosis, which could be another manifestation of Horner's syndrome.
L595: 7.1.5.6 Additional analyses and explorations
L596: The solicitation of adverse events through use of a checklist led to higher rates reporting. This increase was seen in both Dysport and placebo treated subjects. However, the overall profile of adverse events remained largely unchanged, regardless of the collection method employed.
L597: Table 7.1.5.6-1 shows the most common adverse events by treatment cycle. The proportion of subjects reporting adverse events remained nearly constant across cycles despite the likely possibility that subjects who were more susceptible to bothersome adverse effects of the drug would not continue treatment into subsequent cycles. Events that appeared to diminish in frequency in later cycles included muscular weakness and musculoskeletal pain, dry mouth, fatigue and eye disorders while falls and skin disorders appeared more frequently. Overall there was no evidence of cumulative effects with repeated cycles of treatment.

L600: In the double-blind phase, the overall incidence of adverse events was similar for naive and non-naive subjects in both the Dysport and placebo groups. Dysphagia and dyspnea had a numerically higher incidence in naive subjects in both the placebo and Dysport groups, compared to their non-naive counterparts. The relative incidence of these adverse events (compared to the placebo group) was, however, similar in both naive and non-naive subjects. Most of the increase in dysphonia or dysarthria associated with Dysport treatment was among naive subjects. The odds ratio for naive subjects was 10.91 (95% CI 1.70 – Infinity, p=0.0075)
L601: while for non-naïve subjects it was 1.36 (95% CI 0.27 – 7.30, p=0.92). There was a similar pattern among subjects reporting any sort of neck, jaw, limb, or muscle weakness. The odds ratio for naïve subjects was 24.5 (95% CI 3.51 – 1084, p&lt;0.00005) while for non-naïve subjects it was 3.11 (95% CI 1.11 – 9.69, p=0.02).
L602: Subjects age 65 or older were more likely to report any sort of new, jaw, limb or muscle weakness but this was not affected by treatment assignment. Women were also more likely to report any sort of new, jaw, limb or muscle weakness but, again, this was not affected by choice of treatment. There were no significant differences in adverse events due to subject weight or body mass index. The only indication of an interaction between age, sex and treatment was a lower risk of dry mouth from Dysport-treatment in men over 65 (odds ratio for treatment 0.41, 95% CI 0.01-4.87, p=0.80) compared to all other subjects (odds ratio 3.59, 95% CI 1.51 – 9.53, p=0.002). There were no interactions between subject weight and age.
L603: There appeared to be some interaction with the use of concomitant medications and the development of adverse events with Dysport treatment. Headache was strongly associated with Dysport treatment both in subjects who took antidepressants and in subjects who took muscle relaxants (and negatively associated in subjects who did not take these drugs). Complaints of neck pain were associated with Dysport treatment and the use of benzodiazepines. Loss of visual acuity appeared more commonly in Dysport-treated subjects who also took anticholinergic drugs.
L604: Subjects with respiratory illnesses at baseline appeared to have fewer complaints of adverse events in a number of categories when treated with Dysport compared to placebo. There were no apparent interactions between Dysport treatment and the presence of neurological disease (other than cervical dystonia) on the incidence of adverse events.
L605: There was only one study (051) with subject locations both in and out of the U.S. and it showed a significant disparity in reporting of adverse events. Russian subjects who received placebo were less likely to report at least one adverse event than American placebo subjects (odds ratio 0.32, 95% CI 0.05 – 1.41, p=0.16) but this difference was less remarkable than that among Dysport-treated subjects where only one of fourteen Russian subjects reported an adverse event but events were reported in 26 of 41 Americans (odds ratio 0.05, 95% CI 0.001 – 0.37, p=0.0005). Overall, Russian subjects were one-seventh as likely as American subjects to report at least one adverse event (odds ratio 0.14, 95% CI 0.03 – 0.46, p=0.0003). Comparing Dysport to placebo, the odds ratios for subjects reporting adverse events was 1.95 (95% CI 0.77 – 5.07, p=0.18) for American subjects and 0.29 (95% CI 0.003 – 4.29, p=0.60) for Russian subjects. None of the Russian subjects reported any of the adverse events that were most commonly reported among American subjects: dysphagia, injection site discomfort, muscular weakness, neck pain or sensory loss. The absence of reporting of any of these events suggests a failure of adverse event detection and reporting in the Russian centers; the Russian subjects and their data should probably be excluded from statistical analysis of adverse events although the number of subjects is too small to affect the conclusions; results were not re-analyzed with these subjects excluded.
L606: 7.1.6 Less Common Adverse events
L607: Examination of less frequent adverse events did not show any surprising events or new safety concerns.
L608: 7.1.7 Laboratory Findings
L609: 7.1.7.1 Overview of laboratory testing in the development program
L610: Laboratory testing was only performed in Phase 3, Studies 045 and 051 and their open label extensions. In study 051, laboratory samples were obtained at baseline, four weeks after initial treatment and at the end of the study (usually 12 weeks). In the open-label extension (Study 731), specimens were obtained on the day of any retreatment and four weeks after retreatment. In Study 045, laboratory testing was performed on samples from baseline and four, eight and twelve weeks after treatment. In the open label phase (Study 045b) specimens were obtained 4 weeks after any retreatment. Table 7.1.7.1-1 shows the number of subjects tested at baseline in each study.

L613: 7.1.7.2 Selection of studies and analyses for drug-control comparisons of laboratory values
L614: Laboratory results were evaluated using mixed effects models to stratify by subject and trial. Two approaches to comparison were made: 1) comparing changes from baseline in Dysport and placebo subjects during the double-blind phase and 2) comparing laboratory results obtained in subjects during exposure to Dysport with those obtained when they were not being treated. The first method has the advantage of not being influenced by changes occurring in subjects over
L615: time that are not related to treatment. The second method allows inclusion of data from open label extensions and treats subjects as their own control.
L616: 7.1.7.3 Standard analyses and explorations of laboratory data
L617: 7.1.7.3.1 Analyses focused on measures of central tendency
L618: Tables 7.1.7.3.1-1 and 7.1.7.3.1-2 are analyses of the effect of Dysport on laboratory examinations with quantitative results. The results were obtained using a mixed effects linear models with stratification for trial and subject comparing differences in mean values.

L623: There are several laboratory tests that merit further discussion:
L624: Albumin: Average serum albumin levels obtained after treatment with Dysport were 2.1% lower than albumin levels obtained in the same subjects at baseline or during treatment with placebo. This difference is also reflected in total protein levels that averaged 1.1% lower and even in serum calcium levels that were 0.7% lower. These changes, however, are not apparent in the comparison of Dysport to placebo in the double-blind phase. This could be the result of a larger sample size in the on drug / off drug comparison but could also reflect changes in subject condition unrelated to treatment or changes in laboratory procedures over the course of the studies. It could also be the result of a cumulative effect of Dysport treatment. Figure 7.1.7.3.1-1 shows the shift in distribution of serum albumin levels on and off Dysport. The differences appear to be a general effect on all Dysport-treated subjects rather than a large effect on a few subjects.
L625: Figure 7.1.7.3.1-1
L626: Serum AlbuminThe graph displays two overlapping bell-shaped curves representing the distribution of serum albumin levels. The x-axis is labeled 'gm/L' and has major tick marks at 30, 35, 40, 45, 50, and 55. The y-axis represents frequency or density. A legend box at the bottom center contains two entries: 'On Dysport' (represented by a solid line) and 'Off Dysport' (represented by a dashed line). Both curves start near zero at 30 gm/L, rise to a peak between 40 and 41 gm/L, and then decline back towards zero by 55 gm/L. The 'On Dysport' curve is consistently slightly higher than the 'Off Dysport' curve throughout the distribution, particularly at the peak.Line graph showing Serum Albumin levels (gm/L) for two groups: 'On Dysport' and 'Off Dysport'. The x-axis ranges from 30 to 55 gm/L. Both curves show a peak around 40-41 gm/L, with the 'On Dysport' curve being slightly higher than the 'Off Dysport' curve at the peak.
L627: Alkaline Phosphatase. There is a decline of about 2% in serum alkaline phosphatase levels in both comparisons with greater statistical significance in the on drug / off drug comparison because of larger sample size (Figure 7.1.7.3.1-2). The similarity in magnitude of the two results makes a secular trend or cumulative effect less likely. It is possible that small amounts of botulinum toxin are released systemically and affect osteoclast or liver function.
L628: Clinical ReviewMarc Stone, M.D.BLA 125274
L629: Dysport® (Clostridium botulinum toxin type A hemagglutinin complex)

L632: Bicarbonate: The modest increase in serum bicarbonate levels in the drug / placebo comparison that becomes prominent in the on drug / off drug comparison could be the result of changes in lab testing or secular trends in subjects but there is good reason to believe that this could be a cumulative effect of respiratory acidosis caused by respiratory depression from Dysport. Figures 7.1.7.3.1-3, 7.1.7.3.1-4 and 7.1.7.3.1-5 show the distribution of serum bicarbonate levels.

L640: Glucose. This is the only laboratory test where the effect of Dysport is more prominent in the double-blind period than in the on drug / off drug comparison. This could be the result of a general trend toward lower blood glucose values in the population over time, an adaptation to drug effect or a statistical anomaly. Figures 7.1.7.3.1-6, 7.1.7.3.1-7, 7.1.7.3.1-8 and 7.1.7.3.1-9 show the patterns of changes in random glucose levels. An effect on glucose levels could plausibly be the result of the effects of small amounts of botulinum toxin on pancreatic islet cells or hepatic glucose uptake.

L654: Lactic Dehydrogenase (LDH): The small reductions seen in both the drug / placebo and on drug /off drug comparisons could, once again, be the result of some minor effect on the liver.
L655: SGOT (AST) and SGPT (ALT): Elevations of these laboratory values are more prominent than the other hepatic-related changes but this could be the result of a single Dysport-treated subject with Hepatitis C. Removing this subject from the data affected the results for SGOT and SGPT, mostly in the drug / placebo double-blind phase comparison (Table 7.1.7.3.1-2), but had negligible effect on serum albumin and other tests. Because these analyses are based upon changes from baseline values, the rise in SGOT and SGPT values in the Hepatitis C infected subject occurred after the initiation of Dysport and could be treatment related. The rise in other subjects noted in the on drug / off drug comparison could be the result of a trend unrelated to treatment but could also be the result of a cumulative toxicity of Dysport to which the Hepatitis C infected subject was more sensitive.

L661: 7.1.7.3.2 Analyses focused on outliers or shifts from normal to abnormal
L662: The effect of Dysport treatment on likelihood of a laboratory test result outside of the normal range during the double-blind phase was evaluated using a mixed effects logistic model that stratified by trial and subject and accounted for the number of tests performed. Abnormal values for blood and urine glucose showed the strongest association with Dysport treatment, corresponding to the rise in average blood glucose levels noted in the previous section. The possible role of drug effect on the results for other tests cannot be distinguished from chance variation.

L667: 7.1.7.3.3 Marked outliers and dropouts for laboratory abnormalities
L668: There were 127 laboratory test results identified as clinically significant in 39 subjects. Ninety-four clinically significant results were recorded in 29 subjects while they were receiving Dysport. Sixteen of these 29 either had similar results at baseline or returned to normal while on Dysport treatment.
L669: One subject had blood glucose levels of 139, 111 and 124 mg/dl while receiving Dysport and levels of 98 and 96 mg/dl at baseline and on placebo. Another subject had a normal baseline glucose level of 107 mg/dl in August 1998 and had fluctuations of blood glucose in the range of 95-149 mg/dl during Dysport treatment from 1998 through mid-2000 but had levels of 311 and 288 mg/dl at the end of open-label treatment in early 2001. This subject also had baseline levels of SGOT and SGPT of 37 and 64 IU/L, respectively, which rose steadily during Dysport treatment, peaking at 131 and 308 IU/L when the blood glucose level was 308 mg/dl. LDH, total bilirubin and alkaline phosphatase were normal. The same subject was entered into another trial in 2006 and had normal glucose, SGOT and SGPT levels through five months of treatment. A third subject had a baseline glucose value of 99 mg/dl and levels of 130-141 mg/dl on Dysport. A fourth had a baseline glucose of 148 mg/dl without glycosuria followed by blood glucose levels of 145 and 207 mg/dl on Dysport with trace and + glycosuria, respectively. A fifth patient had a baseline glucose level of 99 mg/dl followed by levels of 112, 109 and 162 mg/dl during Dysport treatment.
L670: For hepatic tests, one subject had baseline and placebo average values of 190, 19.5 and 15 IU/L for LDH, SGOT and SGPT, respectively that rose to 243, 135 and 204 at the end of open-label Dysport treatment. Values for total bilirubin and alkaline phosphatase were essentially unchanged. In the subject with Hepatitis C infection, baseline values of 81, 153, 28 and 24 IU/L and 0.9 mg/dl for alkaline phosphatase, LDH, SGOT, SGPT and total bilirubin, respectively, rose to 87, 199, 100 and 203 IU/L and 1.1 mg/dl by the end of open label treatment.
L671: For serum electrolytes, one subject had baseline and placebo serum bicarbonate levels of 26.5 and 28.0 mmol/L that rose to 33.3 mmol/L by the end of open label treatment. This was accompanied by a rise in serum sodium from 141 to 150 mmol/L, serum potassium from 4.0 to 4.5 mmol/L and serum chloride from 102 to 109 mmol/L. Levels of BUN and creatinine were stable.
L672: For blood uric acid, one subject had baseline and placebo uric acid levels averaging 7.8 mg/dl that averaged 9.5 mg/dl on Dysport, reaching 12 mg/dl on the last measurement. Another subject averaged 7.6 mg/dl off Dysport but climbed to 8.5 mg/dl on the last on Dysport measurement.
L673: On urinalysis, one subject had four negative tests for blood off Dysport but readings of ++ and +++ blood in two of six tests on Dysport. Another had a negative baseline study but trace and + blood on two of three tests on Dysport. Another subject had negative microscopy at baseline but white cells or bacteria present with five of six microscopic examinations on Dysport.
L674: There were no dropouts due to laboratory abnormalities.
L675: 7.1.7.4 Additional analyses and explorations
L676: Because of the apparent association between Dysport treatment and elevation of glucose levels and glycosuria, further analyses of drug effect on blood glucose levels were done. The effect appears to be present in all subgroups.

L679: 7.1.7.5 Special assessments
L680: There were no reported cases in clinical trials or post-marketing reports describing substantial elevation of AST and ALT levels accompanied by abnormal elevation of total bilirubin (Hy's law).
L681: 7.1.8 Vital Signs
L682: 7.1.8.1 Overview of vital signs testing in the development program
L683: All Phase 2 and 3 studies measured height and calculated body mass index at baseline. Study 020 measured weight at baseline and on subsequent visits but made no other vital sign measurements. The other three studies; 042, 045 and 51/731 measured weight, temperature blood pressure and heart rate at baseline and on subsequent visits.
L684: 7.1.8.2 Selection of studies and analyses for overall drug-control comparisons
L685: All four principal randomized controlled studies were analyzed for changes in weight; studies 042, 045 and 51/731 were analyzed for changes in weight, temperature blood pressure and heart rate.
L686: 7.1.8.3 Standard analyses and explorations of vital signs data
L687: 7.1.8.3.1 Analyses focused on measures of central tendencies
L688: Vital signs were analyzed using the same mixed effects models used to evaluate laboratory values (Tables 7.1.8.3.1-1 and 7.1.8.3.1-2). Mean changes in vital sign readings did not appear to be affected by Dysport treatment. In the on drug / off drug comparison, a small increase in average heart rate was seen that was even smaller in the double-blind comparison.

L693: 7.1.8.3.2 Analyses focused on outliers or shifts from normal to abnormal
L694: Table 7.1.8.3.2-1 shows the odds of abnormal readings for heart rate and blood pressure for Dysport treatment compared to placebo during the double-blind phase. Although none of the differences were statistically significant, the proportion of vital sign readings that were abnormal was consistently less among Dysport-treated subjects than subjects treated with placebo. This could be a systemic effect of the drug; published studies have shown a reduction in heart rate variability after therapeutic dosages of botulinum toxin without a change in mean heart rate. It is possible, as well, that symptomatic improvement could have led to greater stability in heart rate
L695: Clinical Review
  Marc Stone, M.D.

L701: 7.1.8.4 Additional analyses and explorations
L702: 7.1.9 Electrocardiograms (ECGs)
L703: 7.1.9.1 Overview of ECG testing in the development program, including brief review of preclinical results
L704: A limited electrocardiographic study was attempted in 25 subjects enrolled in open-label study 731. On visits for retreatment (generally 12 to 16 weeks apart), three 12-lead ECG recordings were to be taken approximately 1 to 2 minutes apart prior to Dysport administration. Three recordings were also to be taken approximately 1 to 2 minutes apart approximately 30 minutes after treatment. The three readings taken immediately before Dysport administration served as the baseline readings for the treatment cycle. At a follow-up visit approximately 4 weeks after treatment, three more readings were taken approximately 1 to 2 minutes apart. At the end of study or at an early withdrawal visit, three readings were to be taken approximately 1 to 2 minutes apart. In practice, four of 25 subjects refused any ECG recording, one subject had only baseline recordings, two subjects each had one set of recordings 30 minutes after treatment without baseline recordings, four subjects had one set of baseline and 30 minute post-treatment recordings without four week follow-up, eleven subjects had one full cycle of baseline, 30
L705: minute and four week follow-up recordings, two subjects had two complete cycles and one subject had one complete cycle plus baseline and 30 minute recordings in a second cycle.
L706: ECG readings were manually performed at a central lab using an electronic caliper system of recordings displayed on a computer screen.
L707: 7.1.9.2 Selection of studies and analyses for overall drug-control comparisons
L708: Only one study obtained usable ECG data. As an open-label study there are no control subjects for comparison and there was no use of a positive control to assess assay sensitivity. The only informative comparisons are between pre-treatment values and values obtained 30 minutes and four weeks after treatment. Estimates of changes in quantitative ECG parameters (RR, PR, QRS, and QT intervals (with rate corrections) and heart rate) were estimated using a linear mixed effects model stratified by subject. The odds of observing an abnormal result, either in quantitative measures or in the qualitative assessment of rhythm or waveform were assessed using a random effects logistic model for events that occurred in both periods that are compared and by exact conditional logistic models when events occurred in only one period.
L709: 7.1.9.3 Standard analyses and explorations of ECG data
L710: 7.1.9.3.1 Analyses focused on measures of central tendency
L711: The results are shown in Tables 7.1.9.3.1-1 and 7.1.9.3.1-2. The most notable finding was an average reduction in heart rate (and increase in RR interval) of about three beats per minute, or 4%, between the time of baseline measurement and 30 minutes after injection. There was an increase in QT interval that would be expected with a reduction in heart rate but the increase persists after the Fridericia correction (generally considered more accurate than the Bazett correction) is applied. The reduction in heart rate could be a drug effect but it could also be the result of a vagal reflex in response to fear or discomfort surrounding the injection procedure itself; this cannot be determined without a placebo control. A reduction in heart rate for drug relative to placebo was not seen with vital sign measurements obtained during controlled trials (Table 7.1.8.3.1-1) but this may be because the vital signs were obtained prior to injection (the timing was not specified in protocol). Evidence of a QT prolongation effect from Dysport is strongly dependent on the choice of rate correction method. Fridericia may be superior to Bazett but it is far from ideal; an optimal rate-adjustment method may result in a larger estimate of QTc or may show it not to exist at all.

L722: One subject had two ECGs with QT intervals longer than 500 ms. In this subject the increase in QT interval appears to be larger than what could be expected solely by a change in heart rate. At baseline, the subject's QT intervals were measured at 485, 497 and 498 ms (average 493.3,
L723: Clinical ReviewMarc Stone, M.D.BLA 125274Dysport® (Clostridium botulinum toxin type A hemagglutinin complex)
L724: average with Bazett's adjustment, 461.7, average with Fridericia adjustment, 472.3) with an average heart rate of 52.7 bpm and RR interval of 1142.3 ms. Thirty minutes after treatment, QT was measured at 489, 512 and 518 ms (average 506.3, average with Bazett's adjustment, 470.7, average with Fridericia adjustment, 482.0), the average heart rate was 51.7 bpm and the average RR interval was 1159.3 ms. The increase in average RR interval was 1.5% while the increase in average QT interval was 2.6%. The pair of pre/post treatment ECG readings that were most closely matched by RR interval had an RR interval of 1141 ms pretreatment and 1145 ms thirty minutes after treatment with QT intervals of 485 ms and 512 ms, respectively. The same subject had an average PR interval of 237.7 ms at baseline that increased 2.9% to 244.7 ms thirty minutes after treatment. This subject's QRS interval also increased 5.8% from 108.3 ms at baseline to 114.7 thirty minutes after treatment.
L725: Only one subject had an increase in QT interval of more than 10 ms between baseline and 30 minutes after treatment using both the Fridericia and Bazett corrections. The average heart rate was 74.7 bpm before treatment and 72.0 bpm after. Uncorrected QT went from 379.7 ms to 400 ms. Using the Fridericia correction, QTc went from 408.3 ms to 425.3 ms and was 419.3 four weeks after treatment. With Bazett's correction, average QTc changed from 424 ms to 438.3 and was 433.3 four weeks later. The two most closely matched RR intervals were 808 ms before treatment and 813 ms thirty minutes after. The QT interval went from 384 ms to 402 ms.
L726: 7.1.9.3.3. Marked outliers and dropouts for ECG abnormalities
L727: There were no marked outliers or dropouts.
L728: 7.1.9.4 Additional analyses and explorations
L729: None
L730: 7.1.10 Immunogenicity
L731: Neutralizing antibody tests were performed on 173 subjects at baseline; 7.5% were positive. After treatment with Dysport, 3.6% of subjects who were negative at baseline developed antibodies on retesting. For subjects with positive antibody tests at baseline, 8.3% of those retested after treatment were found to be negative. The presence of neutralizing antibodies at baseline or their development during treatment had no apparent effect on the occurrence of adverse events.
L732: Data from the double-blind placebo controlled phases of Category 1 and 2 studies showed no difference in the incidence of adverse effects indicative of hypersensitivity; pruritus, urticaria, asthma, wheezing or hypotension; following Dysport injections (1.9%) compared with placebo (2.2%). These reactions occurred in 4.2% of Dysport-treated subjects treated in all phases. The sponsor could not exclude a causal relationship in two subjects with rash or pruritus from these 15 subjects with possible hypersensitivity. There was no evidence of causal association in remaining 13 subjects due to presence of an alternative cause, negative rechallenger or unlikely temporal relationship. The incidence of possible hypersensitivity events was higher in subjects who had a prior history of hypersensitivity compared with those who had no prior history in
L733: subjects in double-blind phases (5.9% vs. 1.1%) and in all phases (9.7% vs. 2.8%). The sponsor has received no post-marketing reports described or coded as anaphylactic shock.
L734: 7.1.11 Human Carcinogenicity
L735: Due to the infrequent clinical administration and the lack of concern for the mutagenic or clastogenic potential of Clostridium botulinum toxin type-A hemagglutinin complex and in accord with ICH Guidelines no carcinogenicity studies were conducted in animals. No data is available regarding carcinogenicity in humans. One case of prostate cancer was diagnosed during a clinical trial for cervical dystonia and a case of adenocarcinoma of the lung was discovered in a subject in a clinical trial for another indication.
L736: 7.1.12 Special Safety Studies
L737: Because this drug is intended for local administration in very small doses with almost no systemic spread, no special safety studies were required or performed.
L738: 7.1.13 Withdrawal Phenomena and/or Abuse Potential
L739: Dysport is intended to be administered by medical professionals and should have little potential for abuse if its availability is limited to those professionals. Withdrawal effects were not studied or reported in any developmental study for any indication. There was no evidence of withdrawal effects from spontaneous reports to the sponsor.
L740: 7.1.14 Human Reproduction and Pregnancy Data
L741: There were 37 cases detailing possible exposure during pregnancy, 11 in clinical trials and 26 spontaneous. No cases of exposure during pregnancy or lactation were reported from Category 1, 2 or 3 studies. Eleven cases were received from Category 4 studies. Of these, 7 subjects were known to have received Dysport and four cases occurred in ongoing studies which had not been unblinded at data cutoff. These eleven pregnancies are summarized in Table 7.1.1.14-1. Women with unfavorable outcomes were older and had slightly higher average total exposure to Dysport.

L748: 7.1.15 Assessment of Effect on Growth
L749: No pediatric experience is described.
L750: 7.1.16 Overdose Experience
L751: There is no significant information regarding overdose from clinical studies in cervical dystonia. Doses exceeding 2000 U Dysport were rarely studied in clinical settings for any indication. The sponsor identified 54 post-marketing reports of cases where dosages were in excess of the maximum recommended dose for the indication when the indication was licensed (39 cases) or, when the indication was not approved, exceeded the recommended dose for a licensed indication at the same site (15 cases). Cases where Dysport was administered at a site for which no licensed indication existed were not considered. In 31 of 54 case reports, adverse events consistent with pharmacological activity of Dysport occurred, predominantly muscle weakness and its consequences. Breathing difficulties occurred occasionally. The doses ranged from being 1.05 to 6 times higher than those recommended. Of the 20 cases where more than twice the recommended dose was used, eleven were cosmetic uses where the recommended dosages were very low (50-60 U) and three were for axillary hyperhidrosis, where the recommended dosage is 200 U. The assessment of these cases was frequently confounded by the complexity of the disease under treatment, e.g., spasticity or concomitant illness such as evidence of marked defective neuromuscular transmission or breathing difficulties.
L752: 7.1.17 Post-marketing Experience
L753: Table 7.1.17-1 compares the pattern of adverse events found in post-marketing reports from health care professionals of patients treated with Dysport for cervical dystonia to those reported in the Phase 2 and 3 trials. Nearly half (48%) of adverse events reported post-marketing were classified as serious compared to only 3% of adverse events reported in Phases 2 and 3. This is not surprising; investigators are required to report any adverse events that occur during clinical trials while events that occur during normal clinical practice are unlikely to be reported unless they are serious or unexpected. There is, therefore, no reason for the distribution of non-serious events reported post-marketing to resemble the distribution reported in clinical trials. In the post-marketing experience, the only category of non-serious adverse events that was reported frequently but that was relatively uncommon in the clinical development program was skin and subcutaneous disorders, principally rashes. The distribution of serious adverse events in post-marketing experience is also rather different than that reported in clinical trials. Most of the serious events reported in clinical development involved surgical procedures or hospitalization that were unlikely to be related to treatment for cervical dystonia. The post-marketing reports of serious adverse events, however, largely consist of more aggravated instances of non-serious adverse events reported in clinical trials: dysphagia, dyspnea, dysarthria, muscular weakness and fatigue, malaise or flu-like symptoms.

L757: 7.2 Adequacy of Patient Exposure and Safety Assessments
L758: 7.2.1 Description of Primary Clinical Data Sources (Populations Exposed and Extent of Exposure) Used to Evaluate Safety
L759: As noted in Section 7.1, the information in the Integrated Summary of Safety is principally based on findings from the main efficacy studies (Studies 045 and 051), the long-term open label follow-up studies (Studies 045b and 731, respectively), and two Phase 2 randomized studies (Studies 020 and 042). The sponsor refers to these studies as Category 1 and 2. For unclear reasons, a Phase II study comparing batches from different sources in treatment of glabellar lines is also called Category 2 but was not included explicitly in the sponsor's analyses. Category 3 consisted of additional studies in cervical and other dystonias. Category 4 consisted of studies for other indications.
L760: 7.2.1.1 Study type and design/patient enumeration
L761: Studies referenced in this application as Categories 1, 2 and 3 are listed in Table 7.2.1.1-1. They total approximately 3700 Dysport-treated subjects and 7300 Dysport treatments. The Category 4 studies consists of over 3900 additional Dysport-treated subjects.

L775: 7.2.1.3 Extent of exposure (dose/duration)
L776: Table 7.2.1.3-1 shows the cycle duration and dose per cycle for Dysport-treated subjects. Figure 7.2.1.3-1 shows the distribution of doses. L783: Figure 7.2.1.3-2: Cumulative Exposure to Dysport in Category 1 and 2 Studies
L784: Cumulative ExposureDysport Total UnitsLine graph showing Cumulative Exposure to Dysport in Category 1 and 2 Studies. The x-axis is labeled 'Dysport Total Units' with markers at 0, 5000, 10000, and 15000. The y-axis represents cumulative exposure. The curve starts at 0, rises sharply to a peak of approximately 1000 units around 1000 total units, then drops to a local minimum of about 500 units around 2000 total units. It then rises to a second, lower peak of about 800 units around 3000 total units, before declining steadily to a plateau of approximately 200 units by 10000 total units, remaining relatively flat until 15000 total units.
L785: 7.2.2 Description of Secondary Clinical Data Sources Used to Evaluate Safety
L786: 7.2.2.1 Other studies
L787: Category 3 and 4 studies served only as a source for reports of serious adverse events. The Category 3 studies are included in the application only as study reports without individual data listings, data sets or case report forms. Category 4 studies are listed but no other information is given.
L788: 7.2.2.2 Post-marketing experience
L789: Dysport has been licensed outside the United States for cervical dystonia and other indications. A description of adverse events reported to the sponsor is included in the application and discussed in Section 7.1.17.
L790: 7.2.2.3 Literature
L791: The sponsors conducted a literature search on the term “Dysport”. From the 1691 publications found, articles were excluded if they involved Ipsen-sponsored studies (635), were duplicative (27), were nonclinical (62), did not confirm the use of Dysport (3) or were review articles (13). Of the remaining studies, 815 did not investigate cervical dystonia and were reviewed for serious adverse events only. From the remaining 138 studies, 17 were identified using Cochrane criteria
L792: as "high quality". Safety findings reported in these papers were generally limited to adverse effects; no vital signs or clinical laboratory data were described. These studies included patients treated with doses up to 2000 U in a single cycle, up to 31 treatment cycles and cumulative doses up to 27,980 U. The adverse events reported in these papers were consistent with those seen in the Dysport Category 1 and 2 clinical trials. The most common adverse event was dysphagia. Muscular weakness, injection site disorders, dry mouth and fatigue were also described. Most adverse events were mild or moderate, and did not lead to withdrawal of subjects from treatment. Other side effects included brachial neuritis, parotid swelling, vertigo, dysphonia, hoarseness, blurred vision, dizziness and sicca syndrome.
L793: 7.2.3 Adequacy of Overall Clinical Experience
L794: The clinical development program for all indications, the activities of independent investigators, post-marketing experience with Dysport and experience with other botulinum toxin products provide a broad base of experience from which information about adverse effects should be more than adequate.
L795: 7.2.4 Adequacy of Special Animal and/or In Vitro Testing
L796: Animal testing was largely limited to observation of general effects of the drug and specific impact on muscle tissue. One study in rats included blood and urine testing and, interestingly showed some increase in blood glucose (particularly in males) and decrease in serum alkaline phosphatase, similar to what was seen in human subjects. No in vitro or animal testing relevant to cardiac electrophysiology was performed.
L797: 7.2.5 Adequacy of Routine Clinical Testing
L798: Because of the belief in a lack of systemic effect, clinical testing was limited. Blood and urine testing were only conducted in two clinical trials and electrocardiograms only were obtained as part of an uncontrolled open label study. The results of testing, however, are suggestive of some systemic effects that should require further investigation:
L799: • Glucose: The finding of elevations of blood glucose and glycosuria should be examined in more subjects for purposes of confirmation and pattern of potential remission. Glycosylated hemoglobin should also be measured. Studies in diabetic subjects should also be performed.• Bicarbonate: The increase in serum bicarbonate levels over time may be the result of reduced ventilation. This required further investigation in subjects with cervical dystonia and should include arterial blood gas measurement and spirometry.• Liver: Specimens from subjects with declines in LDH and alkaline phosphatase should be analyzed for isoenzyme changes. All liver biochemistry studies should be examined in more subjects.• Electrocardiogram: The findings of lowered heart rate and prolonged QTcF are sufficient to require a thorough QT study with placebo and active control.• Vital Signs: More information is needed concerning the timing of vital sign measurements relative to Dysport injection. Given the observation in the ECG data of a
L800: decline in heart rate 30 minutes after injection with resolution after 4 weeks, vital signs should be obtained 10, 20, 30, 45, 60, 90 and 120 minutes after injection as well as at one, three, seven, fourteen and twenty-one days.
L801: 7.2.6 Adequacy of Metabolic, Clearance, and Interaction Workup
L802: Although there is evidence of systemic effects from Dysport treatment, absorption is so limited, if it were not the severe toxicity of botulinum toxin would have catastrophic effects, that clearance and metabolism are unlikely to play a role.
L803: 7.2.7 Adequacy of Evaluation for Potential Adverse Events for Any New Drug and Particularly for Drugs in the Class Represented by the New Drug; Recommendations for Further Study
L804: The non-U.S. post-marketing experience with Dysport and the worldwide experience with other botulinum toxin products for the treatment of cervical dystonia and other indications have created a solid understanding of potential adverse events. In particular, the applicant has recognized the possibility of rare cases of systemic botulism.
L805: 7.2.8 Assessment of Quality and Completeness of Data
L806: As noted in Section 7.1, there is reason to suspect inadequate ascertainment of adverse events in Russian subjects in Study 051. Many of the narratives of serious adverse events are lacking in detail, a common problem in applications when the sponsor is not informed of the event before the subject is lost to follow-up.
L807: 7.2.9 Additional Submissions, Including Safety Update
L808: A 120-day safety update was submitted that contained limited new information. Primarily, there was additional information on subjects in Study 731, the long-term open label study including eight additional subjects who had previously been in the placebo arm of Study 051. There were also 32 post-marketing reports of serious adverse events and one death. This information has been included in the analyses in Sections 7.1 and 7.2.
L809: 7.3 Summary of Selected Drug-Related Adverse Events, Important Limitations of Data, and Conclusions
L810: There are nine adverse effects that appear with some consistency in clinical studies and post-marketing reports. In rough descending order of frequency:
L811: • Muscular Weakness: This was generally a result of an excess of therapeutic effect involving the muscles of the neck, face and jaw. It was the most common event, reported in 18.2% of Dysport-treated subjects and 4.4% of placebo-treated subjects in double-blind studies (Odds Ratio 5.42, 95% CI 2.42-12.14, p &lt; 0.0005) and in 28.9% of all Dysport-treated subjects. A few of these events (1.9% of Dysport-treated double-blind
L812: subjects and 3.6% of all Dysport-treated subjects but no placebo treated subjects) reported muscle weakness remote from the injection site. Muscle weakness also appeared to be dose-dependent, occurring in 28% of subjects assigned to 1000 U and 11% and 12% of subjects assigned to 250 U and 500 U, respectively, in the fixed dose study. It has also appeared in post-marketing reports as a serious event. Muscular weakness was more strongly related to Dysport treatment in Dysport-naïve subjects, possibly because of a lack of prior experience with optimal dosage and site of administration.
L813: • Dysphagia: This is the most common serious event in post-marketing reports; severe dysphagia was also one of the most common serious events reported in clinical studies and may have been involved in the development of pneumonia and a death. The likely cause is a local effect of the drug of the muscles of the head and neck involved in deglutition and, possibly, the upper esophagus. Dysphagia was reported in 17.4% of Dysport-treated subjects and 3.8% of placebo-treated subjects in double-blind studies (Odds Ratio 5.29, 95% CI 2.29-12.18, p &lt; 0.0005) with increasing incidence by dosage in the fixed dose study. Dysphagia was reported by 29.1% of all Dysport-treated subjects in the principal cervical dystonia studies.
• Injection Site Pain or Discomfort: This may be as much the result of the injection procedure as the drug: the incidence among Dysport users in the double-blind phase was only slightly higher than that among placebo (18.1% vs. 13.8%, Odds Ratio 1.60, 95% CI 0.91-2.82, p = 0.10). There was, however, a dose-related increase observed in the fixed dose study.
• Dry Mouth: This effect is consistent with an effect of the drug on the salivary glands or their innervations. This was seldom severe, likely because only some of the salivary glands were affected. There was clearly an excess of these events associated with Dysport treatment in the double-blind phase (15.9% vs. 6.6%, Odds Ratio 2.75, 95% CI 1.35-5.59, p = 0.005); it also showed a higher incidence in subjects treated with 1000 U in the fixed dose study and was reported in 20.1% of Dysport-treated subjects in the principal cervical dystonia studies.
• Fatigue, Malaise or Flu-Like Symptoms: This was reported only slightly more commonly among Dysport subjects than placebo subjects (12.7% vs. 10.9%, Odds Ratio 1.40, 95% CI 0.74-2.68, p = 0.30). There was also no dose-response relationship. Nonetheless this was a common complaint.
• Dysphonia or Dysarthria: This is another effect that was generally not considered severe and was somewhat less common than the first four conditions listed here (12.0% of all Dysport-treated subjects) but showed a likely excess of events relative to placebo (8.1% vs. 2.2%, Odds Ratio 3.80, 95% CI 1.17-12.35, p = 0.03) and a dose-response relationship. Dysphonia and dysarthria were more commonly reported in Dysport-naïve subjects. They are likely the result of Dysport on head and neck muscles involved in speech and the recurrent laryngeal nerve.
• Blurred Vision or Loss of Visual Acuity: These two terms were each recorded in less than 5% of Dysport-treated subjects but combined they occurred in 5.2% of Dysport-treated subjects and 1.1% of placebo subjects in the double-blind phase (Odds Ratio 5.78, 95% CI 1.14-29.3, p = 0.03) and the general category of Eye Disorders showed a dose-response pattern in the fixed dose study. The most plausible mechanism is loss of papillary accommodation because of drug effect on the superior cervical ganglion but this could also be an effect remote from the site of injection.
L814: • Blood Glucose Increased: Due to limited laboratory testing, this was recognized in only a few subjects and does not appear in post-marketing reports. The information included in this application does, however, indicate an effect of Dysport on glucose metabolism that could have substantial impact on subjects with diabetes. Further investigation is needed.• Dyspnea: This was an uncommon event in the double-blind phase that was slightly more common in Dysport users. It was, however, reported in 5.0% of all Dysport-treated subjects and respiratory complaints are a common serious complaint in post-marketing reports; 8.7% of serious adverse reactions involved dyspnea. The mechanism for this complaint is likely a weakening of accessory muscles of respiration in the neck, perhaps in conjunction with pre-existing lung disease.
L815: All of the adverse effects listed above, other than fatigue, merit inclusion in labeling.
L816: There are several other safety issues which require more information and that may require Phase 4 commitments for additional study. In addition to the issues involving clinical testing discussed in Section 7.2.5, the nearly complete absence of information in non-Caucasian subjects needs to be rectified. Because there is no reason a priori to expect result to be different in other ethnic groups, it is not sufficient reason to prevent approval.
L817: 7.4 General Methodology
L818: 7.4.1 Pooling Data across Studies to Estimate and Compare Incidence
L819: 7.4.1.1 Pooled data vs. individual study data
L820: Most of the analyses of safety issues provided by the sponsor involved pooling all of the Category 1 and 2 studies into a single dataset. This was not appropriate particularly because of the presence of different allocations of subjects between drug and placebo in different studies and the active solicitation of adverse events with a checklist in some studies and not in others. There were, however, no exceptional findings that were specific to particular studies.
L821: 7.4.1.2 Combining data
L822: Analyses were conducted by this reviewer using mixed effect models in which the main effect of treatment is stratified by the random effect of study. Study is properly considered a random effect because not all studies have the same expected result; they differ by treatment regimen, subject population and other factors. Adjustments were also made for the appearance of the same subject in more than one study.
L823: 7.4.2 Explorations for Predictive Factors
L824: Predictive factors were analyzed by adding variables for the presence of these factors and their interaction with treatment assignment to the model.
L825: 7.4.2.1 Explorations for dose dependency for adverse findings
L826: Most explanations for dose dependency were limited to study 020, the only study where subjects were randomized to specific doses. Analyses of dose dependency in variable-dose studies are confounded by pharmacodynamic factors; subjects who require larger doses may not be more resistant to drug effect but may have anatomical factors that limit how much of the drug reaches the site of action for either therapeutic or adverse effect.
L827: 7.4.2.2 Explorations for time dependency for adverse findings
L828: Time to onset and duration of events were analyzed by the sponsor for events of particular interest. For events other than injection site reactions (median time to onset 0 days, median duration 5 days), events followed a similar time pattern of median time of onset of 5 to 7 days after treatment and median duration of 2 – 6 weeks.
L829: 7.4.2.3 Explorations for drug-demographic interactions
L830: Demographic factors were analyzed by adding variables for the presence of these factors and their interaction with treatment assignment to the model.
L831: 7.4.2.4 Explorations for drug-disease interactions
L832: Co-morbid conditions were analyzed by adding variables for the presence of these factors and their interaction with treatment assignment to the model.
L833: 7.4.2.5 Explorations for drug-drug interactions
L834: Concomitant medications were analyzed by adding variables for the presence of these factors and their interaction with treatment assignment to the model.
L835: 7.4.3 Causality Determination
L836: The sponsor makes no explicit causality determinations other than the opinion of the site investigator as to whether an adverse event was likely to be drug-related. In this review, opinions as to causality are based upon:
L837: 1. Clinical plausibility: the consistency of the phenomenon with the anatomy of the area of injection and the known pharmacological effects of the drug. When individual events are considered, the timing of the event is an important factor in the assessment of plausibility.2. Possibility of confounding: the likelihood that the phenomenon is a manifestation of the condition being treated. When individual events are considered, the likelihood that a co-morbid condition could be responsible for the event is also considered.3. Statistical association: the plausibility that the association of the phenomenon with drug treatment is due to chance. Because these associations are not considered prospectively and the number of potential associations is large, it is likely that some observed associations are due to chance, even if nominal p-values are small. The probability that any single association may be due to chance is, however, proportionate to its p-value.
L838: 4. Evidence of dose-response: the tendency for the phenomenon to occur more frequently at higher doses in fixed dose Study 020.
L839: 8 ADDITIONAL CLINICAL ISSUES
L840: 8.1 Dosing Regimen and Administration
L841: Most of the evident adverse effects of Dysport show dose dependency. Emphasis should be placed on using the lowest dose that can be expected to be effective.
L842: 8.2 Drug-Drug Interactions
L843: The very low amount of systemic absorption of Dysport make it very unlikely that any interaction based of pharmacokinetic factors would occur. Pharmacodynamic interactions are possible. For some of the associations of adverse events with concomitant medications noted in this application, the concomitant medication may be a reflection of a subject's general medical condition that may include a propensity to report the adverse effect with Dysport treatment. This could be the case of subjects who took antidepressants or muscle relaxants and reported headache with Dysport or subjects taking benzodiazepines who reported neck pain. Reduced parasympathetic control from anticholinergic drugs may combine with reduced sympathetic control due to the effect of Dysport on the superior cervical ganglion may cause blurred vision by preventing regulation of pupil size.
L844: 8.3 Special Populations
L845: There was very little information on the use of Dysport for cervical dystonia in ethnic minorities.
L846: There is very little information of the effect of Dysport in pregnancy. Most reports of Dysport use in pregnant women were for cosmetic uses where dosages are much smaller than for cervical dystonia. Although the very limited systemic absorption of the drug would suggest that treatment of a pregnant woman should not be particularly dangerous to the fetus, it should be avoided unless the reasons for treatment are compelling.
L847: 8.4 Pediatrics
L848: There is no information on pediatric use in this application.
L849: 8.5 Advisory Committee Meeting
L850: No Advisory Committee meetings have been conducted or are contemplated.
L851: 8.6 Literature Review
L852: No review was conducted other than that contained in the application.
L853: 8.7 Post-marketing Risk Management Plan
L854: No risk management was proposed by the sponsor. It is possible that FDA may propose a plan for all botulinum toxin products to deal with the issue of systemic spread.
L855: 8.8 Other Relevant Materials
L856: OSE recently completed a review of reports of iatrogenic botulism submitted by Solstice and Allergan, the sponsors of the two botulinum toxin products currently licensed in the U.S. The report concluded that there is a risk for the development of systemic botulism in patients receiving botulinum toxin injections. This risk is probably greatest in children treated for spasticity associated with cerebral palsy but has also been observed in adults. The dosages used appear generally comparable to dosages used to treat cervical dystonia. This application also refers to a case of systemic botulism in a patient treated with Dysport as well as the observation of muscular weakness remote to the injection site in a few Dysport-treated subjects; the findings of the review of the other botulinum toxin products should be applicable to Dysport.
L857: 9 OVERALL ASSESSMENT
L858: 9.1 Conclusions
L859: There are no safety issues that would preclude the approval of Dysport for the treatment of cervical dystonia. Adverse reactions of concern and recommendations for additional evaluation can be found in Sections 7.2.5 and 7.3.
L860: 9.2 Recommendation on Regulatory Action
L861: There are no safety issues that preclude approval.
L862: 9.3 Recommendation on Post-marketing Actions
L863: 9.3.1 Risk Management Activity
L864: The Division is currently evaluating risk management activities for the class of botulinum toxin products. These would be applicable to Dysport.
L865: 9.3.2 Required Phase 4 Commitments
L866: There should be a commitment to five Phase 4 studies:
L867: 1. A study of Dysport in patients with diabetes2. A study of changes in ventilation due to Dysport treatment including serum bicarbonate and arterial blood gas measurement and pulmonary function tests. If possible, the study should be enriched with subjects with mild obstructive lung disease.
L868: 3. The effect of Dysport injection on vital signs in the immediate post-treatment period in patients with cervical dystonia4. A thorough QT study5. A study of safety and effectiveness of Dysport that includes substantial numbers of patients with African, Asian and Hispanic ancestry
L869: Please refer to Sections 7.2.5 and 7.3 for justifications for Phase 4 commitment recommendations.
L870: 9.3.3 Other Phase 4 Requests
L871: Further analysis of the effects of Dysport on liver-related biochemistry tests should be requested. Please refer to Sections 7.2.5 and 7.3 for justification.
L872: 9.4 Labeling Review
L873: An annotated label indicating recommended changes to the sponsor's proposal is given in Appendix 10.2. Principal recommendations consist of:
L874: 1. Inclusion of class labeling discussing potential for systemic spread of toxin2. More detailed discussion of potential breathing and swallowing difficulties3. Inclusion of discussion of changes in blood glucose and electrocardiographic findings seen with Dysport treatment in the clinical development program.4. A request a more extensive listing of adverse events reported in post-marketing experience.
L875: 9.5 Comments to Applicant
L876: Comments regarding Phase 4 activities and changes in the proposed label are listed in Sections 9.3.2 and Appendix 10.2.
