L893: 1.1 Recommendation on Regulatory Action
L894: Ipsen has submitted a Biologics License Application with two Phase 3, multi-center, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled studies using Dysport (Clostridium botulinum type A toxin-hemagglutinin complex) by intramuscular injection for the treatment of cervical dystonia (CD). The primary efficacy endpoint for both studies was the change from baseline in the Toronto Western Spasmodic Torticollis Rating Scale (TWSTRS) Total score at Week 4 assessments. Both pivotal studies showed Dysport 500U treatment to be statistically significant, compared to placebo, at the Week 4 for the efficacy endpoints.
L895: Analysis of the secondary and tertiary endpoints generally showed statistical significance, or strongly suggestive outcomes, favoring Dysport treatment for CD. Study assessments were planned at Weeks 4, 8, and 12. The trials allowed the subjects to switch to open-label extension studies any time after the Week 4 assessments. The statistical analyses were conducted on the ITT population throughout, with missing data (from early exits) at the Week 8 and Week 12 assessments, imputed using the subject's baseline scores. In the event of imputing missing values for TWSTRS and VAS for pain scores at Weeks 8 and 12, the last observation carried forward (LOCF) was used. Although the sponsor was able to show statistical significance at these later assessment visits, the high rate of subjects switching early to the open-label studies limits the conclusions that can be drawn from them, particularly in Study 045 which had the higher exit rate (prior to the Week 12 assessments, 13/70, 19% of the placebo group, and 28/66, 42% of the Dysport group had already switched over to the open-label study).
L896: The subject enrollment in both studies was well-balanced except for the under-representation of non-Caucasian subjects. One trial was done entirely in the US, the other was done in the US and Russia. Both de novo and treatment-experienced subjects were enrolled in the studies, with a 16 week washout period required if subjects had been previously treated with any botulinum toxin for CD.
L897: The dosage choice (500U) used in the Phase 3 trials appears to have been adequately established by the sponsor's earlier studies, and is the standard recommended starting dose. Both the extension studies and the post-marketing safety reports seem to validate the choice of the dosage used in the pivotal trials and justify the favorable risk/benefit conclusions.
L898: Based on the efficacy review of the submitted data from the Phase 3 trials and supportive data from the safety review, statistical review, Phase 2 trials and the literature, I would recommend approval for the biologics licensing application.
L899: 1.2 Recommendation on Postmarketing Actions
L900: Recommendations on post-marketing actions are currently under discussion for this product, and have not been finalized at the time of this review. The issues under consideration are not directly related to the quality of the efficacy trials. The issues deal mainly with product stability, and with possible The following recommendations include the recommendations of the safety review, pharm/tox. review, and chemistry review.
L901: 1.2.1 Risk Management Activity
L902: The safety review has stated that no risk management activity is recommended specifically for Dysport beyond standard labeling and post-marketing surveillance for adverse events. Any risk management activity required for the class of botulinum toxin products should be applicable to Dysport.
L903: 1.2.2 Required Phase 4 Commitments
L904: There should be a commitment to five Phase 4 studies:
L905: 1. A study of Dysport in patients with diabetes2. A study of changes in ventilation due to Dysport treatment including serum bicarbonate and arterial blood gas measurement and pulmonary function tests. If possible, the study should be enriched with subjects with mild obstructive lung disease.3. The effect of Dysport injection on vital signs in the immediate post-treatment period in patients with cervical dystonia4. A thorough QT study5. A study of safety and effectiveness of Dysport including substantial numbers of patients with African, Asian and Hispanic ancestry
L906: 1.2.3 Other Phase 4 Requests
L907: Further analysis of the effects of Dysport on liver-related biochemistry tests should be requested.
L908: 1.3 Summary of Clinical Findings
L909: 1.3.1 Brief Overview of Clinical Program
L910: Dysport is a neurotoxin complex manufactured by Ipsen Biopharm, Ltd. The product is a Clostridium botulinum type A toxin-hemagglutinin complex (contains lactose and human albumin). The drug prevents the release of acetylcholine from vesicles at the motor end-plate which chemically denervates the muscle temporarily until the sprouting of new nerve end terminals.
L911: The clinical studies submitted are in support of Dysport for the treatment of cervical dystonia (CD) in adults. For CD treatment, the sponsor's recommended starting dose is 500 U by intramuscular (IM) injection divided among 3 to 4 affected cervical muscles.
L912: Ipsen has sponsored &gt; 80 clinical trials with Dysport; 17 were for the treatment of cervical dystonia.
L913: Ipsen has submitted two randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled pivotal trials for the indication of CD (submitted under BB-IND# 7434):
L914: • Study 051, conducted in the US and Russia,
• Study 045 conducted in the US.
L915: In addition, Ipsen has submitted safety data from the following studies:
L916: • Study 020, a European dose-ranging study with treatment naïve subjects
• Study 042, a European confirmatory dose study,
• Study 045b, the open-label extension of Study 045,
• safety data (up to a cut-off date of September 20, 2006) for the on-going Study 731, the open-label extension of Study 051.
L917: The two pivotal studies are shown in Table 1 (Section 1.3.2). The summaries of all previous Dysport trials are to be found in Appendix 10.1.2.
L918: The number of subjects in the safety database that received Dysport is 365 in the principal clinical studies. Ipsen Biopharm Ltd. has a large post-marketing base of data for use of the drug overseas for a variety of indications. See the safety review by Dr. Marc Stone.
L919: 1.3.2 Efficacy
L920: The Category 1 studies conducted by the sponsor for the use of Dysport in the treatment of cervical dystonia are listed in Table 1. Study 051 and Study 045 are the Phase 3 studies for safety and efficacy.
L921: Table 1. Placebo-Controlled Efficacy Studies of Dysport in the Treatment of Cervical Dystonia
L922: Study NumbersY-97-52120-045 [a] (Study 045)Y-47-52120-051 (Study 051)

L929: a NOTE: Study Y-47-52120-731 (Study 731) is currently in progress. The initial submission of the BLA includes data as of the first interim analysis cut-off date of September 28, 2006. The 120 Day Safety Update includes data from the second interim analysis.Source: Clinical Overview.
L930: The choice of primary and non-primary endpoints was appropriate. The TWSTRS has been used extensively for evaluation of cervical dystonia in the US. Within each of the studies, the study populations showed a typical ratio of female to male subjects (~65% to 35%, cervical dystonia has a slightly higher prevalence in females), and there was subject similarity with respect to age (mean age 53 years), height and weight, cervical dystonia history, previous botulinum toxin treatment, and use of concomitant medications. Non-Caucasian subjects were under-represented in the studies.
L931: The primary endpoint for both Study 051 and Study 045 was the change from baseline in the TWSTRS (Toronto Western Spasmodic Torticollis Rating Scale) Total Score. The TWSTRS Total Score (max. = 85 points) is composed of 3 subscales (Severity, Disability, and Pain). The secondary and tertiary endpoints for both studies are also very similar using the VAS (Visual Analog Scale) Pain and VAS Symptoms assessed by both subjects and investigators, and the TWSTRS subscales (Study 045 used all three
L932: subscales as non-primary endpoints, Study 051 used the Severity and Disability sub-scale scores as non-primary endpoints).
L933: The sponsor considers the pivotal studies to be 12-week safety and efficacy studies. The difficulty with interpretation of the efficacy data from the studies is due to the sponsor's decision to allow subjects to switch from the placebo-controlled to the open-label phase of treatment any time after the Week 4 assessments. Subjects could exit due to a "non-responder" status (&lt;10 point improvement on the TWSTRS Total score from baseline) or because the investigator or subjects wanted to change the dosage or frequency of treatment. As a result, only the Week 4 assessments have an adequate statistical data base for generalizations and assumptions in Study 045.
L934: The early exit rate was higher in Study 045. Study 051 was conducted after a protocol review by the FDA, but despite a lower "early exit rate" still had some of the same design and analysis problems with many exiting (especially from the placebo group) prior to the end of the study.
L935: The Study 045b, the open-label extension of Study 045 consisted of 2 parts. The sponsor labeled these the "open-label phase" and the "extended use phase". The "open-label phase" allowed for 3 additional Dysport treatments. The first "open-label phase" treatment was fixed at 500 U since Study 045 was still blinded, and the next two re-treatments at variable in dosage and time between treatments. Study 045b did not have a separate protocol, so definitions were lacking on when subjects could be switched to open-label use, whether on the basis of the non-responder definition based on a change of "&lt; 10 point change" in TWSTRS-Total score on an assessment during Study 045, or whether the subject or investigator chose to switch to open-label phase because a change in dosage or frequency was desired.
L936: 1.3.3 Safety
L937: Dr. Marc Stone is submitting a separate safety review for BLA 125274. The most frequently reported adverse events (AEs) were dysphagia, cervical muscle weakness, dysphonia or dysarthria, dry mouth, blurring or other loss of visual acuity, dyspnea, and injection site discomfort. These safety findings are in accordance with the findings of previous studies on the botulinum toxins. The safety review also noted an elevation of blood glucose, which may require additional study to determine whether the finding is an artifact or reproducible. There were no deaths or serious adverse events reported in the controlled studies reviewed. Dysphagia appears to be the most serious adverse effect; since it can result in aspiration, and the need for a feeding tube. Dyspnea likely results from weakening of accessory muscles of ventilation and may pose a risk for patients with lung disease.
L938: The safety review states that "Issues requiring further investigation include the effect of Dysport on patients with diabetes, the cumulative effect of the drug on ventilation, changes in heart rate and electrocardiogram seen during treatment and the results of treatment in ethnic minorities."
L939: The frequency of AE reporting is considerably higher in the data from the Russian sites than from the US sites. The DSI report on the four Russian sites is pending.
L940: 1.3.4 Dosing Regimen and Administration
L941: The sponsor submitted literature report studies which established the dose-response curve using the extensor digitorum brevis (EDB) of the foot of healthy volunteers, the studies for the treatment of glabellar line, and the cervical dystonia dose-ranging study. The Dysport clinical studies have shown a significant dose-response curve. Although better efficacy in the treatment of cervical dystonia is reported using higher doses, a higher incidence of adverse events occurs.
L942: In the current submission, the sponsor recommends an intramuscular injection starting dose of 500 U Dysport divided among affected cervical muscles (max. = 3 to 4) for treatment of CD. Retreatment should be done as needed, but the sponsor recommends that it should be at least 12 weeks from the previous injections. Dose titration to the optimal dosing is recommended for subsequent treatments (250 to 1000 U) with titration in increments of 250 U. The recommendation of 500 U for the recommended starting dose is in accordance with Ipsen's dose-ranging study (Study 020), and current use patterns in Dysport for CD overseas, for which postmarketing reports were submitted.
L943: The Ipsen units apply only to Dysport and do not apply to other botulinum toxin compounds (i.e., are not interchangeable). Due to differences in specific details such as the vehicle, dilution scheme and laboratory protocols for various mouse LD50 assays, units of biologic activity cannot be compared to or converted into units of any other botulinum toxin. One unit (U) is defined as the median lethal intraperitoneal dose in mice.
L944: The lack of unit standardization between Dysport and Botox (the botulinum toxin type A currently marketed in the US) has raised issues of concern in the review process for Dysport. Different products (with the same active ingredient and generic name) have not previously been approved by CDER. There are safety concerns that different units could result in accidental underdosing, or more seriously, overdosing if the products are switched during the course of a patient's treatment. Previous studies have suggested a conversion ratio of ~ 1:3 for Botox and Dysport (100 U Botox ~ equal to 300 U Dysport).
L945: Another issue that has arisen is a loss of potency (of 20%) over 10 months from lot release. The sponsor has reported that the batch may have affected trials in treatment of cervical dystonia (in Study 045, batch 159J), and also a trial for treatment of glabellar lines. Additional subjects were added to the Study 045, and the subjects receiving the batch 159J were not included in the primary statistical analysis of endpoint. However, the issue of duration of potency retention may also present the possibility of medication errors if more than one botulinum toxin product is on the market. Similarly, the effect of the drug's strength may vary from one treatment session to another based on how close the drug is to the expiration date, and result in underdosing or overdosing.
L946: 1.3.5 Drug-Drug Interactions
L947: Formal drug interaction studies have not been conducted.
L948: On the Dysport label approved for UK use, caution is recommended for patients on the following medications:
L949: • Aminoglycoside antibiotics (possible increased effect of botulinum toxin)• Chloroquine and hydroxychloroquine (possible reduced effect)• Blood thinning agents, e.g. warfarin or aspirin (increased bruising)
L950: Other sites state that penicillamine, procainamide, spectinomycin, polymixins, tetracyclines and lincomycin can also interfere with the way Dysport works and should not be taken concurrently.
L951: 1.3.6 Special Populations
L952: Use of botulinum toxin is contraindicated in patients with a known sensitivity to any ingredient in the formulation.
L953: Patients with peripheral motor neuropathic diseases (e.g., amyotrophic lateral sclerosis, motor neuropathy) or neuromuscular junctional disorders (e.g., myasthenia gravis or Lambert-Eaton syndrome) should not receive Dysport. Patients with neuromuscular disorders may be at increased risk of systemic effects including severe dysphagia and respiratory compromise from typical doses.
L954: Safety and efficacy in pediatric use is not well established. Botulinum toxin use should be avoided in pregnant women and nursing mothers.
L955: In the controlled studies, there appears to be similar safety and efficacy results in subjects &gt; age 65 and &lt; age 65. Very few subjects over age 75 were enrolled in clinical trials, so no conclusions can be made for that age group.
L956: 2 INTRODUCTION AND BACKGROUND
L957: 2.1 Background
L958: Clostridium botulinum, a bacterium that causes food poisoning (botulism), produces a potent neurotoxin. Clostridium botulinum bacteria and their spores are found in abundance world-wide in soil and marine sediment, and often found on the surfaces of plants and vegetables, and in seafood. The bacteria and spores are harmless, but the toxin produced is the most powerful poison known. The toxin is produced under anaerobic, low acidic conditions, between 40 and 120 degrees Fahrenheit (4.5 to 49 degrees Celsius).
L959: Of the seven known types of C. botulinum toxin (types A to G, and 3 subtypes of A), only types A and B are licensed for medical treatments. The purified botulinum toxins have been used in minute quantities, both on-label and off-label, for a number of dystonias, neuromuscular disorders causing involuntary muscle contractions or spasms.
L960: These include conditions such as blepharospasm, strabismus, hemifacial spasms, cervical dystonia (also called spastic torticollis), oromandibular dystonia, urinary retention, spastic dysphonia, vocal tremor, stuttering, and limb spasticity. The possible use of botulinum toxin as a bioweapon is receiving on-going investigation.
L961: Botulinum toxin prevents the nerve signals from reaching the muscles by binding to nerve endings blocking the release of acetylcholine by a 3-stage process. The result is the loss of muscular contraction (flaccid paralysis). When minute quantities of purified botulinum toxin are injected into a muscle, the muscle is paralyzed or weakened, but should not result in local spread to other muscles or systemic spread. The treatment is used on muscles in chronic contracture or spasm causing pain, abnormal postural or abnormal function. Botulinum toxin can also reduce sweating by blocking the sympathetic nerve fibers supplying sweat glands. Treatment with botulinum toxin is more effective for small rather than large muscles, and the effects are temporary. Nerve regeneration occurs. Retreatment on average is needed every three to four months, although effectiveness may last longer for some individuals, and a small percentage (~2 to 5% do not respond to treatment). Antibodies can develop, and their role in response to therapy effectiveness is not yet clarified.
L962: Cervical dystonia (also known as spastic or spasmodic torticollis) is the most common type of focal dystonia. It is an asymmetrical hyperkinesia the neck, and sometimes the shoulders, due to muscle contractions (typically sustained, but some occur as spastic jerks or head tremor) causing abnormal posturing, often accompanied by pain. The cause may be primary or secondary (such as physical trauma); a few cases of inherited CD are known with early onset and associated with the DYT1 gene. In ~ 30% and associated postural hand tremor is present. The resultant neck positioning is directional and described as laterocollis (side-tilting of the head), retrocollis (head tilting backward) or anterocollis (forward tilting of the head). Characteristic of CD is the temporary relief with “a sensory trick” (a geste antagoniste) such as touching the face or back of the head which results in correction of the abnormal posture. Dysphagia may occur especially in patients with extreme retrocollis. The abnormal posture also tends to disappear during sleep. The postural deviations do not typically involve contractures, but with chronic postural change hypertrophy of neck muscles and fixed deformities can occur. No definite pathologic etiology has been established. No confirmatory tests are available. Diagnosis is made based on clinical symptoms and the ruling out of alternative diagnoses. Current theories favor abnormalities in the basal ganglia or brainstem pathways. Putaminal lesions have been demonstrated to produce contralateral dystonia. Secondary CD due to trauma generally does not show improvement with “sensory tricks” or improve during sleep.
L963: The prevalence rate of CD is ~ 9 in 100,000 (Nutt, et al, 1988). The overall incidence rate was established at 1.2 per 100,000 (Claypool et al, 1995) with the estimate for the US population at ~80,000. Onset of CD can occur at any age, but is usually between ages 30 to 55 years, with peak onset at 40 to 49 years. There is a slight female preponderance (~ 65% women, 35% men) noted in previous studies, and also seen in the sponsor's
L964: submitted clinical trials. CD may have “spontaneous remissions” especially early in the course of the disease, but it typically tends to recur and stabilize by about the fifth year.
L965: 2.1.1 Product Information
L966: Dysport is manufactured by:Ipsen Biopharm Limited, Ash Road, Wrexham Industrial Estate,Wrexham LL13 9UF, UKThe product license holder is:Ipsen Limited, 190 Bath Road, Slough, Berkshire, SL1 3XE UK
L967: Each pack contains 1 vial of Dysport. Each vial contains (as a small pellet of white lyophilized powder) 500 units of C. botulinum type A neurotoxin-hemagglutinin complex as the active ingredient. The complex contains lactose (2.5 mg) and a small amount of albumin solution (125 mcg) obtained from human blood. The powder must be mixed with sterile 0.9% sodium chloride solution. There is no antimicrobial agent, so the product is for the use of one patient only on one occasion, and any remaining product must be discarded.
L968: The Dysport label for use in the UK has recommendations for:
L969: • Cervical dystonia: divided between 2 or 3 neck muscles, no dosage specified, injections in a hospital ~ every 12 weeks• Arm spasticity: dose \leq 1000 units total divided among affected muscles, injections in hospital or specialty clinic ~ every 12 weeks, in conjunction with physiotherapy• Blepharospasm: first injection ~120 units per affected eye, reduced to 60 or 80 units per eye on subsequent visits, injected just under the skin around each eye, injections ~ every 12 weeks• Hemifacial spasm: same as for blepharospasm but on affected side only• Pediatric cerebral palsy: equinus foot deformity (children \geq 2 years of age) first dose 20 units/kg divided between both calf muscles (or 10 mg/kg if only one affected calf muscle), frequency ~ every 16 weeks
L970: 2.2 Currently Available Treatment for Indications
L971: Botox (Allergan) and Myobloc (Solstice) both have indications for the treatment of cervical dystonia (CD) in the US. In the United States, Oculinum was approved by the FDA in December 1989 for the treatment of blepharospasm (eyelid spasm) and strabismus (squint). It is now marketed in the US as Botox (C. botulinum toxin type A) for multiple indications including cervical dystonia. Myobloc (C. botulinum toxin type B) was approved in the US for cervical dystonia in December, 2000.
L972: Dysport, Botox and Myobloc each have distinct methods of assay. The units of potency are not equivalent, and they cannot be used interchangeably. The FDA is currently
L973: considering group labeling addressed at the spread of toxin to areas distant from the site of injection.
L974: The Botox label states only that Phase 3 studies enrolled patients with extended histories of tolerating previous Botox injections, and a mean dose of 236 units was used. The recommended initial dose of Botox is not stated on the label. A “lower initial dose” is recommended for patients without previous treatment.
L975: The recommended initial dose of Myobloc for patients with a history of tolerance of prior botulinum toxin injection is 2500 to 5000 U divided among affected muscles, and a lower dose (not specified) is recommended for patients without previous treatment.
L976: Other treatments for cervical dystonia have not shown much success, and botulinum toxin injections have become the primary treatment choice. The other options include oral anticholinergics, anticonvulsants, haloperidol, benzodiazepines, L-dopa, acupuncture and physical therapy. Rhizotomy, myotomy, thalamotomy and deep brain stimulation have also been used for treatment with varying success.
L977: 2.3 Availability of Proposed Active Ingredient in the United States
L978: Ipsen is using Dysport manufactured by Ipsen Biopharm, Ltd (IBL) using bulk active substance (BAS) material from the site intended for commercialization (IBL, Wrexham, UK) which they are registering as a new BAS facility in Europe (see Section 2.1.1). The Dysport for Study 051 came from the Wrexham facility, but Study 045 used BAS material produced at the (b) (4)
L979: See CMC review for additional details.
L980: 2.4 Important Issues with Pharmacologically Related Products
L981: The safety profile for use of all the purified botulinum toxin injections has generally been good. There have been some issues with local spread of the toxin causing situations such as ptosis associated with cosmetic use. In cervical dystonia treatment, a dysphagia is a fairly common side effect, and can (rarely) cause life-threatening aspiration pneumonia. Recently the botulinum toxins have been associated with a few cases of systemic spread (botulism) causing life-threatening illness and even death in children. So far, the botulism cases have occurred with off-label use for spasticity. The safety reviews of the botulinum toxins are in progress at the FDA, and may potentially involve labeling revisions for the class of drugs. In the on-going FDA safety review of the botulinum toxins, data from the Dysport trials and international post-marketing experience are included along with the safety information collected on the other two botulinum toxins currently marketed in the US.
L982: Patients with diagnosed or previously undiagnosed underlying neurological disorders, such as myasthenia gravis or swallowing difficulties, are at increased risk. Patients with underlying weakness may be at increased risk for use of the botulinum toxins even at normal treatment levels, or with a history of previously tolerated treatment. There may
L983: also be increased systemic or muscle sensitivity to the botulinum toxins in some patients. If there is a history of dysphagia and aspiration, treatment should only be done with extreme caution.
L984: Since the product contains a small amount of human albumin, the possibility of viral infection transmission, although remote, and never reported to date, cannot be excluded, and there is a theoretical possibility of Cruetzfeld-Jakob disease.
L985: 2.5 Pre-submission Regulatory Activity
L986: Orphan Drug Application 98-1411 was accepted by the FDA under Section 5a26 of the Food and Drug and Cosmetic Act (FD &amp; C) on August 12, 1998 granting Dysport Orphan Drug status for the treatment of cervical dystonia. Since the onset for cervical dystonia is usually in the fourth decade of life, the sponsor has requested a pediatric waver. A Special Protocol Assessment for Study 051 was done by the FDA and included recommendations for the categorizing of the safety and efficacy data. See Table 3.

L992: product is proposed by the sponsor since if approved, Reloxin would have a different US distributor than Dysport.
L993: 3 SIGNIFICANT FINDINGS FROM OTHER REVIEWS
L994: 3.1 CMC
L995: Dysport is supplied as a single use freeze dried product in a 3 ml glass vial containing 500 U of drug, 125 mcg of albumin and 2.5 mg of lactose to be reconstituted with 1 ml of 0.9% Sodium Chloride Injection USP.
L996: The initial dose for injection is designated as 500 U Dysport (divided among affected cervical muscle groups, max = 3 to 4). The sponsor recommends titration for subsequent injections with dose adjustments by 250 U steps, and a total SD of 250-1000 U given 12-16 weeks apart.
L997: The CMC review for BLA 125274 was conducted by Dr. Susan Kirshner and will be reviewed separately. Issues regarding stability and standardization of units are still under discussion between the FDA and the sponsor at the time of this review.
L998: 3.2 Animal Pharmacology/Toxicology
L999: The Pharm/Tox review was conducted by Dr. Barbara Wilcox, and will be filed separately. Under discussion during the course of the reviews has been the effect of drug stability on animal studies.

L1014: 4.3 Review Strategy
L1015: The efficacy review focused primarily on the two pivotal studies (Study 051 and Study 045) which were multi-center, randomized, placebo-controlled studies. Choice of dosage in the studies was well-established by previous Phase 1 and Phase 2 trials, and by the accumulated experience with Dysport in countries where it is already marked for the treatment of cervical dystonia. The other data sources for the review were evaluated for any evidence of confirmation or inconsistency with the findings of the pivotal studies. Information from the foreign labeling of Dysport was also reviewed, along with all available post-marketing safety data.
L1016: Statistical analyses were evaluated within the efficacy and safety reviews, and also by a separate statistical review by Dr. Ohidul Siddiqui. Dr. Marc Stone reviewed the safety data for this BLA, and a separate report will be filed. Separate reviews were also done by Dr. Barbara Wilcox for PharmTox, and Dr. Sharon Kirscher for CMC. A Clinical Pharmacology review was not applicable for this drug.
L1017: 4.4 Data Quality and Integrity
L1018: Division of Scientific Investigations (DSI) consult information (audit processes and report) is pending for the two Moscow sites used in Study 051. An audit of two of the Arizona sites used in Study 045 did not indicate any problems that would cause concern for the review.
L1019: Study 051 included 4 Russian sites, 2 in St. Petersburg, and in Moscow. These sites represented a relatively large number/percentage of subjects that these sites contributed to Study 051. The 4 Russian sites together contributed 28/116 (24.1%) of the enrolled subjects, and 28/83 (33.7%) of the subjects that completed Study 051.
L1020: 4.5 Compliance with Good Clinical Practices
L1021: The clinical trial was monitored by a third party, a Contract Research Organization.
L1022: 4.6 Financial Disclosures
L1023: In accordance with 21 CFR § 54.4(a)(3)(i)-(iv) the BLA submission included information on the financial disclosure and certification of the clinical investigators. One clinical investigator (for study Y-97-52120-096) disclosed a financial interest consisting of a consulting agreement in 2004 and 2005 with Inamed Corporation (Ipsen's clinical development partner for Dysport for treatment of glabellar lines (BBIND# 10673). Allergan (Irvine, CA) acquired Inamed Corporation (Santa Barbara, CA) in March, 2006. No other irregularities were disclosed.
L1024: 5 CLINICAL PHARMACOLOGY
L1025: 5.1 Pharmacokinetics/Pharmacodynamics/Exposure-Response Relationships
L1026: According to the clinical pharmacology review of Dr. Vaneeta Tandon, botulinum toxin type A at recommended dosages is not expected to be present in the peripheral blood at measurable levels. The application does not include pharmacokinetic or other clinical pharmacology data as there are no valid methods for measuring this product at levels in the blood that are not highly toxic.
L1027: 6 INTEGRATED REVIEW OF EFFICACY
L1028: 6.1 Indication: Treatment of Cervical Dystonia in adults
L1029: 6.1.1 Methods
L1030: The efficacy review focused primarily on the two pivotal trials, Y-47-52120-051(Study 051, conducted in the US and Russia) and Y-97-52120-045 (Study 045, conducted in the US).
L1031: Each of the pivotal trials had an open-label extension study, however the sponsor states that efficacy data from the open-label extension trials is available only for Study 045b. The data available from the Study 045b was reviewed.
L1032: The Phase 2 Study 020 for dose-ranging with treatment naïve subjects also provided some efficacy data. It was not conducted to provide efficacy data, but was included in the review because of the data provided on treatment-naïve subjects. Post-marketing information from annual reports was scanned for efficacy, with follow-up as needed, but generally provided only safety data.
L1033: Review of literature was included in the efficacy review, especially focused on studies that provided information on head-to-head studies with other botulinum toxins.
L1034: 6.1.2 General Discussion of Endpoints
L1035: The primary endpoint for both Study 051 and Study 045 was the change from baseline at Week 4 evaluation on the TWSTRS Total score.
L1036: In Study 051, secondary endpoints included the following:
L1037: • Change from baseline in TWSTRS Total scores at Weeks 8 and 12• Subject and investigator visual analog scale (VAS) Symptom Assessment at Weeks 4, 8, and 12 -• Short form 36 (SF-36) QoL (Quality of Life) scores at Week 8• Proportion of subjects considered by investigator to be overall treatment successes at Week 12
L1038: Exploratory (Tertiary) endpoints included:
L1039: • Change from baseline in subject VAS for pain at Weeks 4, 8, and 12• Change from baseline in TWSTRS subscale scores(severity, disability and pain) at Weeks 4, 8 and 12• Change in subject VAS for pain in the subgroup with baseline scores &gt; 40 mm• Proportion of responders (defined as decrease in TWSTRS total scores \geq 30\%) at Weeks 4, 8, and 12• Time to retreatment
L1040: In Study 051, a hierarchy was assigned to determine the level of significance of the endpoints. The endpoints were tested sequentially for statistical significance at the 5% level. No claims of significance were made for results ranked lower than the first nonsignificant result.
L1041: In Study 45, the secondary endpoints included change from baseline to Week 4 in:
L1042: • TWSTRS Total score for the PP (per protocol) population• Subject VAS for pain• Subject VAS for symptoms• Investigator VAS symptoms
L1043: Tertiary endpoints included:
L1044: • Change from baseline in TWSTRS Total score at Weeks 8 and 12, and TWSTRS subscale scores (severity, disability, pain) at Weeks 4, 8, and 12• Subject VAS for pain at Weeks 8 and 12• Proportion of “responders” at Weeks 4, 8, and 12 (defined as decrease in TWSTRS Total score \geq 30\% and at least 10 points when compared to baseline score)• Duration of response (time to retreatment eligibility defined as a return of the TWSTRS Total score to within 10% of baseline)• Proportion of subjects reporting an improvement in VAS symptoms (of \geq 50\% at Week 4, improvement without specified % at Weeks 8 and 12)• Investigator report of subject improvement in VAS symptoms (of \geq 50\% at Week 4, improvement without specified % at Weeks 8 and 12)• Proportion of subjects considered, on completion of the double-blind phase, to be “overall treatment success” (defined as a Global Efficacy Assessment of at least moderate improvement and a Global Safety Assessment of no worse than moderate)
L1045: The use of the TWSTRS Total score and subscale scores, the subject and investigator VAS (pain and symptoms), and the SF-36 mental and physical QoL scores were all appropriate measurement with a history of previous use in cervical dystonia studies. The endpoints selected using these assessments were also appropriate for the trials. As a result, the assessment of benefit assigned to the results of the studies should be considered valid for the Week 4 primary, secondary and exploratory/tertiary assessments. The ability to make generalizations on the endpoints at the Week 8 and Week 12 assessments is compromised by the early exit of subjects (anytime after the Week 4 visit)
L1046: Study 020 was not designed as an efficacy trial. It was a dose-ranging Phase 2 controlled trial for treatment naïve CD subjects conducted in Germany. Efficacy data was collected using the Tsui scale for the assessment of cervical dystonia.
L1047: 6.1.3 Study Design
L1048: The study designs of the two pivotal studies were very similar in every regard. Both were double-blind, multi-center, placebo-controlled, parallel group (\sim 1:1 ratio) trials with
L1049: very similar inclusion/exclusion criteria. There was a washout period of 16 weeks for symptoms to return to baseline if subjects had previously received botulinum toxin for CD. Following baseline assessments and single dose cervical injections with the assigned treatment, follow-up assessments for efficacy were done at Weeks 4, 8, and 12. Among the slight differences between studies, in Study 051, one additional muscle was included on the the list of possible injection sites, and there were slight difference in the secondary and exploratory/tertiary endpoint (mainly in prioritizing). For the botulinum toxin injections, there was inclusion of both treatment naïve and previously treated CD subjects. Quality of Life and “treatment success” assessments were completed by both subjects and investigators. Blinding precautions, data collection and storage arrangements were adequate. Injections could be done with or without EMG guidance, at the discretion of the investigator, but no distinction in data analysis was make based on EMG use.
L1050: The proposed 12 week pivotal studies would have been of adequate duration, however there was loss of data with the early exit of many of the subjects. They were allowed to transfer to the open-label, variable dosing schedule extension studies after the Week 4 assessments. This occurred more frequently in Study 045 (prior to discussions on protocol design with the FDA), but was a factor in both studies. It did not compromise the statistical analysis of the Week 4 data, and despite the “early exit” limitation, the design still manages to provide a reasonable assessment of benefit.
L1051: Study 045b was an open-label study in which the subjects from Study 045 could receive an additional 3 injections of Dysport. The initial treatment was at the 500 U fixed dose, but the subsequent injections were of variable dose and variable intervals (\geq 12 weeks) between treatments at the discretion of the investigator and the subject's symptoms. A study visit was set for Week 4 after each injection treatment. Subjects were then eligible to enroll in the extended open-label study. An open-label extension study (Study 731) without the possible 3 additional injections transition followed Study 05, but did not have data available for review. The open-label studies are given less significance in the review process, but do provide useful information on typical dosing and duration of effectiveness in the general clinical setting.
L1052: 6.1.4 Efficacy Findings
L1053: The primary endpoint for both pivotal studies (Study 051 and Study 045) was the change from baseline in the TWSTRS Total Score at Week 4 for Dysport compared to placebo. All efficacy analyses were conducted on the ITT (intent to treat) population for the Dysport and placebo groups, as stated in the protocols. Analysis of the primary efficacy endpoint was also performed on the PP (per protocol) population to evaluate whether the exclusions might have influenced the outcome. The analyses showed that there were no statistical differences found using the ITT or PP population for the primary endpoint, so only the ITT population was used for the analyses of the secondary and tertiary/exploratory endpoints.
L1054: Study 051 -
L1055: In Study 051, the efficacy endpoints were ranked by the primary endpoint, then secondary endpoints in the pre-determined order of priority, and tested sequentially for statistical significance at the 5% level. This ranking was agreed to in the meeting with the FDA in December, 2006. Tertiary/exploratory endpoints were required only to have descriptive results and confidence interval (CI) information provided for review.
L1056: The primary endpoint for Study 051, the change from baseline in the TWSTRS Total Score at Week 4, is statistically significant for the Dysport group compared to the placebo group (see Table 5). The Dysport group (n=51) had a mean change from baseline of -13.995, and the placebo group (n=58) had a mean change of -5.228 (p&lt;&lt;0.0001). The p-value when analyzed by baseline score was p=0.015, by previous botulinum treatment p=0.405, and by center p=0.130.
L1057: Using the PP population, Dysport group (n=41) had a mean change from baseline of -15.594, and the placebo group (n=50) had a mean change of -5.025 (p&lt;&lt;0.0001). For the PP population, the p-value when analyzed by baseline score was p=0.009, by previous botulinum treatment p=0.657, and by center p=0.169.
L1058: The secondary efficacy endpoints were tested on the ITT population only. Last observation carried forward (LOCF) values were used for missing values.

L1063: Source: Study 051
L1064: As shown in Table 5, the study reached statistical significance in the primary endpoint and most of the secondary endpoints. Due to the hierarchy system of evaluation, endpoints 9a to 10 were not considered statistically significant, but were supportive of the other results.
L1065: The proportion of subjects considered treatment success was designated as a secondary endpoint (#10). Overall treatment success was defined by a global efficacy assessment of “better” or “much better”, and a global safety assessment of no worse than “moderate” using an odds ratio. The investigator’s global assessment of overall treatment success at Week 12 (Table 17) was higher in the Dysport group (32/55 subjects, 58%) than in the placebo group (10/61 subjects, 16%). The odds ratio for overall treatment success rate in the Dysport group compared to placebo was 7.206 (95% CI: 3.009, 17.260, p&lt;0.0001). As noted, due to the hierarchical structure used in the analyses, since the score could not be considered as statistically significant for the study, but does have the value of providing an easily visualized percentage for subjects considered to have benefitted from the treatment. See Table 6.

L1069: Study 045 -
L1070: Study 045 was completed prior to the sponsor’s meeting with the FDA. It did not utilize a hierarchical ranking system. Another difference from Study 051 was the higher “early exit” rate after the Week 4 evaluations.
L1071: Study 045 was already underway (with 57 subjects, 29 received Dysport) when a batch of the treatment drug (Batch 159J) was found to have fallen below the potency requirements. After discussions with the FDA, the sponsor extended the enrollment (to 136 subjects) to assure an adequate number of subjects for the planned statistical analyses. The main data analyses for Study 05 depended on the Group 1 subjects (80, 37 Dysport=37, and placebo=43) who were all enrolled later, and did not receive Batch 159J. The sponsor also included the analyses of the Group 3 subjects, who received treatment before the Batch 159J discovery, and the Group 2 (which included all subjects,
L1072: both Groups 1 and 3). Statistical analyses did not show significant differences between the groups. Group 1 had about the same rate of improvement with Dysport treatment as did Group 1.
L1073: The change from baseline in mean TWSTRS-Total score at week 4 in Group 1 was the primary efficacy analysis. Mean (SD) TWSTRS-Total score at baseline was 45.1 (\pm 8.7) in the Dysport group and 46.2 (\pm 9.4) in the placebo group. Mean TWSTRS Total score at week 4 improved \sim 9.6 points from baseline in the Dysport group compared with \sim 3.7 points the placebo group, so it reached statistical significance (p = 0.013). (Table 7.)

L1076: Data presented as mean (SD) with [median] and {range}; Change from baseline presented as adjusted mean (SE); Missing data presented as number of missing variables
L1077: Source of data: Modified from Study 045
L1078: Of significance, especially in Study 045, is the number missing data (see Table 7) at the Week 8 and 12 assessments. At the Week 12 visit, 23/37 (62%) of the Dysport group, and 35/43 (81%) of the placebo group had already left the study; nearly all exited to the open-label study (Study 045b). The sponsor acknowledged that “This lead to some concerns about the homogeneity of the variance and the validity of the model.” The analyses were performed on the ITT population using the patient’s own baseline value for missing data. Analyses using different methods of imputing missing data (using a 5-point increase from baseline, and with no replacement of missing data) resulted in about the same statistical conclusion, but it is a marked limitation on interpretation of the Week 8 and Week 12 endpoints data, and limits the conclusions and generalizations that can be attributed to them.
L1079: There were also some inconsistencies within the analyses of groups, for example, with the TWSTRS scores at weeks 8 and 12, all the sensitivity analyses concluded that there was a statistically significant difference between Dysport and placebo in Group 2; however, they did not achieve statistical significance in Group 1. Due to the general consistency of the findings, the few inconsistencies are relatively minor.
L1080: Table 8 shows the full list of the endpoints for Study 045. As previously noted, there was not a hierarchy of ranking for the analysis or the secondary and tertiary endpoints. The majority of the endpoints favored Dysport treatment over placebo. Since the Week 8 and Week 12 results have limited interpretation value, of interest here is the consistent strength of the Week 4 assessments in favor of Dysport treatment compared to placebo.

L1084: Source: Clinical Overview Report
L1085: Study Responders
L1086: In Study 045, the proportion of treatment responders at weeks 4, 8, and 12 was a tertiary efficacy analysis. Patients were classified as responders if the decrease in TWSTRS-Total score was &gt; 30%, and at least 10 points, compared with the baseline score. All other patients were classified as non-responders. The proportion of responders in the
L1087: DYSPORT group was higher than in the placebo group at Week 4 (38% versus 16%). At Week 8, it was 27% versus 9%, and at Week 12, 19% versus 7%, respectively.
L1088: At week 4, 14 Dysport patients and 7 placebo patients were classified as responders (not yet eligible for retreatment). The mean (SD) duration of response for DYSPORT responders was 22.8 (\pm 12.5) weeks compared with 18.8 (\pm 8.8) weeks for placebo responders. Accordingly, at Week 4, 30/43 of the placebo subjects compared to 25/37 of the Dysport subjects were eligible for retreatment. Most of the placebo “eligibles” (when TWSTRS-Total score returns to within 10% of baseline) sought retreatment (26/30, 87%), but only ~ half (12/25, 48%) of the eligible Dysport subjects requested retreatment, so the responder definition may have been set rather too high and missed some levels of efficacy. In general (irregardless of the responder criteria), in the placebo group, about half improved (24/43, 56%), and the other half (19/43, 44%) did not change or worsened. In the Dysport group, 29/37, 78% showed improvement in Dysport group, 3/37 with worsening.
L1089: Overall Assessment of Drug Effectiveness
L1090: The global assessment of efficacy and safety was performed by the investigator on the last visit. Overall treatment success was defined as: “a global efficacy assessment of at least moderate improvement and a global safety assessment of no worse than moderate in regards to adverse events”. If patients who did not fit this definition, they were classified as treatment failures. More Dysport patients (13/37, 35%) than placebo patients (10/43, 23%) were considered treatment successes. Of these, 3/13 (23%), 5/13 (39%), and 5/13 (39%) Dysport patients made excellent, good, and moderate improvements in cervical dystonia, respectively, compared with 0/10, 4/10 (40%), and 6/10 (60%) placebo patients, respectively.
L1091: Duration of Effect
L1092: The data from Study 051 is more reliable than Study 045 for duration of effect since the “early exit” rate was lower. Figure 1 shows the change in TWSTRS Total score for the study by visit. Notable is that Week 4 seems to represent the maximum benefits, and by Week 12 there is already significant return in scores toward baseline (although still statistically significant in the study).
