    Study Flow Chart – Study 045. A flowchart detailing the progression of participants through a clinical study. It starts at Week 0 with Screening/Eligibility Criteria, Stratified Randomization, Baseline Assessments, and Study Medication Administration. At Week 2, there is a Selective Contact Safety Assessment. At Week 4, an Efficacy/Safety Assessment leads to two groups: Responders (TWSTRS-Total Score1 Decrease &gt; 10%) and Non-responders (TWSTRS-Total Score1 Decrease &lt; 10%). Responders proceed to Week 8, while non-responders enter a second double-blind phase followed by an open retreatment with DYSPORT (500 units)2. At Week 8, responders are assessed for 'Response Ongoing' or 'Response Lost'. Those with an ongoing response may be indicated for retreatment and enter a second double-blind phase. Those with a lost response also enter a second double-blind phase. At Week 12, another Efficacy/Safety Assessment is performed. Similar to Week 8, 'Response Ongoing' leads to potential retreatment and a second double-blind phase, while 'Response Lost' leads directly to a second double-blind phase. All participants eventually reach a Follow-up Visit (Retreatment Discussed), followed by a final Efficacy/Safety Assessment and a final End Double-Blind Phase.
L1526: s
L1527: 1Change from baseline.
L1528: 2Retreatment was to be delayed until week 8 whenever possible, or in the case of any study drug-related adverse event, until the event had fully resolved.
L1529: Source: Study Y-97-52120-045
L1530: 10.1.1.3.5 Efficacy parameters
L1531: Primary Efficacy Variable
L1532: Change from baseline to Week 4 in TWSTRS-Total score.
L1533: Secondary Efficacy Variables
L1534: • Change from baseline in TWSTRS-Total score at week 4 (per-protocol population)• Patient visual analogue pain scale at week 4• Patient assessment of change in the signs and symptoms of cervical dystonia at week 4
L1535: Tertiary Efficacy Variables
L1536: • Change from baseline in TWSTRS-Total score at weeks 8 and 12, and TWSTRS Subscale (severity, disability, pain) scores at weeks 4, 8, and 12• Patient visual analogue pain scale at weeks 8 and 12• Proportion of responders at weeks 4, 8, and 12 (Patients were classified as responders if the decrease in TWSTRS-Total score was \geq 30\% and at least 10 points compared with the baseline score.)• Duration of response (time to retreatment eligibility), defined as the time between administration of study medication and the need for retreatment (as determined by a return of the TWSTRS-Total score to within 10% of baseline)• Proportion of patients reporting an improvement in signs and symptoms of cervical dystonia of at least 50% at week 4 compared to baseline• Proportion of patients for whom investigators report an improvement in signs and symptoms of cervical dystonia of at least 50% at week 4 compared to baseline• Patient assessment of change in signs and symptoms of cervical dystonia at weeks 8 and 12• Investigator assessment of change in signs and symptoms of cervical dystonia at weeks 4, 8, and 12• Proportion of patients considered, on completion of the double-blind phase, to be overall treatment successes, as defined by a Global Efficacy Assessment of at least moderate improvement and a Global Safety Assessment of no worse than moderate
L1537: Safety Variables
L1538: • Incidence of treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs)• Neutralizing antibody status
L1539: 10.1.1.3.6 Statistical analysis
L1540: Statistical methods
L1541: The efficacy variables were to be assessed using the intent-to-treat (ITT) population, with a supportive analysis based on the per protocol (PP) population for the primary endpoint. Safety assessments were to be based on the safety population (all patients who received the study drug).
L1542: Group 1 was used as the primary efficacy analysis on the ITT population. Efficacy analyses were also performed on the ITT population of Group 2, and Group 3. A
L1543: supportive secondary analysis was done on the PP (per-protocol) population.
L1544: Safety analyses were performed on the safety population for Groups 1 and 2. The primary safety analysis was performed on the safety population for Group 2.
L1545: All statistical testing was 2-sided and performed using a 5% significance level. ANCOVA was performed on the primary efficacy endpoint. A model consisting of treatment, center, strata (previous or no previous treatment with botulinum toxin), and baseline TWSTRS-Total score was fitted to the data. Baseline TWSTRS-Total score was considered as a covariate. All covariates remained in the final model, regardless of significance. The primary comparison was between the DYSPORT and placebo groups. The primary and secondary efficacy variables were to be analyzed using an analysis of covariance, except for the assessment of treatment successes which was to use a logistics model.
L1546: Exploratory efficacy and safety variables were to be summarized by descriptive statistics, treatment differences with 95% confidence intervals, and shift tables.
L1547: No interim analysis was planned.
L1548: Determination of sample size
L1549: Initial estimates had been for a total of 60 subjects (30 per treatment group) for the primary efficacy endpoint. That would allow for a &gt;90% chance of showing a significant difference at a 2-sided significance level (p = 0.05) if the true difference in TWSTRS-Total score was 9 point, assuming that the standard deviation (SD) of the difference in TWSTRS-Total score from baseline was 10 points (the SD found by Lew et al). (*) Recruiting started with a goal of 80 subjects which would allow for drop-outs.
L1550: When batch 159J was discovered to have fallen below the minimum potency specifications, 57 subjects had already entered the study (as of October, 16, 1998). After discussion with the FDA, it was decided that an additional 80 subjects should be recruited which brought the total to 137 subjects.
L1551: 10.1.1.3.8 Changes to the planned statistical analysis
L1552: Following approval of the RAP (dated May 9, 2001), a decision was made (prior to database lock) not to analyze analgesic use. The information was recorded in the concomitant medication section of the CRF. Also, the value for the lower range for sodium was changed from &lt;132, the value reported in the analysis plan, to &lt;135, the value reported in the database.
L1553: 10.1.1.4 Study Results
L1554: 10.1.1.4.1 Trial characteristics
L1555: The trial design is quite similar to the design discussed with Study 051. One of the chief differences is that from week 4 onwards, patients not responding to blinded phase treatment (\geq 10\% decrease in TWSTRS-Total score compared with baseline) were
L1556: considered eligible for retreatment, providing any study drug-related adverse events (if applicable) were resolved. Another difference was the sites selected. Study 045 was conducted solely in the US (13 sites).
L1557: 10.1.1.4.2 Demographics
L1558: The analysis plan identified four patient populations:
L1559: • All Subjects Population: included all subjects who consented to participate in the study and underwent screening assessments.• Intention-to-Treat (ITT) Population: included all subjects randomized to receive study medication• Per-Protocol Population: all patients in the ITT population excluding major protocol violators• Safety Population: all patients who received study medication (Additionally, the open phase safety sub-population presented in this report comprised all patients who received open-label DYSPORT at week 4 of the double-blind phase.)
L1560: Baseline Demographic Characteristics
L1561: The baseline demographic characteristics from Study 045, shown in Table 3, are consistent with the findings of previous studies. The subject population showed a preponderance of females (~ 65%) to males. The mean age of the subjects was ~ 55 years, however that does not give the age at onset of symptoms. The time since onset of symptoms of CD for the subjects was ~ 10 years making the mean age at onset about a decade younger than the mean subject age in the study, and at the younger edge of the typical onset age reported in previous studies. The age range of the Study 045 subjects (mid-20s to late 70s) also shows subjects in their 20s, a younger than “usual” age for CD subjects. The percentage of non-Caucasians was underrepresented in the study.
L1562: Table 31, in addition to showing the subject age at onset of symptoms, also shows the information on previous exposure to botulinum toxin treatment for CD. For the primary endpoint study group, Group 1, 24% of the Dysport treatment subjects had not previously received botulinum injections. This percentage was similar to the percentage in the placebo group (28%).
L1563: The various groups were reasonably well dispersed by the study randomization in terms of both demographic characteristics and previous treatment characteristics. None of the differences between groups was disproportionate enough to engender concerns with skewing of the study comparisons.

L1578: Baseline Medical Comparison
L1579: Prior and Concomitant Medication
L1580: Table 32 shows the use of concomitant medications at entry onto the study. Analgesics and antiinflammatories both represent frequently used medications; about a third of the subjects use one type or both. The use of muscle relaxants by the subjects is quite rather low, only 11% overall (15/ 136 total) and the most disproportionate between groups (20% Dysport, 3% placebo). Most frequently listed is the use of centrally-acting drugs, although whether used off-label to aid in control of dystonia symptoms, or for management of secondary (or unrelated) symptoms, such as depression cannot be ascertained from the data collection. These include the antiepileptics, the antipsychotics, the antidepressants and the Parkinson's medications, all of which are used with greater frequency than the muscle relaxants suggesting that muscle relaxants do not tend to work on the muscular spasms of dystonia.

L1585: Labs
L1586: There was no significant difference in baseline lab values between groups.
L1587: Neutralizing antibodies were detected at baseline for 9% of the subjects in both the treatment and placebo groups (6/66 Dysport subjects, 6/70 placebo), and in no de novo subjects.
L1588: Subject Disposition
L1589: The patient disposition for Study 045 is shown in Table 33. The study screened 156 patients, and randomized 137 subjects (19 patients did not meet inclusion criteria). The study started with 136 subjects because one patient was enrolled directly to the open-label phase due to an error (receiving the open-label study medication instead of double-blind study medication), and not included in the ITT analyses. The subjects were randomized to 66 on Dysport, 70 on placebo as the total for Group 2 (all subjects).
L1590: Group 1 is the eighty (80) subjects (37 Dysport, 43 placebo) enrolled after October 16, 1998 who received Dysport batches other than 159J. Group 1 data was used for the analysis of the primary endpoint of Study 045. Group 2 is all subjects regardless of Dysport batch received (66 Dysport, 70 placebo). Group 3 is the fifty-six (56) patients (29 Dysport, 27 placebo) who were treated with batch 159J.

L1599: Discussion of subject disposition is confusing for Study 045. The sponsor considered all subjects who completed the Week 4 assessments as having “completed the double-blind phase”, even though it was a 12 week follow- up with additional assessments scheduled for Week 8 and Week 12. Subjects were allowed to exit the study and were enrolled in the extension study (Study 045b) any time after the Week 4 assessments. As a result, the sponsor considers the study completion rate as ~ 99% in Group 1 and Group 2, and 100% on Group 3. One subject received the injections, but withdrew before the Week 4 assessments; and was analyzed as a ‘treatment failure’ in the analyses of Group 1 and 2. The reason for withdrawal was listed as “unable to return for subsequent visits”.
L1600: 10.2.1.4.3 Protocol amendments and violations
L1601: The protocol was amended twice. The protocol and all amendments are provided in Appendix 16.1.1.
L1602: Changes in the first amendment (April 08, 1998) included:
L1603: • A name change from Speywood Pharmaceuticals to Ipsen Ltd., as part of the continued integration with Beaufour-Ipsen, the parent company;
• Assignment of the BB-IND number;
• Increasing the amount of blood drawn for antibody assays;
• Modifying the visual analogue scale to include a “no change from baseline” measurement;
• Clarification of the procedure for assessing patients who did not enter the open-label phase on the same day as the last double-blind visit;
• Clarification that the ITT analysis would be the primary analysis for all endpoints;
• Adjustments to study timelines.
L1604: Changes in the second amendment (November 27, 1998) included:
L1605: • Amendments to address issues raised by the discovery that DYSPORT batch 159J had fallen below the minimum potency specification, including the restarting of recruitment;
• Allowing patients to be continued in the extended-use program;
• FDA recommendations to make the global assessment of efficacy and safety a tertiary, rather than secondary objective;
• Prohibiting minor violations of entry criteria.
L1606: Table 35 shows the major protocol deviations. In total, there were 17 reported (in 15 subjects) in the DYSPORT group, and 14 (in 12 subjects) in the placebo group.
L1607: The most common major protocol deviation was initiation of or change in concomitant medication(s) for CD from 6 weeks before study entry up to the week 4 assessments (10 Dysport, 8 placebo). The most common minor protocol deviations were assessments performed outside the scheduled visit window 44 (67%) Dysport, and 38 (54%) placebo visits.

L1610: Source of data: Study Y-97-52120-045
L1611: Dosage levels
L1612: The study drug, Dysport, was administered at 500 U intramuscularly divided among two to four muscles to subjects in the Dysport group. An injection of the placebo was administered in the same volume and the same method to the subjects assigned to the placebo group.
L1613: Drug plasma concentrations are not relevant as the toxin is not measurable except by its bioactivity, so testing was not done.
L1614: 10.1.1.4.4 Efficacy endpoints
L1615: The efficacy endpoints will be discussed by type of assessment rather than a strict adherence to the order (primary, secondary, or tertiary). The rationale is that the assessment types are easier and more logical to evaluate looking at results from all the study visits at the same time. Some of the results may have been designated as different objectives for the same assessment than those of subsequent visits.
L1616: Evaluation of the primary objective
L1617: The change from baseline in mean TWSTRS-Total score at week 4 in Group 1 was the primary efficacy analysis. Mean (SD) TWSTRS-Total score at baseline was 45.1 (\pm 8.7) in the Dysport group and 46.2 (\pm 9.4) in the placebo group. Mean (SD) TWSTRS Total score at week 4 improved 9.9 (adjusted mean 9.6) points from baseline to 35.2 (\pm 13.8) in the Dysport group compared with 3.8 (adjusted mean 3.7) points
L1618: from baseline to 42.4 (\pm 12.2) in the placebo group (p = 0.013) (Table 36). The results for the Dysport group reached a level of statistical significance for Week (the primary endpoint) as well as for Weeks 8 and 12 (a tertiary endpoint).

L1622: Source of data: Study Y-97-52120-045
L1623: The above analyses were performed on the ITT population using the patient's own baseline value for missing data. Analyses using different methods of imputing missing data (using a 5-point increase from baseline and with no replacement of missing data lead to the same conclusion. Patients could leave the study at any time after the Week 4 assessments. Consequently, by week 12, 23/37 (62%) of DYSPORT patients and 35/43 (81%) of placebo patients had missing data because they had left the double-blind phase.
L1624: The sponsor concedes the missing data due to early exits lead to “some concerns about the homogeneity of the variance and the validity of the model”. For that reason, other statistical analyses were applied to the TWSTRS-Total score data to see if there was consistency in the results. These are shown in Table 37, and include sensitivity analyses for the change with missing data imputed using the patient's own baseline value using a ranked analysis of covariance, and also the non-parametric Wilcoxon Rank Sum Test. The alternative sensitivity analyses on the primary endpoint provided similar results. For the TWSTRS scores at Week 8 and Week 12, all the sensitivity analyses concluded that
L1625: there was a statistically significant difference between DYSPORT and placebo in Group 2; however, they did not achieve statistical significance in Group 1.

L1629: Evaluation of the secondary and tertiary endpoints
L1630: The secondary endpoints included:
L1631: • Change from baseline in TWSTRS Total score at Week 4 for the per protocol (PP) population
• Subject assessment of change in the signs and symptoms (VAS – Symptoms) of cervical dystonia at Week 4
• Subject visual analogue pain scale (VAS – Pain) at Week 4
L1632: The sponsor evaluated the primary endpoint (change from baseline in TWSTRS Total score at Week 4 in Group 1) using the PP (per protocol) population as a secondary efficacy variable. A total of 111 patients (53 Dysport, 58 placebo) were included in the per-protocol population, with 65 in Group 1 and 46 in Group 3 (received Batch 159J). Of the 65 PP population subjects in Group 1, 29 were in the Dysport group, and 36 in the placebo group. Table 38 shows that analysis of the PP population instead of the ITT population for the primary study endpoint still showed statistical significance for Dysport group compared to the placebo group.

L1636: Subject Rating of Change in the Signs and Symptoms of Cervical Dystonia (VAS–Symptoms)
L1637: The patient's assessment of change in the signs and symptoms of cervical dystonia at week 4 was a secondary efficacy analysis (assessed using a 100 mm visual analogue scale with the center designated as no change from baseline, 0 mm = worst/most symptoms, 100 mm = improved/least symptoms). At week 4, the patient's assessment of change in the signs and symptoms of cervical dystonia was more toward improvement in the Dysport group compared to the placebo group. The difference in points between the groups was 16.4 mm, with the Dysport group reporting an improvement of 15 mm and the placebo group reporting no significant change from baseline (1.4 mm worse). The difference reached statistical significance (p = 0.001) (Table 39).
L1638: The patient's assessment of change in the signs and symptoms of cervical dystonia at weeks 8 and 12 was a tertiary efficacy endpoint. The Dysport group showed an improvement of 9.8 mm and 1.9 mm in Weeks 8 and 12 respectively, compared to a worsening of 3.6mm and 6.4 mm respectively reported by the placebo group. The differences reached statistically significant levels for both Week 8 (p = 0.002), and Week 12 (p= 0.022). However, it is to be noted that the Week 12 score change for the Dysport group was nearly back to the level of symptoms subjects considered their baseline, and was statistically significant only because the placebo group score an increased worsening of symptoms (or, more likely, it was affected by the attribution of assigned values for missing data disproportionately between the groups).

L1646: The investigator’s assessment of change in the signs and symptoms of cervical dystonia (assessed using a visual analogue scale with the center being no change from baseline) at weeks 4, 8, and 12 was a tertiary efficacy analysis. The scoring and the analysis of the data is the same as for the subjects’ VAS – Symptoms. The investigator assessment was more towards symptom free in the Dysport group compared with the placebo group at weeks 4, 8, and 12 (Table 40). Again, as in the subject VAS – Symptoms, the Week 12 score of the Dysport group is notable for the low score (only 1.2 mm from center) which rates the symptoms back to nearly baseline. The low Week 12 Dysport score has CI significance only because of the large change in the placebo group, but both scores are probably due to the distorting effect of assigned values for missing data, and there is no reliability except for the Week 4 scores.

L1652: Subject Visual Analogue Pain Scale (VAS– Pain)
L1653: The subject visual analogue pain scale score at Week 4 was a secondary efficacy endpoint, and the scores at Week 8 and Week 12 was a tertiary endpoint analysis. The subject’s VAS-Pain was presented as a simple 100 mm line with 0 mm representing “no pain” and 100 mm representing “worst pain possible”. The subject was instructed to mark a point that corresponded to their pain level.
L1654: As shown in Table *, at week 4, the change from baseline in the mean patient visual analogue pain scale score in the DYSPORT group improved more than the score in the placebo group. The change from baseline was 13.4 mm decrease (improvement) for the Dysport group compared to a 1.9 mm decrease for the placebo group (p = 0.024).
L1655: Patient visual analogue pain scale score at Weeks 8 and 12 was a tertiary efficacy analysis. At Weeks 8 and 12, the pain scale score in the DYSPORT group decreased by 7.4 and 3.1 mm respectively compared to the placebo group whose pain score stayed close to baseline. The level of change in the pain scores reached statistical significance for the Dysport group at Week 4 and 8, but not at Week 12.
L1656: The analyses in Table 41 were performed on the ITT population using the patient’s own baseline value for missing data. Analyses using different methods of imputing missing data (using a 5 mm increase from baseline, and with no replacement of missing data) showed no significant change in the results obtained. Again, the same caveats on the statistical analysis of the Week 8 and Week 12 conclusions apply.

L1661: Patient and Investigator Reports of Improvement of at least 50% at Week 4
L1662: The patient and investigator reports of improvement of at least 50% at week 4 was a tertiary efficacy analysis. An improvement \geq 50\% in the signs and symptoms of cervical dystonia, as rated by the VAS – Symptoms scoring, was assessed more often in the Dysport group than in the placebo group by both the patient and the investigator (Table 42). For both the subjects and investigator scoring, 14/37 (38%) of the Group 1 subjects were judged to have achieved at least a 50% or better reduction in CD symptoms compared to 16% (subject rating) and 12% (investigator rating) in the placebo group.

L1667: Change from Baseline in TWSTRS Subscale Scores (Severity, Disability, Pain)
L1668: The change from baseline in TWSTRS Sub-scale score for severity, disability and pain at Weeks 4, 8, and 12 was a tertiary efficacy analysis (Table 43). The TWSTRS Severity scale had a possible maximum of 35 points, the TWSTRS Disability scale had a max. = 30, and the TWSTRS Pain scale had a max. = 20 points. A decrease from baseline reflects improvement in the scores. At the Week 4 assessments, the TWSTRS Severity score improved in the Dysport group by 4.6 point, compared to 2.1 points in the placebo group. The TWSTRS Disability score improved 2.5 points, compared to 0.6 points in the placebo group, and the TWSTRS Pain score improved 2.8 points, compared to 1.2 points in the placebo group. Evaluation of the subscales shows that the changes in the TWSTRS Total score was composed of rated improvement in all the subscores fairly equally, with the Disability rating contributing slightly more in terms of a larger gain compared to the placebo group.

L1674: Proportion of Responders at Weeks 4, 8, 12
L1675: The proportion of treatment responders at weeks 4, 8, and 12 was a tertiary efficacy analysis. Patients were classified as responders if the decrease in TWSTRS-Total score was \geq 30\%, and at least 10 points compared with the baseline score. All other patients were classified as non-responders. The proportion of responders in the Dysport group was higher than in the placebo group at Week 4 (38% vs. 16%), Week 8 (27% vs. 9%), and Week 12 (19% vs. 7%) (Table 44). The proportion of responders in the Dysport group at Week 4 (the most reliable data) was more than double the proportion in the placebo group (38% vs. 16%), and probably provides the most clear-cut indication of the percentage of CD target population that would be expected to show quite significant improvement, based solely on efficacy reports, with the use of Dysport.

L1680: Global Assessment of Efficacy and Safety/Proportion of Subjects Considered Treatment Successes
L1681: The global assessment of efficacy and safety was performed by the investigator on completion of the study (Week 12). Overall treatment success was defined as a global efficacy assessment of at least moderate improvement, and a global safety assessment of no worse than moderate in regards to adverse events. Patients who did not satisfy this definition were classified as treatment failures. The rating evaluates the efficacy gains with treatment against the adverse events encountered. More Dysport patients (13/37, 35%) than placebo patients (10/43, 23%) were considered treatment successes. Of these, 3/13 (23%), 5/13 (39%), and 5/13 (39%) Dysport patients made excellent, good, and moderate improvements in cervical dystonia, respectively, compared with 0/10, 4/10 (40%), and 6/10 (60%) placebo patients, respectively (Table 45).

L1686: Duration of Response (Time to Retreatment Eligibility)
L1687: The duration of response for patients classified as responders at week 4 was a tertiary efficacy analysis. By study definition, the duration of response is the time between administration of study medication, and eligibility for retreatment. Subjects (responders only) were considered eligible for retreatment if the change in TWSTRS-Total score returned to within 10% of baseline. At the Week 4 assessments, 14 Dysport patients, and 7 placebo patients, were classified as responders. The mean (SD) duration of response for Dysport responders was 22.8 (\pm 12.5) weeks versus 18.8 (\pm 8.8) weeks for placebo responders. The sponsor makes the point that 13/25 (52%) of the Dysport subjects eligible for retreatment at Week 4, did not seek retreatment, while only 4/30 (13%) of the eligible placebo subjects did not seek retreatment, suggesting that the Dysport subjects may have been receiving some benefit from treatment even if it did not reach the study required definition. The very small numbers of subjects remaining in the study make any conclusions suspect; by Week 16, only 4 subjects remained in the Dysport group eligible for retreatment.
L1688: Handling of Dropouts or Missing Data
L1689: Missing data at Weeks 4, 8 and 12 was imputed using the patient's own baseline value. Since the patient's and investigator's assessment of change in signs and symptoms of cervical dystonia, measured on a 100 mm scale, was not assessed at baseline, an alternative approach to handling missing data was used. The method of attribution of moderate worsening was implemented by imputing all missing data with a value of 40 mm on 100 mm scale.
L1690: Additionally, for the primary and secondary efficacy parameters only (change in TWSTRS total score, patient visual analogue pain scale and patient's assessment of change in signs and symptoms of cervical dystonia), as sensitivity analyses, the following methods of handling missing data were also used:
L1691: a) TWSTRS Total Score
L1692: • The method of attribution of moderate worsening was implemented by imputing all missing data with a value representing a 5 point increase from baseline• No imputation
L1693: b) Patient Visual Analogue Pain Scale
L1694: • The method of attribution of moderate worsening was implemented by imputing all missing data with a value representing a 5 mm increase from baseline (on 100 mm scale)• No imputation
L1695: c) Patient's Assessment of Change in Signs and Symptoms of Cervical Dystonia
L1696: • The method of attribution of no improvement was implemented by imputing all missing data with a value representing no improvement (50 mm on 100 mm scale)• No imputation
L1697: Missing data during the open phase was not imputed.
L1698: The results of the TWSTRS analyses at Week 4, for the Per-Protocol population, were identical when using the three different imputation methods since no patients
L1699: in this population were imputed at this week.
L1700: Changes in the Conduct of the Study or Planned Analyses
L1701: 10.1.1.4.5 Safety
L1702: Dr. Marc Stone conducted the safety review for BLA 125274, and the review will be filed separately.
L1703: Safety analyses used Group 2 (all subjects) for evaluation. Of the Dysport subjects, 59/66 (89%) reported TEAEs (313, 5.3/subject), compared to 53/70 (76%) of the placebo subjects (234, 4.4/subject). The reported TEAEs with an incidence of \geq 10\% in the Dysport group were: injection site pain (36%), neck/shoulder pain (29%), asthenia (26%), dry mouth (23%), headache (23%), myasthenia/neck (23%), dysphagia (21%), and voice alteration (11%). The proportion of TEAEs classified as mild, moderate, or severe was similar for the Dysport and placebo groups (mild 55% vs 50%, moderate 33% vs 35%, and severe 11% vs 14% respectively).
L1704: There were no deaths, and no withdrawals due to adverse events during the study (although one Dysport subject declined to enter the open-phase due to adverse events including dysphagia). Five Dysport subjects reported nine serious adverse events (SAEs), including five reports of dysphagia considered severe.
L1705: At baseline 6/66 (9%) Dysport subjects and 6/70 (9%) placebo subjects had neutralizing antibodies for botulinum toxin. At Week 12, 8/66 (12%) of the Dysport subjects were positive for neutralizing antibodies (and still 9% of placebo group), so 2 subjects had changed their antibody status.
L1706: 10.1.1.5 Reviewer's Summary
L1707: Efficacy Conclusions and Comments
L1708: Study 045 was problematic in the design which allowed an early exit rate that made conclusions based on the Week 8 and Week 12 assessments less reliable, but all the Week 4 assessments could be considered reliable. Based on the Week 4 assessments, Study 045 provides support for the BLA approval.
L1709: The Dysport and placebo groups were well-balanced in terms of medical backgrounds, concomitant medications, and other factors that had the potential to affect outcome. The statistical review, done by Dr. Ohidul Siddiqui, concurs with the appropriateness and adequacy of the study's statistical analyses. After the start of the trial, it was discovered that one batch of the study drug (batch 159J) was below potency requirements. By agreement with the FDA, the sponsor expanded the number of enrollees and evaluated
L1710: the data for Group 2 (all enrollees), Group 3 enrollees (subjects enrolled before Batch 159J was removed from the study), and Group 1 (subjects enrolled after Dysport batch 159J was removed from the study). Only Group 1 subjects were considered in the primary endpoint analysis.
L1711: The primary efficacy endpoint for this study, change from baseline in TWSTRS Total score at week 4, was statistically better in the Dysport group compared with the placebo group, and at the Week 4 assessments, the change can be considered reliable, and clinically significant. The mean change in the TWSTRS Total score (max. = 80 points) from baseline for the Dysport group was a decline (improvement) of 9.6 points compared to a decline of 3.7 points in the placebo group, with a p-value of 0.013. The statistical significance of the change was rechecked (as a secondary endpoint) by using the per protocol population instead of the ITT population, and gave the same result.
L1712: The design of the study allowed subjects to switch to the open-label extension study any time after the Week 4 assessments. This allowed any subjects receiving placebo to be retreated quickly while maintaining the study blinding, but it left too few subjects for the reliability of any conclusions based on the Week 8 and Week 12 assessments. The other Week 4 assessments (secondary and tertiary endpoints) all showed greater improvement in the Dysport group compared to the placebo group. The Week 4 assessments included subject and investigator ratings of CD symptoms and pain, proportion of subjects with \geq 50\% improvement in CD symptoms, and proportion of responders. All of these Week 4 assessments were consistent in the benefits shown for Dysport treatment, and there were no inconsistent results.
L1713: The proportion of Week 4 responders, defined as subjects with a decrease (improvement) in the TWSTRS Total score of \geq 30\%, and \geq 10 points compared to baseline, was 38% for the Dysport group, compared to 16% for the placebo group.
L1714: The sub-group analysis of patients who had received Dysport from batch 159J, which had fallen below the minimum potency specification (Group 3), demonstrated a similar degree of improvement to the patients in the primary analysis (Group 1). Comparison of the study endpoints of the subjects previously treated with botulinum toxin to those who were treatment naïve showed no significant differences.
L1715: Statements on the duration of response to Dysport tend to be problematic. The sponsor stated that, based on Study 045, the response lasted for a mean of 22.8 weeks. However, due to the early exit rate, no solid conclusions can be drawn. Too few subjects (4) eligible for retreatment remained for evaluation in the Dysport group past 12 weeks.
L1716: Analyses of the secondary and tertiary efficacy endpoints demonstrated greater improvement for Dysport subjects. Most of the secondary and tertiary endpoint analyses (especially at Week 8) reached the level of significance by p-values or confidence intervals, and the few that did not showed evidence improved outcome for the Dysport group compared to the placebo group. There were no inconsistencies (endpoints favoring
L1717: placebo group) that would have called into question any of the other endpoint conclusions.
L1718: 10.1.3 Study Y-97-52120-020 (Study 020)
L1719: “A Multicentre, Dose-ranging, Placebo-Controlled Study of Botulinum Toxin A (DYSPORT®) in Spasmodic Torticollis”
L1720: Sponsor:
L1721: Speywood Pharmaceuticals1 Bath RoadMaidenheadSL6 4UHEngland
L1722: Co-ordinating Investigator:
L1723: Professor Dr W PoeweUKRF der FU BerlinStandort WeddingNeurologische KlinikSpandauer Damm 130, D-1000 Berlin 19Germany
L1724: 10.1.3.1 Objective
L1725: To determine the minimal and optimal effective dose of Dysport and evaluate the safety and tolerability of different doses in the treatment of torticollis (cervical dystonia).
L1726: 10.1.3.2 Study Design
L1727: Study 020 was a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel group dose-ranging study conducted in 5 clinics in Germany. The study enrolled adult subjects with simple, rotational torticollis who had not previously received treatment with a botulinum toxin. The study was designed primarily as a dose-ranging, safety and tolerability study.
L1728: 10.1.3.3 Study Population and Procedures
L1729: Study 020 enrolled 75 subjects, aged \geq 18 years, with simple rotational cervical dystonia (CD). They were randomized into four treatment groups to receive either Dysport 250 units, 500 units, 1000 units, or placebo. The subjects received a single injection of study drug (0.75 ml into the contralateral sternocleidomastoid muscle and 1.75 ml into the splenius capitis muscle, ipsilateral to the direction of chin/head rotation). Effects of the treatment were evaluated at Weeks 2, 4 and 8. At Week 8 week the study blind was broken and subjects unresponsive to treatment could be reinjected. Responders were followed for an additional visit (at ~ Week 16).
L1730: Measurements at baseline and each visit included:
L1731: Demographic information, medical history at baseline, and:
L1732: • Torticollis rating (Tsui score) – includes measurement of amplitude of movements, shoulder elevation, and head tremor, but not pain• Pain rating – subject rated as absent, mild, moderate or severe• Clinical Overview Rating (of the CD) – rated by patient and investigator together on a 4-point scale: symptom free, mild symptoms, moderate symptoms, or severe symptoms• Improvement since injection (objective and subjective) – rated independently by investigator and subject on a 5-point scale compared to baseline: condition worsened, no improvement, improvement &lt; 50%, improvement &gt; 50%, symptom free• Assessment of Swallowing – for both liquids and solids using a 5-point scale: no difficulty, mild difficulty, moderate difficulty, severe problems, swallowing not possible• Checklist of Adverse Events (AEs) - a checklist of 10 specific AEs associated with botulinum toxins was assessed as: none, mild, moderate, or severe. Other spontaneous AEs were also collected, along with dates.
L1733: An additional end-of-study assessment was the Clinical Global Improvement score assessing overall change in CD by the investigator. A two-dimensional scale compared efficacy of treatment (excellent, good, moderate, slight improvement, no change, condition worsen) with safety (adverse events – none, mild, moderate, extreme).
L1734: 10.1.1.3.1 Study duration
L1735: Study initiation date : March 1993
L1736: Study completion date : March 1994
L1737: The trial was conducted in accordance with legal requirements in Germany. Approval was obtained from the local ethics committee from each study site prior to initiation. Informed consent prior to participation and subjects were informed that they were free to withdraw at any time. The study monitor was (b) (6)
L1738: with site visits every 6 to 8 weeks for review of study documents.
L1739: 10.1.3.3.2 Entry criteria
L1740: Inclusion Criteria:
L1741: • Adult subjects (\geq 18 years of age) with signed consent• Predominately simple, uncomplicated rotational torticollis (CD)
L1742: Exclusion Criteria:
L1743: • Any previous exposure to a botulinum toxin product• Any torticollis (CD) other than simple rotational torticollis (requiring injection into any muscles other than the splenius capitus and sternocleidomastoid muscles)• Subjects with myasthenia gravis and other diseases of the neuromuscular junction• Subjects with known swallowing abnormalities
L1744: • Pregnant or lactating women
L1745: 10.1.1.3.3 Study medications
L1746: Prohibited concurrent therapy
L1747: Subjects could not be enrolled if they had previously received treatment with any of the botulinum toxins.
L1748: Permitted concomitant therapy
L1749: Subjects already on medications for treatment of CD were allowed to continue their use. Subjects were supplied with sodium diclofenac tablets (50 mg.) for pain relief, if required.
L1750: 10.1.1.3.4 Study schedule/procedures
L1751: Table 46 shows the injection procedures by group. A box containing 3 vials was assigned for each subject (labeled A,B, C). Each vial was reconstituted with 1 ml sterile saline, then 1 ml was withdrawn from both vials A and B, and 0.5 ml from vial C (total 2.5 ml) for injection. The splenius capitus muscle was injected with 1.75 ml, and the sternomastoid muscle with 0.75 ml.
L1752: Subjects were supplied with sodium diclofenac tablets (50 mg) for pain relief, if needed.

L1755: 10.1.1.3.5 Efficacy parameters
L1756: Efficacy Variables
L1757: Study 020 was not designed as an efficacy study. Assessments were done and variables evaluated in the course of the study. Change from baseline in the total Tsui score was considered the primary efficacy variable. Additional endpoints evaluated were not designated as secondary, tertiary, or exploratory endpoints or evaluated in a hierarchical system. The additional variables included:
L1758: • Rating Scale for CD (modified Tsui score) – see Appendix 4, rating of CD at each visit including amplitude of movement, shoulder elevation and head tremor
• Improvement Since Injection – rated independently by subject and investigator at each visit on a 5-point scale (condition worsened, no improvement, improvement &lt;50%, improvement &gt;50%, symptom free).
• Degree of Pain – rated as absent, mild, moderate or severe
L1759: • Overview of the Condition (called Clinical Global Rating in the study) – rating by subject and investigator together at each visit using a 4-point scale (symptom-free, mild symptoms, moderate symptoms, or severe symptoms)
• Clinical Global Improvement – assessment at Week 8 by the investigator with a 2-dimensional scale comparing efficacy (excellent, good, moderate, slight improvement, no change, condition worsened) with safety (adverse events – none, mild, moderate, extreme)
L1760: 10.1.1.3.6 Statistical analysis
L1761: Statistical methods
L1762: Statistical analyses included:
L1763: • Homogeneity tests – to verify between-site homogeneity (baseline checks on age, sex, duration of CD, swallowing difficulties and total Tsui score)
• Descriptive statistics – in tabulated form according to scale types such as cell frequencies, classifications, and standard statistical characteristics (mean, SD, min., max., n)
• Conclusive statistics – for the Total Tsui score at Week 8, between group comparisons used the Kruskall-Wallis analysis, and results were also verified by the Maentel-Haenszel test. Analysis of variance between groups was used for onset of action, maximum improvement, and comparison of responders and non-responders.
L1764: Determination of sample size
L1765: The sponsor did not provide discussion on the determination of the sample size. The study was small since it was a Phase 2 dose-ranging study, and since the study only enrolled subjects who had not previously received botulinum toxin.
L1766: 10.1.1.3.7 Protocol amendments
L1767: Information on protocol amendments was not provided by the sponsor since Study 020 was not a pivotal trial submitted for review.
L1768: 10.1.1.3.8 Changes to the planned statistical analysis
L1769: Analysis of the concurrent use of pain medications was dropped from the final analysis.
L1770: 10.1.1.4 Study Results
L1771: 10.1.1.4.1 Trial characteristics
L1772: As discussed in Sections 1.0.1.3.2 and 1.0.1.3.3.
L1773: 10.1.1.4.2 Demographics
L1774: Baseline Demographic Characteristics
L1775: The demographic distribution of the groups is summarized in Table 47. There was a slightly higher percentage of male subjects recruited, which differs from the typical patterns of CD which has a slight female predominance. The mean age of the subjects was 47 years with a duration of CD symptoms showing some variation between the groups, with 99.9 months duration for the 500 U Dysport group compared to 73.8 months for the 250 U group, and a marked range with in the groups. The rest of the data shows a fairly even distribution between groups.

L1779: Baseline Medical Comparison
L1780: Concomitant Diseases
L1781: Hypertension was the most frequently occurring current non-dystonia condition (7 subjects) and was fairly evenly distributed between groups. One subjects reported epilepsy. A history of head trauma in the past was reported by 8 subjects.
L1782: Ten of the subjects reported other dystonias. The most frequently occurring were writer's cramp and Meige's syndrome. Four subjects had a family history of CD, and in each group, 1 to 4 subjects had a family history of dystonia or movement disorders.

The most frequently listed drug for non-CD use was antihypertensives.

L1789: Study Results
L1790: Subject Disposition
L1791: Efficacy analyses were performed on 73 of the enrolled 75 subject, and safety analyses on 74 (one by phone interview). Two subjects withdrew from the placebo group after the Week 4 assessments. A subject in the 500 U Dysport group
L1792: 10.1.1.4.4 Efficacy endpoints
L1793: Evaluation of Efficacy
L1794: The study was not designed as an efficacy study. The purpose of the study was to evaluate a minimal and an optimal dosage in terms of safety, tolerability, and benefits by establishing a dose responsiveness. Since some data collection was included in the study, that data as well as the appropriateness of the dose testing was evaluated in the review of this BLA.
L1795: Evaluation of the primary objective
L1796: Mean Tsui score
L1797: At baseline, the mean Tsui scores were fairly well-matched by groups with scores of 14.4, 14.3, 13.1, and 14.5 for the placebo to high dose respectively. The mean score at Weeks 2, 4, and 8 along with the percentage of change is shown in Table 49.
L1798: Significant differences compared to placebo were found at Week 4 for the 500 U and 1000 U Dysport groups compared to placebo (p=0.045 for each). The 1000 U Dysport group showed the highest percentage change at both the Week 4 and Week 8 assessments, although not reaching statistical significance at Week 8. Comparison of the 1000 U group change at Week 8 compared to the other groups suggests a longer duration of effectiveness for the highest dose. The change in Tsui score indicates a dose responsiveness with the groups.

L1803: Evaluation of additional endpoints
L1804: Gender Differences in Dose Response
L1805: The study evaluated the possibility that, due to difference in neck muscle size, there may be a greater response to the same size treatment dose in women than men. Table 50 shows the responses. There is apparently a greater Tsui score decrease for women in the 250 U and 500 U groups (although not reaching statistical significance), but not for women in the 1000 U group. No conclusions can be drawn since the numbers were too small.

L1809: Source: Study Y-97-52120-020
L1810: Improvement &gt;50% Since Injection
L1811: Improvement since injection was assessed by the physician and subject together on each visit. Of the subjects assessed to have &gt;50% improvement (defined as responders), a dose response relationship was shown. The placebo group remained constant at 10-11%. The 250 U group showed best results at Week 4 with 16% reporting &gt;50% improvement. The 500 U and 1000 U groups were both most significant at Week 8 with 6/16 (38%, p=0.0053), and 9/18 (50%, p=0.0015) respectively.
L1812: Pain Ratings (Percentage rating their pain as moderate or severe)
L1813: At baseline, 22/74 subjects (30%) reported they were pain-free, and they were distributed equally in the groups. Table 51 shows the percentage of subjects reporting moderate or severe pain at each visit. For the placebo group, 9/20 (45%) of the subjects reported moderate to severe pain at baseline, and the percentage varied from 30% to 39% with subsequent visits. In the 250 U group, the lowest percentage was at Week 4 (16%, down from 58% at baseline). For the higher doses, the results are confusing. In the 500 U group, the lowest percentage was at Week 2 (13%, down from 58% at baseline), and for the 1000 U group, at Week 4 (12%, down from 28% at baseline). At Week 8 the results show only a 10% decline from baseline for the 500 U group, and a 6% decline from baseline for the 1000 U group. No significant conclusions can be drawn from the pain ratings, partially due to the inclusion of the subjects rating themselves as pain-free at baseline yet still included in the pain analyses.

L1817: Clinical Global Improvement
L1818: The Clinical Global Improvement score was the two-dimensional score determined by the investigator at the Week 8 visit rating the efficacy of the treatment against the risks (adverse events) for each subject. Table 52 shows the possible scores and the numbers from each group within each score. The subject scores within the box with the black border (AEs of none, mild or moderate along with efficacy of excellent, good or moderate) were judged to have a positive benefit-to-risk ratio for this study. Within the positive ratio were 2/18 (11%) of the placebo group, 7/19 (37%) of the 250 U group, 7/18 (39%) of the 500 U group, and 11/18 (61%) of the 1000 U Dysport group. As a result, the risk:benefit ratio looked best for the highest dose treatment group.

L1830: 10.1.1.4.5 Safety
L1831: See the safety review for BLA 125274 by Dr. Marc Stone for full details.
L1832: The majority (70%) of all the adverse events reported were considered mild, however, the incidence of moderate and severe events increased with dose. Dysphagia was the most common reported AE, followed by neck muscle weakness, dry mouth, injection site discomfort, and to a less extent, vocal changes, tiredness, and visual changes. Adverse events were clearly dose-related with a increasing frequency of event reporting: for the placebo (30%), 250 U (42%), 500 U (59%), and 1000 U (72%). In terms of severe AEs related, or possibly related to treatment, the reports were of 0 in the 250 U, 1 in the 500 U and 4 in the 1000 U groups. None required treatment.
L1833: The findings in Study 020 are consistent with the occurrence of adverse events in pivotal studies. The onset of adverse events was generally within the first 7 to 14 days following treatment, and persisted for ~ 4 weeks. Study 020 showed a consistent relationship between increased adverse events with increased dosage.
L1834: The safety profile showed that dysphagia, neck muscle weakness, dry mouth, pain at the site of injection and voice changes were the most frequently reported events. Swallowing was closely monitored at baseline and each subsequent visit. Most occurrences involved difficulty with liquids, rather than solids, and most were rated as mild severity. Differences between treatment groups for dysphagia were not statistically significant (p = 0.3778). Dysphagia was reported by 2/20 (10%) in the placebo group, 4/19 (21%) of the 250 U, 5/17 (29%) of the 500U, and 7/18 (39%) of the 1000 U groups. The 1000U dose was statistically different from the placebo and 250 U dose for vocal change/hoarseness with 5/18 (28%), and from placebo and both lower doses in neck muscle weakness with 10/18 (56%).
L1835: 10.1.1.5 Reviewer's Summary
L1836: Summary and Comments
L1837: Study was included in the efficacy review for BLA 125274 since, although it was not a pivotal study, it was a controlled trial, contained efficacy assessments and, most importantly, evaluated treatment-naïve subjects. Establishing the optimal dose recommendation on subjects previously not exposed to botulinum toxin was laudable on the part of the sponsor since it makes the recommendation for a starting dose more reliable for general public prescribing. Study 020 evaluated only 3 doses (250 U, 500 U, and 1000 U of Dysport), so in terms of risk/benefit balance, 500 U may not be the optimal dose, but at least Study 020 establishes it as a reasonable starting dose from which future adjustment can be made.
L1838: Study 020 was a more homogeneous study than the pivotal trials. Not only did it enroll only treatment-naïve subjects, but the treatment protocol was also more narrowly-defined. Only subjects with uncomplicated rotational CD (i.e. with minimal involvement of antero/retrocollis) were included in the study, and the injections were limited to only two muscles (splenius capitus and sternocleidomastoid) at predetermined volumes for each. As such, the design was quite good for a fact-finding trial, and served as a good basis for establishing the protocols of the pivotal trials.
L1839: Statistical analyses were appropriate and adequate. Homogeneity tests between sites showed no significant differences, and the study groups were adequately randomized in terms of demographics, concomitant med, and medical background.
L1840: The demographic distribution of the subjects was well-balanced between the treatment groups, however there was a lack of non-Caucasian subjects. There were slightly more males (39) than females (36) unlike the general pattern of CD, but there were slightly more females in the 500 U and 1000 U Dysport groups. The differences were not enough to affect statistical analyses. In clinical practice, women often receive smaller doses because of smaller neck muscles. In this study with fixed doses, there was a greater Tsui score decrease for women than men in the lower doses, but not at the 1000 U dose. The inconsistency may be due to the small numbers involved.
L1841: The primary efficacy outcome (change in the Tsui score from baseline – smaller score shows improvement) showed minimal overall change for the placebo group, with a maximal decrease of 1.3 (9%) at Week 2. The maximal decrease in the 250 U Dysport group was also at Week 2, with a decrease of 10.7 (25%) which was virtually unchanged at Week 4, and returning toward baseline (11.6 points, 19%) at Week 8.
L1842: For the Dysport 500U and 1000U groups, the maximal decrease was at Week 4 (scores of 9.8, 25%, and 9.8, 32%, respectively) with the difference reaching statistical significance (p=0.0454 for both). The response at Week 8 for the 1000 U group was not statistically significant for either of the two higher doses (11.6, 19%, and 10.5, 28%, respectively), but the 28% decrease from baseline for the 1000 U group did suggest a more prolonged duration of effect than with the other dose levels. The lack of change adequate to reach statistical significance at Week 8 for the 500 U dose proposed for the BLA acceptance (and even the 1000U dose), raises some concern but is probably due to the differences between the Tsui and TWSTRS scales, and the small numbers involved in the Study 020. The Tsui scale is less sensitive to change than the TWSTRS scale. The Tsui score is dependent on change in postural deviation. A change of 15 – 20 degrees in one plane in postural head rotation gives a change of only 1 to 2 points on the Tsui scale, but may be considered as a more significant change by the subject or investigator. The differences between the Tsui and the TWSTRS assessments may account for the differences in effectiveness. The Tsui scale does not include pain assessment, and 70% of the subjects reported a pain component to their CD at baseline, although most of those (58%) rated it as mild pain. The TWSTRS Total score does have a pain sub-score (max. = 20 points on the 85 point total score). In Study 020, there was no dose response correlation to change
L1843: in pain, but due to baseline variability and concomitant meds, generalizations are not reliable.
L1844: The Rating of Symptoms indicated that all three Dysport doses showed a reduction in symptoms compared to placebo, but significant reductions were only in the 500 U and 1000 U doses (44% and 50% reduction respectively from baseline scores of moderate or severe symptoms at Week 4, and little change at Week 8).
L1845: Responders were defined as subjects showing a &gt; 50% improvement in symptoms. When investigator plus subject rating of “improvement of &gt;50% since injection” was evaluated, both the 500U and 1000 U groups showed their greatest gains at Week 8, with 38% (p=0.005), and 50% (p=0.002) respectively.
L1846: The overall assessment of improvement, considering both safety and efficacy, together also indicated a dose response relationship with the 1000 unit dose proving to be the most favorable, but the higher number of AEs was probably influential in the dose choice of 500U for the pivotal studies.
L1847: Overall, the study showed a dose relationship for both safety and efficacy. The 250 unit dose produced a sub-maximal response of short duration with few adverse events. The 500 U dose showed effectiveness for 2 – 3 months, and associated with an increased incidence of adverse events. The 1000U dose was the most effective in alleviating the symptoms of CD, and the duration of effect was longer, but there is a greater risk of adverse events. The optimal initial dose is 500 U which can be titrated to patient's response.
L1848: Reinjection at Week 8 was optional, but was requested by 9/16 (56%) of the 500 U group, and by 7/18 (39%) of the 1000 U group (also by 17/18, 94% of the placebo group, 9/19, 47% of the 250 U group). The majority (56%) of the 500 U dose group requesting reinjection at Week 8 shows the need for additional information on duration of effectiveness. The duration of effectiveness has not been well established in any of the studies reviewed, so the sponsor's claims to a 12-to-15 week duration of effectiveness is not well-substantiated, even in subjects previously treatment-naïve that would be expected to be more sensitive to a longer drug effect.
L1849: The Dysport 500 U dose was the only dosage used in the two pivotal trials. Comparison of the results of the Study 020 to the two pivotal trials in both efficacy and adverse events shows them to be comparable. None of the findings in the Study 020 are in contradiction to the conclusions of the pivotal studies, and although the primary efficacy endpoint did not reach statistical significance at Week 8, the overall findings of Study 020 makes a case for the acceptance of Dysport treatment for cervical dystonia.

L1869: CategoryGeneral CriteriaSafety and Efficacy Reporting




Category 1 – Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
          Y-97-52120-051
          Y-97-52120-045
          Y-97-52120-045b
          Y-47-52120-731[a]
        
Definitive Efficacy and Safety
          Studies conducted in cervical dystonia relied on by the sponsor to establish dose, efficacy, safety (including long term) or to address any other issues relevant to the BLA.
        
Full Safety and Efficacy
          Full Clinical Study Reports
          Case Report Tabulation
          Case Report Forms
        


Category 2 –Safety and Supportive Efficacy Studies
          Y-97-52120-020
          Y-97-52120-042
          Y-97-52120-096[b]
        
Supportive Efficacy and Safety
          Controlled and Open label studies conducted in Cervical Dystonia (CD) but were not conducted with FDA agreed endpoints.
        
Full Safety
          Full Clinical Study Reports
          Case Report Tabulation (CRT)
          Case Report Forms (CRF)
        


Category 3 – Post-Marketing / Other Information
          Y-97-52120-019
          Y-97-52120-022
          Y-97-52120-023
          Y-97-52120-024
          Y-97-52120-029
        
Supportive Safety
          Studies in CD without Clinical Study Reports (CSRs), CRFs, CRTs or reliable databases. These are further categorized as:
          
Adequate studies (CSRs available, SAE reconciliation could be performed, exposure denominator could be defined, all significant adverse events could be identified by subject)
Studies With Incomplete Safety Information (SWISI), and
Ongoing studies (studies ongoing at cut-off date)

          Spontaneous, postmarketing authorization reports in subjects treated for CD
        
Limited Safety Reporting
          Literature Publication and/or Abbreviated CSRs
          
Report Body without report appendices and with limited safety CRTs

          For Adequate and SWISI studies:
          
Deaths and Serious Adverse Events extracted from central pharmacovigilance database (ARISg),
AEWD (study 024 only) extracted from CSR into the ISS Category 3 study safety dataset.

          For ongoing studies:
          
line listings of SAE data extracted from ARISg database.
L1870: Category 4 - Studies in Other Indications / PMS Data See Table 5.2-2, 2.7.6

Supportive Safety Studies in other indications. These are further categorized as:

• Adequate studies (CSRs available, SAE reconciliation could be performed, exposure denominator could be defined, all significant adverse events could be identified by subject),
• SWISI studies (studies with incomplete safety information), and ongoing studies (studies ongoing at cut-off date)

Spontaneous, postmarketing authorization reports in subjects treated for other indications

Limited Safety Reporting
No CSRs
For adequate and SWISI studies: summary data on deaths and SAEs, extracted from ARISg into the ISS Category 4 study dataset.
For ongoing studies: line listings of AE data extracted from ARISg.
